--- PAGE 4 ---
Preface
T
his is the sixth edition of the coursebook for the Advanced Programming
course (SE233), which encompasses 30 lecture hours and 30 laboratory
hours. This course is a core component of the Bachelor of Science pro-
gram in Software Engineering at the College of Arts, Media and T ech-
nology, Chiang Mai University, Thailand.
Building upon the foundation established in the Object-Oriented Program-
ming (OOP) course, this course explores more advanced programming concepts,
providing students with a comprehensive toolkit of techniques for effective problem-
solving. This coursebook is structured into six chapters, each addressing a funda-
mental aspect of software development. The first chapter introduces the event-
driven programming paradigm using JavaFX, the primary technology employed
throughout this coursebook. The second chapter examines exception handling
techniques for enhancing the robustness of software applications. The third chap-
ter illustrates multithreaded programming, a technique for improving the utiliza-
tion of computational resources and for understanding the standard operation of
graphical user interfaces (GUIs). The fourth chapter covers error localization and
correction through debugging techniques. The fifth chapter provides an introduc-
tion to software testing, with an emphasis on unit testing. Finally, the sixth chapter
addresses topics related to software build automation and dependency manage-
ment.
A defining addition to this sixth edition is the continuous evaluation of Artifi-
cial Intelligence (AI)-assisted development tools. Each chapter features a dedicated
AI advisory section that critically examines the appropriate application and poten-
tial risks of AI generation within the corresponding technical domain. The instruc-
iii

--- PAGE 5 ---
iv
tional sequence emphasizes mastering fundamental concepts and understanding
failure modes before introducing automated tools. This framework ensures that
AI assistance serves to amplify existing technical capabilities rather than substitute
for foundational reasoning.
This coursebook is intended to serve as a comprehensive resource for students
and other individuals interested in the theoretical and practical application of the
topics covered within this course.
Passakorn Phannachitta
June 2026

Course Syllabus
University Chiang Mai University
Program Bachelor of Science (Software Engineering)
Course SE 233 (953233) Advanced Programming 3(2-2-5)
credits
Prerequisites SE 231 (953231) Object-Oriented Programming
Course Description Event Driven Development. Exceptions handling.
Multi-Thread Programming. Debugging. Unit test.
Build management tools.

--- PAGE 19 ---
xviii Course Syllabus
Course Objective • Students will be able to develop the software which

CHAPTER 1
Event Driven Development
Prerequisites
• A solid understanding of Java object-oriented programming (OOP): classes,
inheritance, interfaces, and polymorphism.
• A working development environment with a current Java Development Kit
(JDK) and IntelliJ IDEA (or an equivalent Integrated Development Envi-
ronment).
• The JavaFX Software Development Kit (SDK) installed and configured as
described in the Appendix.
• Familiarity with basic Java project structure, including packages, source
files, and the compilation process.
1

--- PAGE 23 ---
2 Event Driven Development
Expected Outcomes
• Construct a simple JavaFX application with a Stage, a Scene, and a node-
based scene graph.
• Implement event handlers for button clicks, mouse interactions, and key-
board input.
• Apply the Model-View-Controller (MVC) pattern to structure a GUI appli-
cation.
• Implement drag-and-drop interactions between visual components using
mouse event sequences.
• Use state variables and flags to manage application behaviour across multi-
ple events.
W
ith the widespread adoption of graphical user interfaces (GUIs)
in modern software, programming languages are equipped with
comprehensive libraries and toolsets to facilitate GUI development.
A fundamental principle of GUI development is the prompt re-
sponse to user-initiated signals. This responsiveness is enabled by functionality
that continuously monitors for such events, records relevant information upon
their occurrence, and invokes corresponding user-defined procedures. Fundamen-
tally, a GUI application is required to perform three primary functions: accept user
input, process this input, and deliver the resulting output in accordance with the
specified software requirements. A typical GUI application incorporates control
elements, such as buttons, which await user interaction (e.g., a click). Upon the
activation of a control element, the application executes a predefined process and
presents the output to the user. This output is commonly presented through various
visual components, including text or dialogue boxes.
The programming paradigm characterized by the specification of input types
and their corresponding handling procedures is formally known as Event-Driven
Development.

--- PAGE 24 ---
1.1. GUI 3
1.1 GUI
Prior to examining the specifics of GUI development in Java, it is necessary to es-
tablish the fundamental role of OOP in GUI design. The significance of OOP can
be illustrated by considering the task of constructing the GUI depicted in Figure
1.1, including the implementation of its visual elements. This process requires ren-
dering the message box, two text labels, a text field, and anOk button.
Figure 1.1: A Simple GUI with a Button
For the button component alone, a
rounded rectangle must be drawn, as-
signed a specific color (e.g., blue), posi-
tioned with defined coordinates within
the message box, and inscribed with
text (e.g.,Ok in white). In addition to
its graphical representation, the imple-
mentation of the button’ s behavior is
a critical task. This involves defining
methods that dictate the application’ s response to user interactions, such as clicks,
and may include modern visual effects for hovering and clicking. The inherent
complexity of modern GUI components makes the manual creation of each but-
ton from procedural code an impractical approach in software development.
Without the application of OOP principles, the construction of visual elements
becomes procedurally complex, particularly given the large number of components
in a typical application. For example, the message box in Figure1.1 could include
a Cancel button in addition to theOk button. In a non-OOP context, creating the
second button would require a complete replication of the implementation steps,
from rendering the shape to defining its interactive behavior. The core OOP prin-
ciple of reusability addresses this inefficiency. It allows for the creation of a single
Button object for theOk button, which can inherit behaviors and attributes from
pre-existing classes. The button’ s shape, for instance, could be inherited from a
Shape class that already contains logic for coordinate-based placement. Conse-
quently, adding aCancel button becomes a matter of instantiating anotherBut-
ton object, promoting code reuse within the same application and across future
projects. Therefore, OOP is integral to modern GUI development. A comprehen-

--- PAGE 25 ---
4 Event Driven Development
sive understanding of its concepts is a prerequisite for the topics covered in the
remainder of this coursebook.
1.1.1 JavaFX
Historically, GUI development in Java was accomplished using thejava.awt (Ora-
cle, 2026b) andjavax.swing (Oracle, 2026c) packages, commonly known as A WT
and Swing. A subsequent shift has occurred toward JavaFX (OpenJFX, 2026a), a
modern framework that is now the predominant approach for developing user in-
terfaces in Java. Positioned as the successor to A WT and Swing, JavaFX is described
on its official website as a collaborative project among various contributors to pro-
duce a modern, efficient, and full-featured toolkit for developing rich client appli-
cations. Accordingly, this coursebook utilizes JavaFX as the primary framework
for examining GUI development and the event-driven programming paradigm.
Installation and configuration details for the required software are provided in the
Appendix.
Several key features contribute to the adoption of JavaFX for client application
development:
• Full compatibility with all Java APIs, which allows developers to access an
extensive ecosystem of existing libraries.
• Support for cross-platform development, enabling applications to run on
various operating systems without modification.
• Accessibility for new developers through visual design tools that support
drag-and-drop component building.
• V ersatility in application deployment, as it can be used to implement solu-
tions for desktop, mobile, web, and embedded systems. Figure1.2 provides
an example of a JavaFX application in an embedded systems context.

--- PAGE 26 ---
1.1. GUI 5
Figure 1.2: JavaFX Embedded System De-
velopment Example (Arduino-er, 2015)
A GUI in JavaFX is constructed us-
ing a scene graph, which is a hierarchi-
cal tree structure of visual elements re-
ferred to as nodes. In a manner anal-
ogous to the organization of HTML
elements within a Document Object
Model (DOM) tree, JavaFX nodes are
arranged in parent-child relationships.
These nodes encompass a diverse range
of types, such as buttons, labels, text
fields, shapes, and media elements for
audio and video. Collectively, these
nodes are equipped to handle various
user input events, including mouse movements, hovers, and drag-and-drop oper-
ations. This framework provides a comprehensive set of visual elements for the
development of client applications.
A JavaFX application’ s user interface is defined within a hierarchy of containers,
primarily theStage and theScene. TheStage represents the main window or top-
level container, while theScene acts as the container for all visual content. In the
provided example, Script1.1 instantiates aStage and aScene with dimensions of
300x275 pixels. The content of theScene is structured as a hierarchical node graph,
with aStackPane functioning as the root node. The resulting GUI is displayed in
Figure1.3.
It is important to note the convention used for line numbers in Script1.1 and
subsequent code listings. The plus (+) and minus (–) symbols indicate line-level
differences between file versions. A ‘+’ denotes an added line, and a ‘-’ denotes a
deleted line. As this is the initial presentation ofHelloApplication.java, all lines
are marked with ‘+’ . This convention is maintained throughout the coursebook.

8 Event Driven Development
Appending a JavaFX node to a parent node is accomplished using thegetChil-
dren().add() method. This structure implies that thegetChildren() method re-
turns a list-like object that facilitates the addition of new elements. T o confirm this,
the JavaDoc documentation for thegetChildren() method is consulted (Oracle,
2026a). The left-hand side of Figure1.5 shows that the return type of this method
is ObservableList <Node> , which corroborates the initial assumption.
A further examination of theObservableList <Node> type, depicted on the
right-hand side of Figure1.5, shows that it is an interface that extends other widely
recognized interfaces from the Java Collections Framework, includingList<E>,
Collection<E>, andIterable<E>. It also extends theObservable interface. This
extension from standard collection interfaces allows common Java methods for list
operations, such asadd, contains, forEach, andtoArray, to be employed directly
on JavaFX element collections.
In contrast, theObservable interface may be less familiar. Its methods,ad-
dListener andremoveListener, are integral to the event-handling mechanism in
JavaFX. They provide the functionality for an object to register listeners that can re-
spond to user-initiated signals. However, before examining the specifics of listen-
ers, it is beneficial to first establish a foundational understanding of events (Oracle,
2026a).
Figure 1.5: JavaDoc Specifications for the getChildren() Method and the
ObservableList Interface (Oracle, 2026a)

--- PAGE 30 ---
1.2. Events 9
1.2 Events
Events are broadly categorized into two types: foreground and background. This
classification is primarily distinguished by the nature of user interaction. Fore-
ground events are initiated by direct user actions, such as button clicks, mouse
movements, or drag-and-drop operations. Conversely, background events are not
triggered by users but by other software or hardware processes. Examples of back-
ground events include operating system interrupts, hardware or software failures,
timer expirations, and the completion of operations.
In JavaFX, the event model comprises three fundamental components: an event
source, an event listener, and an event handler. An event source refers to a GUI
component that serves as the origin for user-initiated events. An event listener
is an object that monitors for specific events from a source. The event handler is
the method that contains the logic to process the event. In operation, an event
is generated at the source, which is then detected by the registered listener. The
listener, in turn, invokes the appropriate event handler to process the signal and
execute the required application logic. Programmatically, enabling this interaction
requires registering a listener with a source and implementing the logic within the
corresponding handler method.
Script1.3 demonstrates the addition of an event handler to theHelloApplica-
tion.java class. In this modified example, a click event on the button triggers the
display of the textHello World! in the system’ s terminal. On Line 7 of the script, an
event listener is registered with the button instance. The use of the@Override an-
notation in this context illustrates a practical application of polymorphism, a core
principle of OOP .
A noteworthy aspect of this implementation is the creation of the event han-
dler directly within thesetOnAction method, without a separate, formal instance
declaration. This technique demonstrates the use of an anonymous class, which
implements theEventHandler interface and overrides itshandle() method to de-
fine the logic that outputsHello World! to the terminal.

--- PAGE 31 ---

Script 1.3: Implementation of an Event Handler in HelloApplication.java
1 //Imports are omitted
2 public class HelloApplication extends Application {
3 @Override
4 public void start(Stage primaryStage) {
5 Button btn = new Button();
6 btn.setText("Say Hello World");
+ 7 btn.setOnAction(new EventHandler<ActionEvent>() {
+ 8 @Override
+ 9 public void handle(ActionEvent event) {
+ 10 System.out.println("Hello World!");
+ 11 }
+ 12 });
13 StackPane root = new StackPane();
14 root.getChildren().add(btn);
15 primaryStage.setTitle("Hello World");
16 primaryStage.setScene(new Scene(root, 300, 275));
17 primaryStage.show();
18 }
19 public static void main(String[] args) { launch(args); }
20 }
In practical applications, multiple events often occur concurrently, requiring
the application to provide a distinct response for each event. The example pre-
sented in Script1.3, by contrast, is limited to a single event source, listener, and han-
dler in order to illustrate the fundamental principles of event handling in JavaFX.
However, even in this simplified context, a single event can trigger multiple actions.
For instance, a click on a form-submission button might not only transmit data to
a server but also display a confirmation message to the user indicating a successful
submission.
1.3 Event Driven Programming
Event-driven programming can be defined as a paradigm for developing programs
composed of multiple subprograms, where each subprogram is designed to execute
in response to a specific event or signal. In the context of a video game, for example,

--- PAGE 32 ---
1.3. Event Driven Programming 11
the action of moving a character forward constitutes one such subprogram. This
subprogram remains inactive until the user provides a corresponding signal, such
as a specific key press. The activation of a different signal, such as another key press,
would invoke a different subprogram with a distinct outcome, such as initiating an
attack.
The event-driven programming paradigm represents a foundational approach
in numerous modern technologies, including web applications, the Internet of Things
(IoT), and various AI-powered automated systems. Within these technological do-
mains, numerous tasks can be conceptualized as subprograms initiated by specific
signals. For instance, smart irrigation systems employed in modern agriculture
utilize sensors for the continuous monitoring of environmental conditions. The
data generated by these sensors function as event triggers, invoking subprograms
that, based on their underlying algorithms, determine and execute appropriate ac-
tions such as the adjustment of watering schedules. An additional illustrative ex-
ample is the adaptive cruise control system in an automobile. This system operates
in response to signals from the surrounding environment, which in turn invoke
subprograms responsible for modulating vehicle speed and maintaining safe fol-
lowing distances. In both of these examples, the event-driven paradigm provides
the framework for the system to make informed decisions and execute appropri-
ate actions in response to received signals. This capability facilitates efficient and
responsive interactions between the system and its environment.
In summary, event-driven programming is a paradigm that allows for the de-
sign of programs structured around signals or events. These signals, which can
originate from users or other system components, trigger the execution of specific
subprograms, often referred to as event handlers. This approach is particularly
effective for applications where distinct user actions or external events require dis-
tinct programmatic responses. By structuring programs in this manner, developers
can create more flexible, interactive, and responsive software.

--- PAGE 33 ---
12 Event Driven Development
1.3.1 Events and GUI
Figure 1.6: Conceptual Model of a GUI System in Java (Wong,
2017)
Figure1.6 illustrates the conceptual model of a GUI system in Java (W ong, 2017).
The core mechanism of this system is the GUI event processing loop, as depicted in
the center of the figure. This loop operates as an active background process, func-
tioning similarly to an infinite loop dedicated to dispatching events. User actions,
such as a mouse click on a button, are encapsulated as event objects and added to
an event queue. A listener object, which has been previously registered with the
event’ s source component, is then notified. This listener dequeues the event and
invokes the associated handler method to perform the required processing. The
execution of the handler may, in turn, modify the application’ s state, resulting in
updates to the GUI.
In the context of Script1.3, the event is initiated by a user’ s mouse click on the
button. This action is encapsulated as anActionEvent object and placed in the
event queue. The event loop subsequently dequeues the event and dispatches it to
the appropriate listener. The system identifies the correct handler for the button
click based on this pre-registered listener, which in this example is an instance that
implements thejavafx.event.EventHandler<ActionEvent> interface. It should
be noted that multiple listeners can be registered for a single event source or type
of action. Figure1.7 provides a graphical representation of this event-handling
process.

--- PAGE 34 ---
1.3. Event Driven Programming 13
Figure 1.7: Event Handling Process for a Button Click in Java (Wong,
2017)
More examples
Script1.4 demonstrates the addition of two further event listeners to theHelloAp-
plication.java file. These listeners monitor the application’ sScene and are trig-
gered in response to changes in the window’ s dimensions. T o facilitate the registra-
tion of these listeners, theScene object is first instantiated and assigned to a local
variable (Lines 17-18). Consequently, thisscene object serves as the event source
for the two listeners added in this example.
The first listener, defined in Lines 18-23, monitors thewidthProperty of the
application scene. Its overridden method functions as the event handler and is
invoked when the scene’ s width value changes. Upon being triggered, this handler
prints the new width of the scene to the terminal. The second listener, located in
Lines 24-29, is functionally analogous, but monitors the scene’ sheightProperty.
This example highlights a recurring pattern for registering JavaFX event listen-
ers. As illustrated by the code, this pattern can be generalized as:[source ele-
ment].[property to be observed].addListener(new listener interface
[overridden handler method] ); . This structure cohesively defines the event
source, the specific property being monitored, the listener implementation, and
the handler logic within the required overridden method.
Script1.5 provides a concrete example of OOP design, where event listeners are
registered with an event source as part of a reusable component’ s implementation.
The objective of this script is to create aCircle object accompanied by aLabel.
This label displays the mouse coordinates when the pointer is within the circle’ s
bounds; otherwise, it indicates that the pointer is outside the circle.

6 btn.setText("Say Hello World");

10 System.out.println("Hello World!");
11 }
12 });
13 StackPane root = new StackPane();
14 root.getChildren().add(btn);
15 primaryStage.setTitle("Hello World");
– 16 primaryStage.setScene(new Scene(root, 300, 275));
+ 17 Scene scene = new Scene(root, 300, 275);
+ 18 scene.widthProperty().addListener(new ChangeListener<Number>() {
+ 19 @Override
+ 20 public void changed(ObservableValue<? extends Number> observableValue,
Number oldSceneWidth, Number newSceneWidth) {
+ 21 System.out.println("Width: " + newSceneWidth);
+ 22 }
+ 23 });
+ 24 scene.heightProperty().addListener(new ChangeListener<Number>() {
+ 25 @Override
+ 26 public void changed(ObservableValue<? extends Number> observableValue,
Number oldSceneHeight, Number newSceneHeight) {
+ 27 System.out.println("Height: " + newSceneHeight);
+ 28 }
+ 29 });
+ 30 primaryStage.setScene(scene);
31 primaryStage.show();
32 }

--- PAGE 36 ---
1.3. Event Driven Programming 15
A non-event-driven approach would necessitate the continuous monitoring of
the mouse pointer’ s position relative to the circle. The event-driven paradigm, how-
ever, simplifies this logic by executing code only in response to a relevant state
change. This change-oriented design prompts a conceptual shift toward manag-
ing program state. State, in this context, refers to the data or information that
is modified during program execution. For this example, the states correspond
to the mouse pointer entering and exiting the circle’ s bounds. Event handlers are
consequently implemented to manage these state transitions, while no handling is
required as long as the pointer remains outside the circle.
An examination of Script1.5 shows that the component is created by thecre-
ateMonitoredCircle() method (Lines 15-35). The listeners for the circle’ s mouse
events are registered using thesetOnMouseMoved() andsetOnMouseExited() meth-
ods. Specifically, the handler registered withsetOnMouseMoved() is invoked re-
peatedly while the mouse pointer is within the circle’ s boundaries, whereas the
handler forsetOnMouseExited() is invoked at the moment the pointer leaves these
boundaries.
Given this design, the reuse of the component created bycreateMonitored-
Circle(), as illustrated in Script1.6, implies that its associated label should be in-
cluded. Therefore, encapsulating these related elements within a single, new object
represents a practical design approach. In this implementation, the visual elements
are organized within aVBox layout container, which is subsequently placed inside
a StackPane container. Consequently, the return type of thecreateMonitored-
Circle() method is aStackPane node, which acts as the root of this composite
component. T o facilitate the reuse of this composite component, it can be added
as a child to any parent node at a higher level within the application’ s scene graph.
In this particular example, the component is added to a JavaFXFlowPane layout
container, as shown in Line 7 of the script. Upon compilation and execution, the
application will display the result illustrated in Figure1.8.
Figure 1.8: GUI Output of customizedEvent.java

Script 1.5: Implementation of a Reusable Component with Custom Event Listeners
CustomizedEvent.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class CustomizedEvent extends Application {
+ 3 @Override
+ 4 public void start(Stage stage) {
+ 5 FlowPane pane = new FlowPane();
+ 6 StackPane circle1 = createMonitoredCircle();
+ 7 pane.getChildren().addAll(circle1);
+ 8 Scene scene = new Scene(pane);
+ 9 stage.setTitle("Customized Events");
+ 10 stage.setScene(scene);
+ 11 stage.show();
+ 12 }
+ 13 private StackPane createMonitoredCircle() {
+ 14 StackPane pane = new StackPane();
+ 15 VBox layout = new VBox();
+ 16 Label reporter = new Label("Outside Circle");
+ 17 Circle circle = new Circle(50);
+ 18 circle.setOnMouseMoved(new EventHandler<MouseEvent>() {
+ 19 @Override
+ 20 public void handle(MouseEvent event) {
+ 21 reporter.setText("(x: "+event.getX()+", y: " + event.getY()+")");
+ 22 }
+ 23 });
+ 24 circle.setOnMouseExited(new EventHandler<MouseEvent>() {
+ 25 @Override
+ 26 public void handle(MouseEvent event) {
+ 27 reporter.setText("Outside Circle");
+ 28 }
+ 29 });
+ 30 layout.getChildren().setAll(circle, reporter);
+ 31 pane.getChildren().add(layout);
+ 32 return pane;
+ 33 }
+ 34 public static void main(String[] args) { launch(args); }
+ 35 }

1.3.2 Mouse events
Script1.6 demonstrates the reuse of the composite component described previ-
ously (Lines 7-9). This example utilizes listeners for mouse movement and exit
events; however, the JavaFX framework provides a more extensive set of mouse
event types. According to the official JavaDoc (OpenJFX, 2026b), the mouse events
available in the current version of JavaFX include the following:
• setOnMouseEntered
• setOnMouseExited
• setOnMouseMoved
• setOnMousePressed
• setOnMouseReleased
• setOnMouseDragged
• setOnMouseDragEntered
• setOnMouseDragExited
• setOnMouseDragOver    
• setOnMouseDragReleased    
Drag and drop
An effective method for examining multiple mouse events concurrently is through
the implementation of drag-and-drop functionality. The design of such function-
ality requires several key considerations:
Initiation: The user actions that trigger the start of a drag operation.
Draggable Objects: The types of GUI components that are designated as drag-
gable.
Target Detection: The mechanisms for detecting when the mouse pointer is
positioned over a valid drop target.
Visual Feedback: The visual changes to a component during the drag-and-
drop process.
Component Movement: The logic governing the movement of the dragged
component, including any constraints.

--- PAGE 40 ---
1.3. Event Driven Programming 19
T ermination: The user actions that signal the end of a drag operation (i.e., the
drop).
Coordinate Management: The role of mouse and component coordinates in
the implementation.
A thorough analysis of these considerations provides a comprehensive under-
standing of how mouse events are applied in complex user interactions. This sec-
tion details the implementation of a specific drag-and-drop task, illustrated in Fig-
ure1.9, which is defined by the following requirements:
1. Two circles are present: a blue source circle and a red target circle.
2. A mouse click on the source circle brings it to the front of the z-order and
initiates its movement in synchronization with the mouse pointer.
3. If the mouse button is released while the source circle is over the target circle,
the source circle snaps to the position of the target circle.
4. If the mouse button is released while the source circle is not over the target
circle, it returns to its original coordinates.
The implementation of these requirements, while seemingly straightforward,
involves several design considerations. Given that both the source and target cir-
cles share common characteristics (e.g., associated labels), an advisable design ap-
proach is to create a dedicated class for their instantiation. This promotes reusabil-
ity by the main application class or other components. Script1.7 demonstrates this
approach through the implementation of theFigureGroup class.

--- PAGE 41 ---
20 Event Driven Development
Script 1.7: FigureGroup Class for Drag-and-Drop FigureGroup.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class FigureGroup {
+ 3 private StackPane figure;
+ 4 public FigureGroup(int x, int y, int radius, Color color, String str) {
+ 5 Circle circle = new Circle(radius, color);
+ 6 Text text = new Text(str);
+ 7 this.figure = new StackPane( circle, text );
+ 8 this.figure.setLayoutX(x);
+ 9 this.figure.setLayoutY(y);
+ 10 }
+ 11 public StackPane getFigure() { return figure; }
+ 12 }

1.3. Event Driven Programming 21
Script1.8 demonstrates the utilization of theFigureGroup object within the
main application class. Lines 9 and 10 show the instantiation of twoFigureGroup
objects, which produces two circles, each with an associated label. Executing the
script at this stage of the implementation displays the initial GUI depicted in Figure
1.9.
The subsequent implementation step is to define the drag-and-drop behavior.
T o facilitate the movement of the circle in synchronization with the mouse pointer,
the component’ s coordinates are required for positional calculations. Furthermore,
to satisfy requirement #4, which requires that releasing the mouse button outside
the target circle must return the source circle to its original position, it is neces-
sary to record the component’ s starting coordinates. This necessitates storing both
the initial mouse coordinates and the component’ s origin coordinates prior to any
movement. Accordingly, the implementation for the source circle requires the reg-
istration of listeners for the following events:setOnMousePressed, setOnDragDe-
tected, setOnMouseDragged, andsetOnMouseReleased.
The necessary modifications to theFigureGroup andDragAndDrop classes are
presented in Scripts1.9 and1.10, respectively. Within theFigureGroup class, the
modifications are primarily concerned with implementing the functionality to re-
turn the component to its origin. This involves a variable declaration on Line 4 and
the implementation of methods for setting and resetting the component’ s location,
as shown in Lines 12-19.
Furthermore, four event listeners are registered with the source circle in the up-
datedDragAndDrop class (Script1.10). The listener registered via thesetOnMouse-
Pressed() method (Lines 13-19) is invoked when a mouse press occurs on the
event source. Its handler captures and stores the initial coordinates in themousex
andmousey variables. ThesetOnDragDetected() listener is triggered when a drag
gesture is initiated. Its handler brings the source component to the front of the z-
order, ensuring that subsequent drag events are directed to this component until
the gesture concludes. ThesetOnMouseDragged() listener’ s handler continuously
updates the source component’ s position to match the mouse pointer’ s coordinates
during the drag operation. Finally, the handler for thesetOnMouseReleased() lis-
tener implements the logic to return the source circle to its original coordinates
upon the release of the mouse button. Consequently, this implementation initially
causes the source circle to revert to its origin regardless of the drop location.

+ 13 source.getFigure().setOnMousePressed(new EventHandler<MouseEvent>() {
+ 14 @Override
+ 15 public void handle(MouseEvent event) {
+ 16 mouse_x = event.getScreenX();
+ 17 mouse_y = event.getScreenY();
+ 18 }
+ 19 });
+ 20 source.getFigure().setOnDragDetected(new EventHandler<MouseEvent>() {
+ 21 @Override

+ 23 source.getFigure().setMouseTransparent(true);
+ 24 source.getFigure().toFront();
+ 25 source.getFigure().startFullDrag();
+ 26 }
+ 27 });
+ 28 source.getFigure().setOnMouseDragged(new EventHandler<MouseEvent>() {
+ 29 @Override
+ 30 public void handle(MouseEvent event) {
+ 31 double deltaX = event.getScreenX() - mouse_x;
+ 32 double deltaY = event.getScreenY() - mouse_y;
+ 33 source.setPosition(deltaX, deltaY);
+ 34 mouse_x = event.getScreenX();
+ 35 mouse_y = event.getScreenY();
+ 36 }
+ 37 });
+ 38 source.getFigure().setOnMouseReleased(new EventHandler<MouseEvent>() {
+ 39 @Override

+ 41 source.setBackOrigin();
+ 42 source.getFigure().setMouseTransparent(false);

--- PAGE 45 ---
24 Event Driven Development
The final implementation step is to satisfy requirement #3. Because the re-
quired response to a mouse release action is conditional upon the pointer’ s loca-
tion, a flag variable is a suitable implementation tool. A flag, typically implemented
as a boolean variable, is a logical construct analogous to an electronic switch that
controls a binary state (e.g., on/off). In programming contexts, a flag variable is
used to track the state of the system, indicating whether a specific condition is met
or a particular event has occurred.
The use of flag variables is a common technique in introductory programming
for solving problems such as primality testing. For instance, to determine if an
integer n is prime, an algorithm can test for divisibility by numbers from 2 up to
the square root of n. A boolean flag, initialized to false, can be used to track whether
a divisor has been found. If any integer in the range divides n evenly, the flag is set
to true. Upon the loop’ s completion, the final state of the flag indicates whether n
is a prime number.
This same concept is frequently employed in GUI programming for tasks such
as managing the state of toggle buttons, menus, or conditionally rendered com-
ponents. In the context of the current drag-and-drop example, a flag variable is
likewise necessary to implement the conditional logic of requirement #3. While a
mouse release event signifies the termination of the drag operation, the program-
matic response is dependent on the location of the release. A release outside the
target object must cause the source object to return to its origin, whereas a release
over the target must cause the source to snap to the target’ s position.
Script1.11 further modifies the implementation by introducing a flag variable
and two additional event listeners. Initially, a boolean flag variable namedisEn-
tered is declared (Line 4) and initialized tofalse (Line 13). The two new listeners,
defined in Lines 54-65, are responsible for modifying the flag’ s state. Handlers reg-
istered via thesetOnMouseDragEntered() andsetOnMouseDragExited() meth-
ods, which are bound to the target circle, toggle theisEntered variable accordingly.
The value of this flag is then utilized within the modifiedsetOnMouseReleased()
handler of the source circle. As shown in Lines 46-51, when the mouse is released,
the handler logic examines the state of this flag to determine if the release occurred
over the target circle, and executes the appropriate action.

26 Event Driven Development
Dragging and dropping items in a ListView
Figure 1.10: Drag-and-Drop Item Interac-
tion between Lists
Figure1.10 illustrates a common ap-
plication of drag-and-drop functional-
ity: moving items between lists. Un-
like the previous example, this func-
tionality requires data transfer in con-
junction with the drag-and-drop ges-
tures. For instance, dragging the item
Java from the source list to the target
list transfers not only the visual repre-
sentation of the item but also its under-
lying data. Therefore, this task involves several considerations beyond those re-
quired for moving simple shapes:
Data Transfer: The mechanisms for transferring the data associated with an
item between the source and target lists.
Target Acceptance: The logic that determines when and how a target list ac-
cepts data from a source list.
Post-Drop Operations: The actions required on both the source and target
lists subsequent to a successful drop operation.
Addressing these considerations is crucial for implementing a functional drag-
and-drop feature that transfers items and their associated data between lists. The
implementation of this functionality requires at least four primary event types:
dragDetected, dragOver, dragDropped, and dragDone. Because either list can
serve as both a source and a target, an efficient design approach is to create a single
class for the list component, allowing it to be reused for both sides of the applica-
tion window. Script1.12 presents the class for this list component. AListView
control is created in Line 5, and the listeners for the four required event types are
registered with thisListView between Lines 7 and 40.

--- PAGE 48 ---
1.3. Event Driven Programming 27
Script 1.12: Implementation of a Reusable ListView Component with Drag-and-
Drop Functionality DragListModel.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class DragListModel {
+ 3 ListView<String> lV;
+ 4 public DragListModel(){
+ 5 lV = new ListView<String>();
+ 6 lV.setPrefSize(200, 200);
+ 7 lV.setOnDragDetected(new EventHandler<MouseEvent>() {
+ 8 public void handle(MouseEvent event) {
+ 9 Dragboard dragboard = lV.startDragAndDrop(TransferMode.MOVE);
+ 10 String selectedItems = lV.getSelectionModel().getSelectedItem();
+ 11 ClipboardContent content = new ClipboardContent();
+ 12 content.putString(selectedItems);
+ 13 dragboard.setContent(content);
+ 14 }
+ 15 });
+ 16 lV.setOnDragOver(new EventHandler <DragEvent>() {
+ 17 public void handle(DragEvent event) {
+ 18 Dragboard dragboard = event.getDragboard();
+ 19 if (event.getGestureSource() != lV && dragboard.hasString()) {
+ 20 event.acceptTransferModes(TransferMode.MOVE);
+ 21 }
+ 22 }
+ 23 });
+ 24 lV.setOnDragDropped(new EventHandler <DragEvent>() {
+ 25 public void handle(DragEvent event) {
+ 26 boolean dragCompleted = false;
+ 27 Dragboard dragboard = event.getDragboard();
+ 28 if(dragboard.hasString()) {
+ 29 String list = dragboard.getString();
+ 30 lV.getItems().addAll(list);
+ 31 dragCompleted = true;
+ 32 }
+ 33 event.setDropCompleted(dragCompleted);
+ 34 }
+ 35 });
+ 36 lV.setOnDragDone(new EventHandler <DragEvent>() {
+ 37 public void handle(DragEvent event) {
+ 38 lV.getItems().remove(lV.getSelectionModel().getSelectedItem());
+ 39 }
+ 40 });
+ 41 }
+ 42 }

--- PAGE 49 ---
28 Event Driven Development
The handler for thesetOnDragDetected event is responsible for creating a
DragBoard, which is a standard JavaFX container for managing data transfer be-
tween a drag source and a drop target. Although this specific example transfers
only a string value (e.g., Java), theDragBoard is capable of managing the transfer
of entire serialized objects in more complex applications.
Next, the handler for thesetOnDragOver event, which is invoked when an ob-
ject is dragged over theListView, contains the logic to determine if the target list
can accept the data. This determination is made by verifying that theDragBoard
contains data of a compatible type, in this case, a string.
Subsequently, the handler for thesetOnDragDropped event employs a boolean
flag to track the completion status of the drop operation. This flag, declared in Line
27, is set totrue upon the successful transfer of the string data to the target list’ s
data model.

1.3.3 Keyboard events
The handling of mouse events and keyboard events differs in several fundamental
aspects. In the case of mouse events, the user’ s intent is typically unambiguous, as it
is spatially directed toward a specific visual element by the mouse pointer. This ex-
plicitness of the event target simplifies the implementation of event-handling logic.
Keyboard events, by contrast, are often not associated with a specific GUI compo-
nent, and the outcome of a key press may not be intuitively apparent to the user
without prior instruction. Therefore, the behavior associated with keyboard events
must be explicitly predefined, and the event source is typically registered at a global
level, such as the entire application scene. For example, in a video game, specific
keys are designated for character movement, and these key bindings apply globally
regardless of the mouse pointer’ s position.
Script1.14 demonstrates an application in which a component’ s movement is
controlled by keyboard arrow keys. The visual component in this example is an
instance of theFigureGroup class from Script1.9. The code segment in Lines 12-
21 implements the movement logic. This is achieved by creating a listener for key
press events on the main scene. The handler for this listener processes the received
key code and updates the component’ s position relative to its previous coordinates.

--- PAGE 51 ---
30 Event Driven Development
Script 1.14 : Implementation of Keyboard-Controlled Component Movement
KeyboardEventExample.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class KeyboardEventExample extends Application {
+ 3 @Override
+ 4 public void start(Stage stage) {
+ 5 stage.setTitle("Keyboard Event Example");
+ 6 Group root = new Group();
+ 7 Scene scene = new Scene(root, 500, 200);
+ 8 scene.setFill(Color.WHITE);
+ 9 FigureGroup obj = new FigureGroup(200,50,50,Color.CORAL,"FIGURE");
+ 10 scene.setOnKeyPressed(new EventHandler<KeyEvent>() {
+ 11 @Override
+ 12 public void handle(KeyEvent event) {
+ 13 if (event.getCode() == KeyCode.UP) {
+ 14 obj.setPosition(0,-5);
+ 15 } else if (event.getCode() == KeyCode.DOWN) {
+ 16 obj.setPosition(0,5);
+ 17 } else if (event.getCode() == KeyCode.LEFT) {
+ 18 obj.setPosition(-5,0);
+ 19 } else if (event.getCode() == KeyCode.RIGHT) {
+ 20 obj.setPosition(5,0);
+ 21 }
+ 22 }
+ 23 });
+ 24 root.getChildren().add(obj.getFigure());
+ 25 stage.setScene(scene);
+ 26 stage.show();
+ 27 }
+ 28 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 29 Application.launch(args);
+ 30 }
+ 31 }

1.4. Case Study 31
1.4 Case Study
AI Advisory
Event-driven programming with JavaFX is an area where relying on AI
tools too early can lead to code that works but is difficult to understand.
This is because event-driven applications do not run in a simple, linear se-
quence. Instead, program execution is controlled by user interactions, such
as mouse clicks, which act as signals to trigger specific event handlers. Be-
cause this flow of control is managed behind the scenes by the JavaFX frame-
work, letting an AI write these interactions prevents a developer from learn-
ing exactly when and how the code is executed.
Recommended Approach
In this chapter, the links between the Model, View, and Controller compo-
nents are held together entirely by the structure of the code. An AI tool
without heavy configuration may not reliably determine which controller
method is meant to update a specific visual component. Furthermore, the
tool is unlikely to fully comprehend the lifecycle of an application with-
out access to the entire codebase. The most reliable way to learn here is to
manually trace the execution path from the event source to the listener, and
finally to the handler that updates the data. Once a clear understanding of
this flow is established, AI tools can then be used to inspect the code or
generate specific segments. However, the generated code must always be
verified to ensure it matches the intended design and does not introduce
unintended side effects.
Appropriate Uses
✓ Run the application after adding small sections of code to see exactly
what changes.

--- PAGE 53 ---
32 Event Driven Development
✓ Draw a diagram of the project architecture before writing any event han-
dlers.
✓ Read the official documentation to understand a method before using
it.
✓ Use an AI to explain general concepts (for example, asking about the
difference between two methods) only after trying to figure the answer
out manually.
Practices to Avoid
× Do not give an AI the entire project and ask for the event connections
to be written.
× Do not ask an AI to link the controller to the view. External tools can
easily make mistakes with specific variable names or layout details. At
this stage of learning, it remains difficult to verify the correctness of gen-
erated code without a clear understanding of the underlying logic.
× Do not accept AI-generated code for complex tasks like drag-and-drop
without reading and understanding every step of the process.
× Do not ask an AI to fix a NullPointerException before manually
checking if variable names and annotations match perfectly.
Practicing this manual process will prepare the developer for Chapter 3.
When background threads are introduced later, they introduce significantly
more complex bugs that AI tools often struggle to accurately detect or re-
solve unless fully understand the entire context.

1.4. Case Study 35
Figure 1.12: Project Parameter Configuration Example
The assets folder is the designated location for storing all program assets,
which are available athttps://cmu.to/SE233CH1. The project’ s structure is or-
ganized according to the MVC design pattern. TheLauncher class functions as
the main application entry point. The view component encompasses all elements
related to presentation, such as displaying the attribute values stored within aChar-
acter instance. The model component is responsible for data modeling, data struc-
tures, and business logic, with entities implemented as distinct objects. For in-
stance, theBasicEquipment class serves as the parent class for theArmor andWeapon
classes, defining their common attributes. Finally, the controller component con-
tains the core application logic and manages the interactions between the view and
the model.
It is worth noting that the communication between these three components
is largely indirect. The Controller accesses the Model through direct method calls,
retrieving or updating data as needed. The Controller then updates the View either
by invoking methods on view components or by modifying observable properties

--- PAGE 57 ---
36 Event Driven Development
to which the View is bound. The View, however, has no direct reference to the
Model; all data flows through the Controller.
In this chapter, the View is constructed entirely in Java code rather than through
FXML layout files. FXML-based view construction, where the Controller class de-
clares fields annotated with@FXML and links them to visual components via match-
ingfx:id attributes, will be introduced in Chapter 3. When that approach is adopted,
a mismatch between the field name in the Controller and thefx:id in the FXML
file results in anull reference at runtime, a common source of errors that can only
be identified by careful inspection of both files. The Java-code approach used here
makes the binding between components explicit and is therefore a useful starting
point for understanding MVC communication before the additional layer of FXML
indirection is introduced.
1.4.1 Model components
The examination of the project implementation begins with the model compo-
nents. Scripts1.15, 1.16, 1.17, and 1.18 illustrate the classes associated with the
game’ sCharacter entity. The two character types,Physical andMagical, are im-
plemented as subclasses derived from theBasedCharacter class. A significant ad-
vantage of this inheritance structure is that polymorphism allows instances of ei-
therPhysicalCharacter orMagicalCharacter to be treated as theBasedChar-
acter type, simplifying their management in the GUI. In this example,Physical
characters possess a higher Full Health Point (FullHp) attribute compared toMag-
ical characters, which is balanced by a lower base power attribute.
An examination of thePhysicalCharacter andMagicalCharacter classes
(Scripts1.17 and1.18, respectively) reveals an absence of local variable declara-
tions. This design choice is based on the commonality of their attributes; it is more
efficient to declare these shared variables in theBasedCharacter superclass. The
shared variables includename, fullHP, andbasedPow.

pattern similar to that of theCharacter entity, the two item types,Weapon and
Armor, are implemented as subclasses that extend theBasedEquipment superclass.
This use of inheritance allows the inventory list and equipment slots to be typed
asBasedEquipment, enabling them to hold instances of either theWeapon orArmor
subclasses through polymorphism.
Script 1.19: model/item/BasedEquipment.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class BasedEquipment {
+ 3 protected String name;
+ 4 protected String imgpath;
+ 5 public String getName() { return name; }
+ 6 public String getImagepath() { return imgpath; }
+ 7 public void setImagepath(String imgpath) { this.imgpath = imgpath; }
+ 8 }
As shown in the implementations ofWeapon (Script1.20) and Armor (Script
1.21), some variables are declared locally within the subclasses rather than being in-
herited fromBasedEquipment. This design is necessary because certain attributes,
such asdefense andresistance, are specific to one subclass and not shared by the
other.

1.4.2 View components
Subsequent to the implementation of the model, the development process typically
proceeds to the implementation of the view components. The view is responsible
for the visual representation of the model’ s state. As depicted in Figure1.11, the
application’ s main window is composed of three distinct sub-panes. Each of these
sub-panes is implemented as a separate class, named descriptively according to its
function:EquipPane for the left-hand side,CharacterPane for the right-hand side,
andInventoryPane for the bottom sub-pane, respectively. The implementations
for these view component classes are presented in Scripts1.22, 1.23, and1.24.

In Script1.22, anImageView array is created (Line 10) and initialized within a
for-loop (Lines 11-14). This array, sized according to the total number of equip-
ment items, is responsible solely for the visual presentation of the item images. The
underlyingBasedEquipment data objects are stored separately in theequipmen-
tArray variable. ThegetDetailPane() method returns aPane object, which is
a node that can be attached to the scene graph. ThedrawPane() method subse-
quently adds this returnedPane as a child node. Because theInventoryPane class
itself extends the JavaFXScrollPane, it functions as a composite node that can be
directly added to the main application’ s scene graph.
Next, in Script1.23, the core functionality involves generating a different visual
representation based on whether an item is equipped. For example, the conditional
statementequippedWeapon != null (Lines 14-20) checks for the presence of an
equipped weapon. This technique, known as conditional rendering, is common in
GUI development. If a weapon is equipped, its corresponding image is displayed;
otherwise, a default placeholder image (blank.png) is displayed. A similar tech-
nique is employed for displaying the character’ s image in theCharacterPane class,
as shown in Line 21 of Script1.24.
1.4.3 Controller components and programming logic
Following the implementation of the model and view components, the final step is
to integrate these elements within theLauncher class, which functions as the con-
troller. The initial implementation of this class is presented in Script1.25. The core
logic for assembling the GUI is located in Lines 20-30, where all three sub-panes
are combined into a single JavaFX visual element of the typeBorderPane. Addi-
tionally, therefreshPane() method, implemented in Lines 31-35, demonstrates a
common GUI programming technique: the use of a single method to refresh the
state of all application sub-components.
The implementation then proceeds to the core program logic. The initial logic
required is the setup of theCharacter andItem lists, which occurs upon applica-
tion startup and produces the GUI shown in Figure1.11. Script1.26 presents the
setUpCharacter() method for this purpose. This method initially declares achar-
acter variable of the typeBasedCharacter (Line 4). This variable is subsequently
instantiated as an object of aBasedCharacter subclass, eitherMagicalCharacter

1.4. Case Study 49
1.4.4 Flexible-size container
Standard arrays are data structures designed to store a fixed number of elements, a
size that is predetermined and immutable during program execution. In the con-
text of this application, however, the number of items in the inventory is dynamic,
as items may be added or removed during gameplay. Consequently, anArrayList,
which is a dynamic data structure capable of resizing itself, represents a more suit-
able choice for implementing the inventory. Script1.31 demonstrates the refactor-
ing of the item container from a standard array to anArrayList.
Script 1.31: controller/GenItemList.java
1 //Imports are omitted
...
8 public class GenItemList {
– 9 public static BasedEquipment[] setUpItemList() {
– 10 BasedEquipment[] itemLists = new BasedEquipment[5];
– 11 itemLists[0] = new Weapon("Sword",10,DamageType.physical,"assets/sword.png");
– 12 itemLists[1] = new Weapon("Gun",20,DamageType.physical,"assets/gun.png");
– 13 itemLists[2] = new Weapon("Staff",30,DamageType.magical,"assets/staff.png");
– 14 itemLists[3] = new Armor("Shirt",0,50,"assets/shirt.png");
– 15 itemLists[4] = new Armor("Armor",50,0,"assets/armor.png");
+ 16 public static ArrayList<BasedEquipment> setUpItemList() {
+ 17 ArrayList<BasedEquipment> itemLists = new ArrayList<BasedEquipment>(5);
+ 18 itemLists.add(new Weapon("Sword",10,DamageType.physical,"assets/sword.png"));
+ 19 itemLists.add(new Weapon("Gun",20,DamageType.physical,"assets/gun.png"));
+ 20 itemLists.add(new Weapon("Staff",30,DamageType.magical,"assets/staff.png"));

1.4.5 Drag-and-drop functionality
With the fundamental components established, the subsequent focus is the imple-
mentation of the drag-and-drop functionality. The drag-and-drop feature in this
case study is primarily concerned with data transfer. Specifically, this involves drag-
ging an item’ s visual representation and dropping it onto a character’ s equipment
slot, which results in the modification of the character’ s attributes.
The initiation of the drag-and-drop process requires the declaration of a data
format and aDragboard, as demonstrated in Line 4 of Script1.34. The item image
within theInventoryPane acts as the event source. This image can be dragged
and dropped onto the event targets, which are the two equipment slots within
theEquipPane. T o manage this functionality, several handler methods are im-
plemented in Script1.35. The handler registered via thesetOnDragDetected()
method defines aDragboard variable (db) to temporarily store data during the
transfer. This is achieved using theimgView.startDragAndDrop(TransferMode.ANY)
method, which functions as a clipboard for transferring data between the drag
source and drop target.

52 Event Driven Development

With the event sources configured, the implementation focus shifts to the event
targets. Script1.37 introduces handlers for theonDragOver andonDragDropped
events to theAllCustomHandler class. Line 33 demonstrates a technique for veri-
fying the class name of the dragged data. This allows the handler to ensure that only
an appropriate equipment type (Weapon orArmor) is accepted by a given equipment
slot.
Line 47 of the script demonstrates a technique for managing the equipment
slots. The logic checks if the equipment slot container (aPane) already contains a
child node. The presence of a child node signifies that an item is currently equipped.
If the slot is occupied, the currently equipped item is first unequipped before the
new item is equipped.
Subsequently, these event handlers are bound to the target view components.
As shown in Lines 51-66 of Script1.38, listeners that invoke theonDragOver() and
onDragDropped() handler methods are registered with theweaponImgGroup and
armorImgGroup components of theEquipPane class. These components display a

+ 30 public static void onDragOver(DragEvent event, String type) {
+ 31 Dragboard dragboard = event.getDragboard();

1.6 Discussion and Summary
This chapter has established the foundational principles of event-driven program-
ming utilizing the JavaFX framework. The discussion progressed from the applica-
tion of OOP concepts in GUI construction to the architectural design of the scene
graph. Furthermore, it detailed the event-handling mechanism, comprising event
sources, listeners, and handlers, and examined the primary categories of user in-
put, specifically action, mouse, and keyboard events. Finally, the case study syn-
thesized these theoretical concepts into a functional, MVC-structured application,
demonstrating practical implementations of drag-and-drop functionality and at-
tribute propagation.

--- PAGE 84 ---

Knowledge Outcomes
Upon the completion of this chapter, a student should demonstrate the ability to:
• Explain the fundamental role of OOP principles, particularly inheritance
and polymorphism, in the architecture of modern GUI frameworks.
• Describe the JavaFX scene graph as a hierarchical node tree, explicitly iden-
tifying the functions of the Stage, Scene, and root nodes.
• Differentiate between foreground events, such as user-initiated clicks or
keystrokes, and background events, including property-change listeners mon-
itoring window dimensions.

detailing how this mechanism governs the propagation of anActionEvent
from a GUI component to the underlying application logic.

• Evaluate the utilization of state variables and flags in coordinating applica-

modification if a secondary view, such as a battle interface, needed to con-

2. Identify scenarios in which the use of anonymous inner classes for event
handlers introduces maintenance challenges, and evaluate the alternative ap-
proaches provided by the Java language.

67

--- PAGE 89 ---
68 Exception Handling
Expected Outcomes
• Distinguish between checked and unchecked exceptions and explain when
each category applies.
• Implementtry-catch-finally blocks to handle exceptions and maintain
application stability.
• Define and throw custom exception classes tailored to application-specific
error conditions.
• Write lambda expressions as concise replacements for anonymous inner
classes in event handling and collection processing.
• Construct stream pipelines using intermediate operations (map, filter)

A
well-known heuristic principle, commonly referred to as Murphy’ s law
(Bloch, 2003), states thatAnything that can go wrong will go wrong. In
the context of software applications, this concept manifests as a wide
range of potential runtime problems, such as network disruptions, file
access errors, or invalid computational inputs. Such scenarios can lead to critical
system failures, exemplified by the system-halt errors in early versions of the Win-
dows operating system. T o address this class of problems, modern programming
languages, including Java, incorporate the concept of exception handling, which
provides a structured mechanism for managing and mitigating such failures.
It is important to note that exception handling is not a universal solution ca-
pable of preventing all forms of program termination. Rather, it provides a struc-
tured mechanism that enables developers to anticipate, manage, and potentially
recover from anomalous conditions that may arise during program execution. The
exception-handling mechanism in Java allows developers to define alternative ex-
ecution paths within a method, which are invoked when an anomalous condi-
tion, known as an exception, occurs. Such exceptions can originate from various
sources, including invalid arithmetic operations (e.g., division by zero) or resource

--- PAGE 90 ---
2.1. Categorization of Exceptions 69
access issues. This capability facilitates the graceful management of exceptional
circumstances and can prevent abrupt program termination.
2.1 Categorization of Exceptions
Exceptions in Java are classified into two distinct types: checked and unchecked.
The fundamental distinction between these two types is the point at which they are
verified: checked exceptions are verified by the compiler at compile time, whereas
unchecked exceptions are verified only at runtime.
2.1.1 Checked exceptions
As their name implies, checked exceptions are subject to rigorous examination by
the Java compiler. If a method contains code that could potentially throw a checked
exception, the Java compiler enforces a requirement that the method must either
handle the exception internally or declare that it may throw the exception. This
compile-time enforcement ensures that such exceptions are explicitly addressed
by the developer.
Common examples of checked exceptions includeIOException (related to in-
put and output operations) andSQLException (related to database interactions).
These exceptions typically represent recoverable error conditions, and the require-
ment for their explicit declaration encourages the implementation of robust error-
handling mechanisms designed to restore the application to a stable state.
2.1.2 Unchecked exceptions
Conversely, unchecked exceptions are not subject to verification by the compiler.
Their occurrence is contingent upon specific runtime conditions or data values, and
thus cannot be reliably predicted during compilation. Examples includeArrayIn-
dexOutOfBoundsException andArithmeticException (e.g., division by zero), which
are determined by the specific values and operations encountered at runtime.
Unchecked exceptions typically represent logical programming errors or other
conditions from which recovery may not be feasible. TheRuntimeException class
serves as the base class for all unchecked exceptions, encompassing a wide range of

--- PAGE 91 ---
70 Exception Handling
issues such asArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, NullPointerException, and
IllegalArgumentException. Although these exceptions are not enforced at com-
pile time and may seem improbable during development, the principle expressed
by Murphy’ s Law remains relevant:Anything that can go wrong will go wrong .
Therefore, despite the absence of compiler enforcement, diligent developers must
anticipate and handle such exceptions to ensure application robustness and relia-
bility.
2.2 Exception Handling
2.2.1 Catching the exceptions
The fundamental logic of exception handling is analogous to a specialized form of
an if statement, a control flow mechanism that facilitates the conditional logic illus-
trated in the comments of Script2.1. This script further demonstrates the formal
implementation of this mechanism, beginning on Line 8.
This control flow statement, formally known as atry-catch block, provides a
structured approach for managing exceptional conditions. Without such a mecha-
nism, the occurrence of an unhandled exception would lead to an application crash,
as the runtime environment would lack instructions for error recovery. For in-
stance, the system-halt errors common in older operating systems arise from an
inability to resolve a critical, unhandled problem. Although an application cannot
predict the occurrence of all such issues, the developer is responsible for imple-
menting logic that defines the application’ s response to specific categories of ex-
ceptions.
Try-catch block
Prior to examining the design of exception-handling methods, an understanding
of the structure of anException is beneficial. The Java language provides a com-
prehensive hierarchy of predefined exception types to represent various potential
issues, such as those related to input/output (I/O), memory, and arithmetic oper-
ations. Additionally, Java allows developers to create custom exceptions, although

--- PAGE 92 ---
2.2. Exception Handling 71
Script 2.1: Conceptual Overview of Exception Handling ExceptionOverview.java
+ 1 /*
+ 2 if(is no problem occurs) {
+ 3 normal flow
+ 4 } else {
+ 5 exception flow
+ 6 }
+ 7 */
+ 8 public class Launcher {
+ 9 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 10 try {
+ 11 normalFlow();
+ 12 } catch (Exception e) {
+ 13 exceptionFlow();
+ 14 }
+ 15 }
+ 16 }
this practice is less common, given the extensive range of standard exceptions avail-
able.
The fundamental principle of exception handling is the detection and appropri-
ate management of anomalous conditions upon their occurrence. Users typically
expect applications to function seamlessly, even when encountering unexpected
operational issues. For instance, in video games, frequent application crashes that
force players to resume from a distant save point can significantly degrade the user
experience. Therefore, the successful detection and handling of exceptions are cru-
cial for enabling graceful application recovery, thereby preventing data loss and
maintaining a positive user experience.
Scripts2.2, 2.3, and 2.4 present three classes that constitute a concrete exam-
ple of a method call stack. The program reads content from a text file named
dummy.txt, located at a relative path within the project’ sresources folder. The
execution of this program follows these steps:
1. TheLauncher.main method is invoked, which subsequently invokes the con-
structor of theDocument class (Line 3 of Script2.2).

--- PAGE 93 ---
72 Exception Handling
2. Within the constructor of theDocument class, the specified file path is passed
to theFileReader class (Line 4 of Script2.3).
3. Theretrieve() method in theFileReader class reads the content from the
specified file path. It utilizes aScanner to read the file line-by-line and sub-
sequently returns the full content to the calling method (Line 4 of Script2.3).
4. If the retrieval is successful, the content returned by theFileReader.re-
trieve() method is displayed on the standard output by the code in Line 4
of Script2.2.

2.2. Exception Handling 73
Script 2.4: Call Stack Example: The FileReader Class FileReader.java
+ 1 public class FileReader {
+ 2 public static String retrieve(String filepath) {
+ 3 String data = "";
+ 4 InputStream inputStream = getClass().getResourceAsStream(filepath);
+ 5 Scanner myReader = new Scanner(inputStream);
+ 6 while (myReader.hasNextLine()) {
+ 7 data += myReader.nextLine();
+ 8 }
+ 9 myReader.close();
+ 10 return data;
+ 11 }
+ 12 }
However, if the filedummy.txt is not found, an exception of typeFileNot-
FoundException (a subclass ofIOException) will be thrown. This event triggers a
search for a suitable handler, which involves traversing the call stack in reverse
order, starting from the point of failure and moving back through the chain of
method invocations. If no appropriate handler is found within the current method,
the search continues up the call stack. If no handler is located anywhere in the
call stack, the Java Virtual Machine will terminate the program and report an un-
handled exception. The provided scripts do not contain any explicit exception-
handling mechanisms. Consequently, the absence of the specified file will cause
the program to terminate due to this unhandledFileNotFoundException.
The process of handling an exception involves two primary components: the
try block and thecatch block.
• try block: This statement block, as shown in Script2.1, encloses code that
may potentially throw an exception.
• catch block: This statement block, which must immediately follow atry
block, contains the handler logic for a specific type of exception.

--- PAGE 95 ---
74 Exception Handling
Script2.5 provides an example in which an exception handler is added to the
FileReader class from the previous example. The code enclosed within thetry-
catch structure (Lines 4-13) prevents abrupt program termination in the event of
an exception. Instead, it allows for a controlled response, in this case, by notifying
the user of the error and executing the logic defined within thecatch block (Lines
11-13).

However, in practice, it is a recommended practice to handle exceptions using
the most specific exception type applicable, rather than relying on a genericEx-
ception handler. For instance, in file operations, the exceptions that are likely to
be thrown are related to I/O. The most relevant exception class for this domain is
IOException.
T o determine the appropriate exception type, the JavaDoc for theException
class (Oracle, 2026d) serves as the definitive reference. It presents the hierarchy of
exception classes, illustrating the relationships between different types, such as:

Script 2.6: Handling a Specific Exception Type (IOException) FileReader.java
1 public class FileReader {

3 String data = "";
4 try {
...
– 11 } catch (Exception e){

--- PAGE 97 ---
76 Exception Handling
This approach offers several benefits:
• Targeted recovery: Allows for the implementation of distinct recovery ac-
tions tailored to each specific exception type.
• Code clarity: Improves code readability and maintainability by grouping
related error-handling logic.
• Simplified debugging: Facilitates easier troubleshooting and debugging by
isolating error-handling logic for different failure modes.
• Architectural flexibility: Provides the flexibility to handle different excep-
tion types at different levels of the application architecture.
For example, in the context of file operations, I/O-related issues can be decom-
posed into more specific categories, such as:
• A failure to locate the specified file, represented byFileNotFoundException.
• An unexpected end-of-file condition, represented by anEOFException.
Both of these exception types are subclasses ofIOException. Script2.7 demon-
strates how these specific exceptions can be handled in separatecatch blocks (Lines
13-16), thereby allowing for tailored responses based on the specific I/O error en-
countered.
The finally block
A finally block serves as a guaranteed execution block that follows atry block
and any associatedcatch blocks. Its inclusion is optional, providing developers
with flexibility in their implementation. When afinally block is present, the code
within it is guaranteed to execute regardless of whether an exception is thrown or
caught within thetry block. Atry statement can be associated with at most one
finally block.
Although its utility may not be immediately apparent, thefinally block serves
a crucial purpose in robust program design. It provides a designated location for

– 12 System.err.println("Error due to I/O: " + e.getMessage());
+ 13 } catch (FileNotFoundException e){
+ 14 System.err.println("File not found: " + e.getMessage());
+ 15 } catch (EOFException e){
+ 16 System.err.println("The end of the file is reached unexpectedly: " + e.
getMessage());
17 }
18 return data;
19 }
20 }
resource-management code, such as cleanup operations, that must execute under
all possible exit conditions. This design guarantees that essential operations, such
as closing files or releasing system resources, are not bypassed by control-flow state-
ments likecontinue, break, or an unhandled exception. It is therefore a recom-
mended practice to utilize afinally block to ensure the deterministic execution
of such cleanup code. When used in conjunction withcatch blocks, thefinally
block is placed after allcatch blocks. Script2.8 demonstrates a modification of
Script2.5 that incorporates afinally block.
2.2.2 Throwing the exceptions
An alternative to handling an exception within the method where it occurs is to
postpone its handling by propagating the exception up the call stack to the calling
method. This action, referred to as throwing an exception, delegates the responsi-
bility of handling the exception to the caller. For checked exceptions, this propaga-

--- PAGE 99 ---
78 Exception Handling
Script 2.8: Implementation of a try-catch-finally Block FileReader.java
1 public class FileReader {
2 public String retrieve(String filepath) {
3 String data = "";
4 try {
5 Scanner myReader = new Scanner(getClass().getResourceAsStream(filepath));
6 while (myReader.hasNextLine()) {
7 data += myReader.nextLine();
8 }
– 9 myReader.close();
10 } catch (FileNotFoundException e){
11 System.err.println("File not found: " + e.getMessage());
12 } catch (EOFException e){
13 System.err.println("The end of the file is reached unexpectedly: " + e.
getMessage());
+ 14 } finally {
+ 15 System.out.println("Read file done.");
+ 16 myReader.close();
17 }
18 return data;
19 }
20 }
tion must be explicitly declared using thethrows keyword in the method signature.
For unchecked exceptions, athrows declaration is not required, as they are implic-
itly propagated by the runtime system upon encountering an error condition.
In the context of the previous example (Script2.8), thetry-catch block could
be omitted from theretrieve() method of theFileReader class. This would allow
theretrieve() method to propagate any thrown exceptions to theinit() method
of theDocument class, which is its caller. This requires the following modifications:
• For checked exceptions , such asFileNotFoundException andEOFExcep-
tion, the following modifications are necessary:
1. The signature of theretrieve() method in theFileReader class must
be modified to include athrows clause specifyingFileNotFoundEx-

--- PAGE 100 ---
2.2. Exception Handling 79
ception andEOFException.
2. Thetry-catch block is relocated from theFileReader class to the call-
ing method, which is theinit() method in theDocument class.
• For unchecked exceptions, such as a potentialNullPointerException the
following steps are taken:
1. The try-catch block is relocated to the calling method, theinit()
method of theDocument class. An explicitthrows clause is not required
in the method signature for unchecked exceptions.
2. Additionalcatch blocks forRuntimeException or any of its antici-
pated subclasses can be added within theinit() method.
These modifications are presented in Scripts2.9 and2.10. By propagating an
exception, the responsibility for handling it shifts to the calling method, which af-
fords greater flexibility in the overall exception management strategy. In Script2.9,
thetry-catch block is removed from theFileReader class and relocated to the
Document class in Script2.10. The following steps outline the revised exception-
handling process:
1. Upon encountering an exception within theFileReader.retrieve() method,
it propagates to the calling method, in this case, theinit() method of the
Document class, at the point of invocation (fr.retrieve("dummy.txt")).
2. For a checked exception, thethrows keyword is mandatory in theretrieve()
method’ s signature to declare this propagation. For an unchecked exception,
this declaration is not required.
3. Because the point of invocation in the calling method is now enclosed within
a try-catch block, the exception can be handled at this higher level.
4. Alternatively, theDocument method could also declare athrows clause, re-
throwing the exception to propagate it further up the call stack.
5. If an exception propagates to themain method (the top-level entry point in
this example) and is not handled, the application will terminate.

2.2.3 Customized exception
In scenarios where a specific error condition is not represented by any of the pre-
defined exception types in the Java Application Programming Interface (API) (Or-
acle, 2026d), developers have the capability to create custom exception classes. For
instance, if an application must not process empty files and this case should be
handled as an exception, a design decision must be made as to whether the cus-
tom exception should be checked or unchecked. This decision involves choosing a
suitable superclass (e.g.,IOException orRuntimeException) and is guided by the
following considerations:

--- PAGE 103 ---
82 Exception Handling
• Nature of the Exception:
– Checked (IOException): If an empty file is considered an exceptional
but potentially recoverable condition, defining it as a checked excep-
tion ensures that callers are forced by the compiler to either handle it
or declare it.
– Unchecked ( RuntimeException): If an empty file indicates a logical
programming error or a condition that does not require mandatory
handling by the caller, defining it as an unchecked exception simplifies
the calling code.
• Caller Awareness and Handling:
– Checked: This approach forces caller awareness, which is beneficial
when the calling context can meaningfully recover from the error, such
as by providing a default file, prompting the user for action, or logging
a specific message.
– Unchecked: This approach is suitable when the responsibility for han-
dling the error should not be imposed on the caller, often because the
error indicates a bug that should be fixed rather than handled at run-
time.
For the scenario described, a custom exception namedEmptyFileException
is created. Because an empty file represents a recoverable, I/O-related error condi-
tion, extendingIOException is the preferable design choice. This approach ensures
that the calling method is made aware of the potential issue at compile time and
can implement an appropriate response, such as providing a default file, prompting
the user for a different file, or logging the error.
Script2.11 illustrates the implementation of theEmptyFileException class,
which demonstrates its derivation fromIOException in the class signature. Script
2.12 modifies theFileReader class to incorporate the logic for throwing anEmpty-
FileException under the specified condition (Line 9). The method signature for
retrieve() is updated to declare all three potential exception types in itsthrows
clause.
