2.3 Extra Topics: Lambda Expressions and Streams in Java
Introduced in Java 8, lambda expressions provide a concise syntax for representing
anonymous functions. This feature is particularly beneficial when implementing
functional interfaces, which are interfaces that contain a single abstract method.
Lambda expressions facilitate a more functional programming style by emphasiz-
ing a function’ s behavior rather than its formal declaration.
2.3.1 What is a Lambda expression?
Lambda calculus, which serves as a theoretical foundation for functional program-
ming, is a system where all computations are expressed as functions. Java’ s lambda
expressions adopt this principle, allowing for the definition of functions without re-
quiring an explicit name. For instance, the functionsquare_sum(x, y) = x2 + y2

--- PAGE 106 ---
2.3. Extra Topics: Lambda Expressions and Streams in Java 85
can be expressed concisely as(x, y) → x2 + y2 in contexts where an explicit func-
tion name is unnecessary.
Prior to the introduction of lambda expressions in Java 8, anonymous classes
were the primary mechanism for the inline implementation of interfaces, a tech-
nique frequently used in event handling. Script2.14 provides an example from
the case study in Chapter1, where an anonymous class is used to define the event
handler for anImageView object. ThesetOnDragDone() method requires an ob-
ject that implements theEventHandler interface; this requirement is satisfied by
creating an anonymous class that overrides thehandle() method.
Although functional, the syntax for anonymous classes can be verbose, partic-
ularly for simple, single-method implementations where a formal class structure
is unnecessarily cumbersome. In response to this, Java 8 introduced lambda ex-
pressions as a more concise and expressive alternative. Script2.15 demonstrates
the refactoring of the code from Script2.14 using a lambda expression. As demon-
strated, the lambda expression achieves the same functionality as the anonymous
class but with a significantly more compact syntax. The compiler infers that the
provided lambda expression is an implementation of thehandle() method, allow-
ing the event parameter to be passed directly to theonEquipDone() method.
Script 2.14: Event Handler Implemented Using an Anonymous Class
+ 1 imageViewList[i].setOnDragDone(new EventHandler<DragEvent>() {
+ 2 @Override
+ 3 public void handle(DragEvent event) {
+ 4 onEquipDone(event);
+ 5 }
+ 6 });
Script 2.15: Event Handler Implemented Using a Lambda Expression
+ 1 imageViewList[i].setOnDragDone(event -> onEquipDone(event));

--- PAGE 107 ---
86 Exception Handling
2.3.2 Illustrative example: String sorting
The evolution from anonymous classes to lambda expressions can be further illus-
trated through a string-sorting example. Scripts2.16, 2.17, and2.18 each achieve
the same functional outcome, which is the alphabetical sorting of strings, but they
do so using progressively more modern implementation techniques.
Script2.16 presents a solution common prior to Java 1.1, which requires the
implementation of an explicit, customComparator class. An instance of this class
is then passed to theArrays.sort() method. Script2.17 demonstrates the ap-
proach common between Java 1.1 and Java 8, which utilizes an anonymous class
through a Class Instance Creation Expression (CICE). This technique, analogous
to theEventHandler implementation in Script2.14, involves defining the compar-
ison logic within the body of an anonymous class. Finally, Script2.18 presents the
modern, concise alternative, which refactors the anonymous class into a lambda ex-
pression. The lambda expression provides a more streamlined and readable syntax
by directly representing the comparison logic as an anonymous function, thereby
expressing the core functionality without the syntactic overhead of a full class def-
inition.
Script 2.16: String Sorting Using a Custom Comparator Class
+ 1 public class StringLengthComparator implements Comparator {
+ 2 public static final StringLengthComparator INSTANCE = new
StringLengthComparator();
+ 3 public int compare(Object o1, Object o2) {
+ 4 String s1 = ( String) o1, s2 = ( String) o2;
+ 5 return s1.length() - s2.length();
+ 6 }
+ 7
+ 8 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 9 String[] arr = { "ABC","AB","A","ABCD"};
+ 10 Arrays.sort(arr, StringLengthComparator.INSTANCE);
+ 11 System.out.println(Arrays.toString(arr));
+ 12 }
+ 13 }

--- PAGE 108 ---
2.3. Extra Topics: Lambda Expressions and Streams in Java 87
Script 2.17: String Sorting Using an Anonymous Class (CICE)

+ 2 String[] arr = { "ABC","AB","A","ABCD"};
+ 3 Arrays.sort(arr, new Comparator() {
+ 4 @Override
+ 5 public int compare(Object o1, Object o2) {
+ 6 String s1 = ( String) o1, s2 = ( String) o2;
+ 7 return s1.length() - s2.length();
+ 8 }

+ 11 }
Script 2.18: String Sorting Using a Lambda Expression
+ 1 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 2 String[] arr = { "ABC","AB","A","ABCD"};
+ 3 Arrays.sort(arr,(s1, s2) -> s1.length() - s2.length());
+ 4 System.out.println(Arrays.toString(arr));
+ 5 }
Lambda expressions, therefore, represent a powerful tool for writing concise
and expressive code, particularly in scenarios that require the implementation of
single-method interfaces. Their introduction in Java 8 significantly enhanced the
capabilities of the Java language and encouraged the adoption of a more functional
programming style.
2.3.3 Lambda syntax
A lambda expression conforms to a specific syntax composed of three distinct ele-
ments:
1. Parameters: This component defines the input parameters for the anony-
mous function. For multiple parameters, the list is enclosed in parentheses

--- PAGE 109 ---
88 Exception Handling
with parameters separated by commas. Parentheses are optional for a sin-
gle parameter. For a function with no parameters, empty parentheses() are
mandatory. For example:
• x -> x + 1 (single parameter without parentheses)
• (x) -> x + 1 (single parameter with parentheses)
• () -> System.out.println("Hello") (no parameters)
2. Arrow (->): The arrow token (->) serves as a delimiter that separates the
parameter list from the expression’ s body. It signifies a mapping from the
input parameters on the left to the implementation on the right.
3. Body: This component contains the implementation logic of the anonymous
function. It can be one of two forms:
Expression Body: A single expression whose result is implicitly returned
by the function. For example:x -> x + 1 .
Block Body: A block of statements enclosed in curly braces ({}). If
the function returns a value, an explicitreturn statement is required
within the block.
Table2.1 provides further examples, illustrating the syntactic flexibility of lambda
expressions.
2.3.4 Utilizing Lambda expressions
Lambda expressions are frequently employed in event handling, where they of-
ten replace the traditional anonymous class syntax for implementing thehandle()
method. Additionally, they are widely utilized to provide implementations for
functional interfaces from standard libraries, such asComparator andRunnable.
This application results in more concise code, which enhances readability by re-
ducing syntactic complexity and boilerplate.
Modern IDEs, such as IntelliJ IDEA, often provide automated suggestions to
refactor a Class Instance Creation Expression (CICE) into a lambda expression

--- PAGE 110 ---
2.3. Extra Topics: Lambda Expressions and Streams in Java 89
Table 2.1: Syntactic Forms of Java Lambda Expressions with Examples
Syntax Example
parameter -> expression x -> x + 1
// A single-parameter function that returns the result of an
expression.
parameter -> block s -> System.out.println(s);
// A single-parameter function with a block body that per-
forms an action.
parameters -> expression (x, y) -> x + y
// A multi-parameter function that returns the result of an
expression.
parameters -> block (s1, s2) -> System.out.println(s1 + ``," + s2);
// A multi-parameter function with a block body.
parameter(s) with types -> expression (double x, double y) -> Math.sqrt(x * x + y * y)
// A multi-parameter function with explicit type declarations.
parameter(s) with type -> block (List<?> list) -> Arrays.sort(list.toArray());
// A function with an explicit generic type parameter and a
void block body.
where applicable. As depicted in Figure2.1, these suggestions are typically indi-
cated by an icon (e.g., a lightbulb). Accepting this suggestion automatically per-
forms the code transformation.

2.3.5 Introducing streams
The Stream API, introduced in Java 8, provides a mechanism for processing se-
quences of objects. A stream is derived from a source, such as a collection, an
array, or an I/O channel, and supports aggregate operations for efficient bulk data
processing. A standard stream pipeline is composed of three distinct stages:

--- PAGE 111 ---
90 Exception Handling
1. Source: A data source (e.g., aCollection) from which the stream is gener-
ated.
2. Intermediate Operations: A chain of zero or more operations that trans-
form or filter the elements of the stream (e.g.,filter(), map()).
3. T erminal Operation:A single operation that produces a final result or a side
effect and consumes the stream (e.g.,forEach(), collect()).
Stream sources
Script2.19 presents three examples that demonstrate the creation of a Stream from
different data sources. In Line 3, a stream is constructed from a List of strings. In
Line 4, a stream is generated from an array of Integer objects. Finally, in Line 5, a
stream is created from a list of characters. In each of these examples, theforEach()
terminal operation is used to print every element of the resulting stream to the
console.
Script 2.19: Demonstration of Various Stream Sources
+ 1 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 2 List<String> stringList = Arrays.asList("Hello", "World!", "How", "Are", "You");
+ 3 stringList.stream().forEach(a -> System.out.println(a));
+ 4 IntStream.range(0, 10). forEach(a->System.out.println(a));
+ 5 "Hello world!".chars().forEach(x -> System.out.print((char) x));
+ 6 }
Intermediate stream operations
Intermediate operations provide a versatile toolkit for transforming and filtering a
stream’ s elements before a terminal operation is applied to produce a final result.
Common intermediate operations include the following:
• Mapping: Applies a function to each element of the stream, transforming it
into a new element.

--- PAGE 112 ---
2.3. Extra Topics: Lambda Expressions and Streams in Java 91
• Filtering: Selects only the elements from the stream that satisfy a given pred-
icate.
• Distinct: Returns a stream consisting of the unique elements of the original
stream.
• Sorting: Arranges the stream’ s elements according to their natural order or
a specifiedComparator.
Themap() method applies a provided function to each element, transforming
it into a new element. The method’ s argument is typically a lambda expression that
defines this transformation. Script2.20 demonstrates this operation, where the first
character of each string in a list is extracted to create a new stream of characters.
Thefilter() method selects elements from a stream that satisfy a specific con-
dition, or predicate. Its argument is a lambda expression that evaluates to a boolean
value. Script2.21 provides an example where only strings with a length greater than
three characters are retained in the resulting stream.
Thedistinct() operation returns a stream containing only the unique ele-
ments of the source stream, based on theequals() method for comparison. Script
2.22 provides an example in which duplicate characters are eliminated from a list.
Thesorted() operation returns a stream whose elements are arranged accord-
ing to their natural order or a providedComparator. Script2.23 demonstrates this
by arranging a stream of characters into alphabetical order.
Script 2.20: Using the map() Operation to Transform Stream Elements
+ 1 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 2 List<String> stringList = Arrays.asList("Hello", "World!", "How", "Are", "You");
+ 3 List<String> mappedList = stringList.stream()
+ 4 .map(s -> s.substring(0, 1))
+ 5 .collect(Collectors.toList());
+ 6 System.out.println(mappedList);
+ 7 }

+ 3 List<String> sortedList = stringList.stream()

+ 7 System.out.println(sortedList);

Stream parallelism
Parallelism is a technique applicable to stream operations that can significantly en-
hance performance. The overall performance of a stream operation is influenced
by several factors, including the nature of the operations, the size of the data set,
and whether parallelism is employed. Script2.24 presents three functionally equiv-
alent operations that calculate the sum of base-10 logarithms for numbers from1
to108.
Script 2.24 : Performance Comparison of Sequential and Parallel Stream Opera-
tions
+ 1 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 2 long tStart = System.currentTimeMillis();
+ 3 double sum = 0;
+ 4 for(long j=1 ; j<1e8 ; j++)
+ 5 sum += Math.log10(j);
+ 6 long tEnd = System.currentTimeMillis();
+ 7 long elapsedSeconds = tEnd - tStart;
+ 8 System.out.println("sum: " + sum + "\ntime: " + elapsedSeconds + " ms");
+ 9
+ 10 tStart = System.currentTimeMillis();
+ 11 sum = LongStream.range(1, ( long) 1 e8)
+ 12 .mapToDouble(x -> Math.log10(x))
+ 13 .sum();
+ 14 tEnd = System.currentTimeMillis();
+ 15 elapsedSeconds = tEnd - tStart;
+ 16 System.out.println("sum: " + sum + "\ntime: " + elapsedSeconds + " ms");
+ 17
+ 18 tStart = System.currentTimeMillis();
+ 19 sum = LongStream.range(1, ( long) 1 e8)
+ 20 .parallel()
+ 21 .mapToDouble(x -> Math.log10(x))
+ 22 .sum();
+ 23 tEnd = System.currentTimeMillis();
+ 24 elapsedSeconds = tEnd - tStart;
+ 25 System.out.println("sum: " + sum + "\ntime: " + elapsedSeconds + " ms");
+ 26 }

--- PAGE 115 ---
94 Exception Handling
Lines 2-8 of the script compute the sum using a traditionalfor-loop that iter-
ates the variablej from1 to108, where the variablesum accumulates the total. In
contrast, the code in Lines 10-15 performs the same calculation using a sequential
stream, which is created from a range oflong values in Line 11. AmapToDouble()
operation is then applied (Line 12), which uses a lambda expression (Math::log10)
to transform eachlong element into adouble representing its base-10 logarithm.
Subsequently, thesum() terminal operation (Line 13) aggregates thesedouble val-
ues to produce the final result.
A significant advantage of the Stream API is its built-in support for paralleliza-
tion. By incorporating theparallel() intermediate operation, the stream pro-
cessing can be partitioned into subtasks and distributed across multiple CPU cores,
potentially leading to substantial performance gains. This is achieved by invoking
the.parallel() method on the stream. In the provided example (Script2.24), it
is added at Line 20, while the remaining operations are identical to the sequential
stream implementation.
In this specific example, execution on a 2020 MacBook Air with an M1 CPU
yielded an average execution time of 3.31 seconds over three runs for thefor-loop
implementation, while the sequential stream implementation averaged 3.78 sec-
onds. This performance difference is attributable to the overhead associated with
creating and managing the stream pipeline for the sequential operation. However,
when parallelized, the stream’ s average execution time decreased to 1.72 seconds,
which demonstrates a substantial performance improvement over both the tradi-
tionalfor-loop and the sequential stream. This improvement is a direct result of
utilizing multiple processing cores, enabling the computation to be divided into
smaller, independent tasks and executed concurrently. Although there is an inher-
ent overhead in managing parallelism, the ability to leverage multiple computa-
tional units often outweighs this cost, resulting in faster overall execution.
Parallel streams are most effective when the operations performed on the stream
elements are independent and stateless, meaning the outcome for one element does
not affect another. Additionally, parallelism typically yields greater benefits for
large datasets, as the overhead of parallelization becomes less significant relative
to the computational gains. Furthermore, the underlying data source must be ef-
ficiently splittable into sub-problems for parallel execution. It is crucial to bench-
mark performance both with and without parallelization to verify that a net per-

--- PAGE 116 ---
2.3. Extra Topics: Lambda Expressions and Streams in Java 95
formance gain is achieved. In some cases, particularly for small datasets or oper-
ations with inherent sequential dependencies, the overhead of parallelization can
outweigh its benefits.
T erminal stream operations
A terminal operation consumes a Java Stream to produce a final result or a side
effect. In contrast to intermediate operations, which return a new stream and en-
able pipeline chaining, a terminal operation concludes the pipeline. Some common
terminal operations include:
• anyMatch(Predicate<T>), allMatch(Predicate<T>), andnoneMatch(Pred-
icate<T>): These operations test whether any, all, or no elements of the
stream match a given predicate.
• collect(Collector<T, A, R>) : Performs a mutable reduction operation
on the elements of the stream, accumulating them into a container such as
aList orMap. This versatile operation utilizes predefinedCollector imple-
mentations for common tasks.
• count(): Returns a count of the elements in the stream.
• findFirst() andfindAny(): These operations return anOptional describ-
ing the first element of the stream (findFirst), or an arbitrary element of
the stream (findAny).
• forEach(Consumer<T>): Performs a specified action for each element of the
stream. This operation is typically used for its side effects, such as printing
elements to a console.
• min(Comparator<T>) andmax(Comparator<T>): Return the minimum or
maximum element of the stream according to a providedComparator.
• reduce(T, BinaryOperator<T>) : Performs a reduction on the elements of
the stream, using an associative accumulation function, and returns anOp-
tional describing the reduced value.

--- PAGE 117 ---
96 Exception Handling
• sum(): Returns the sum of elements in a primitive numeric stream (e.g.,
IntStream, LongStream, DoubleStream).
• toArray(): Collects the elements of the stream into a new array.
A comprehensive list and detailed specifications for all intermediate and termi-
nal stream operations are available in the official JavaDoc documentation (Oracle,
2026e).
Example: Prime number generation
Script2.25 presents an implementation of a prime number generator that utilizes
streams and lambda expressions. TheprimeNumbers(int n) function employs a
stream pipeline for this purpose. Initially,IntStream.rangeClosed(2, n) cre-
ates a stream of primitive integers from 2 to n. An intermediatefilter() oper-
ation is then applied, which retains only the elements for which theisPrime()
predicate method returnstrue. The subsequent boxed() method converts the
IntStream into aStream<Integer>, which allows the use of thecollect(Collec-
tors.toList()) terminal operation to accumulate the resulting prime numbers
into aList<Integer>.
The helper method,isPrime(int number) , determines the primality of a given
integer. It accomplishes this by generating anIntStream for the range from 2 to the
square root of the input number and utilizing thenoneMatch() terminal operation
to test for divisibility. IfnoneMatch() returnstrue, indicating that no divisors were
found, the number is determined to be prime.
Notably, theIntStream.rangeClosed() method is functionally equivalent to
a traditionalfor loop for generating a sequence of integers. As a potential en-
hancement, the.parallel() operation could be incorporated into this stream
pipeline to leverage multi-threaded computation, which may improve performance
for large input ranges.

--- PAGE 118 ---
2.3. Extra Topics: Lambda Expressions and Streams in Java 97
Script 2.25: Prime Number Generation Using a Stream Pipeline
+ 1 public class PrimeStream {
+ 2 public static List<Integer> primeNumbers(int n) {
+ 3 return IntStream.rangeClosed(2, n)
+ 4 .filter(x -> isPrime(x)).boxed()
+ 5 .collect(Collectors.toList());
+ 6 }
+ 7
+ 8 private static boolean isPrime(int number) {
+ 9 return IntStream.rangeClosed(2, ( int) ( Math.sqrt(number)))
+ 10 .noneMatch(x -> number % x == 0);
+ 11 }
+ 12
+ 13 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 14 long tStart = System.currentTimeMillis();
+ 15 System.out.println(primeNumbers((int) 1 e6));
+ 16 long tEnd = System.currentTimeMillis();
+ 17 long elapsedSeconds = tEnd - tStart;
+ 18 System.out.println("time: " + elapsedSeconds + " ms");
+ 19 }
+ 20 }
Example: Estimating area between curves
Figure 2.2: Area Estimation Between Two Curves

--- PAGE 119 ---
98 Exception Handling
This example demonstrates the estimation of the area enclosed between two curves,
as highlighted in Figure2.2. A straightforward approach to this problem is the par-
titioning of the integration range into two segments: [0, π/4]) and [π/4, π/2]. The
area for the first segment is determined by the integral of cos(x)−sin(x), while the
area for the second is determined by the integral of sin(x) − cos(x). This approach
approximates the total area by subdividing these segments into numerous smaller
intervals, calculating the area for each sub-interval, and summing the results.
Script2.26 presents a solution to this problem that utilizes Java Streams and
lambda expressions. Although the mathematical problem may appear complex, the
conciseness of the implementation highlights the expressive power of Java Streams
and lambda expressions. A general-purpose method,AreaUnderCurve(), is pro-
vided to perform this numerical integration for any two given curves. This imple-
mentation first defines a functional interface,DoubleFunction, to represent math-
ematical functions that accept adouble input and return adouble output. The
AreaUnderCurve() method operates by partitioning a given interval [start, end]
into a large number of sub-intervals. It then uses a parallelLongStream to cal-
culate the differential area between the upper and lower function bounds for each
sub-interval. These differential areas are then summed to produce the final approx-
imation of the total area. Themain() method demonstrates this by calculating the
area between the cosine and sine curves for the two separate intervals and summing
the results.
2.3.6 Map and Reduce paradigm
The map and reduce paradigm is a fundamental concept in parallel and distributed
computing and is widely employed in technologies designed for large-scale data
processing, such as Hadoop and MongoDB.
• Map(f , x): This operation applies a function (f) to each element within an
input collection (x), producing a new collection of the same size where each
element corresponds to the result of applying (f) to an element in (x).
• Reduce(f , x): This operation iteratively combines the elements of a collec-
tion (x) using an associative function (f), progressively reducing the collec-
tion to a single output value.

--- PAGE 120 ---
2.3. Extra Topics: Lambda Expressions and Streams in Java 99
Script 2.26: Numerical Integration Using Streams and Lambda Expressions
+ 1 public class AUCStream {
+ 2 public interface DoubleFunction {
+ 3 public double f(double x);
+ 4 }
+ 5 public static double AreaUnderCurve( DoubleFunction upper, DoubleFunction lower,
double start, double end, int chunk) {
+ 6 double piece = ( end - start)/chunk;
+ 7 return LongStream.range(0, chunk)
+ 8 .parallel()
+ 9 .mapToDouble(x -> upper.f(start+x*piece)*piece - lower.f(start+x*piece)*
piece)
+ 10 .sum();
+ 11 }
+ 12
+ 13 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 14 double area1 = AreaUnderCurve(x->Math.cos(x), x->Math.sin(x),0,Math.PI/4,(int)1
e6);
+ 15 double area2 = AreaUnderCurve(x->Math.sin(x), x->Math.cos(x),Math.PI/4,Math.PI
/2,(int)1e6);
+ 16 System.out.println(area1+area2);
+ 17 }
+ 18 }
In the context of Java Streams, the conceptual reduce operation is often imple-
mented using terminal operations that perform grouping or aggregation, such as
collect().
Example: Word count using Map and Reduce
Script2.27 demonstrates a word-counting implementation using the map and re-
duce paradigm within a Java Stream. The implementation involves the following
steps:
• A string variable,text, is defined, and aPattern object is created to split the
text into words based on whitespace.

--- PAGE 121 ---
100 Exception Handling
• ThesplitAsStream() method generates an initial stream of words. Amap()
operation is then applied to clean each word by removing non-alphabetic
characters and converting it to lowercase.
• A filter() operation removes any empty strings that may have resulted
from the cleaning process.
• A subsequentmap() operation transforms each word into aSimpleEntry
(a key-value pair), with the word as the key and the integer 1 as its value,
representing a single occurrence.
• Thecollect() terminal operation, which serves as the reduce step, aggre-
gates the stream into aMap<String, Integer> . The toMap() collector is
used with a merge function(v1, v2) -> v1 + v2 to sum the counts for
duplicate keys (words).
• Finally, theforEach() method is used to iterate over the resulting map and
print each word and its final count.
Script 2.27: Word Count Implementation Using the Map-Reduce Paradigm with
Java Streams
+ 1 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 2 String text = "Java is a programming language. Python is also a programming
language";
+ 3 Pattern pattern = Pattern.compile(" ");
+ 4 Map<String, Integer> wordCount = pattern.splitAsStream(text)
+ 5 .map(word -> word.replaceAll("[^a-zA-Z]", "").toLowerCase().trim())
+ 6 .filter(word -> word.length() > 0)
+ 7 .map(word -> new AbstractMap.SimpleEntry<>(word, 1))
+ 8 .collect(toMap(e -> e.getKey(), e -> e.getValue(), ( v1, v2) -> v1 + v2));
+ 9 wordCount.forEach((k, v) -> System.out.println(String.format("%s: %d", k, v)));
+ 10 }

2.4. Case Study 101
2.4 Case Study
AI Advisory
Exception handling is a topic where AI tools produce syntactically cor-
rect code almost every time, and where that code can still be danger-
ously wrong. A broadcatch (Exception e) { e.printStackTrace();
} block will compile and silence every error, but it teaches nothing about
the actual failure mode and leaves the application in an undefined state. At
this stage of the course, the objective is to recognizewhy an exception oc-
curs, not merely how to suppress it.
Recommended Approach
Before accepting any AI-generated catch block, the developer must first
identify what has actually gone wrong and whether the handler is recover-
ing from the problem or merely hiding it. A network timeout, a malformed
JSON response, and an unsupported currency code are three fundamentally
different problems; collapsing them into a single generic handler removes
the diagnostic information that makes debugging possible. Similarly, when
working with lambda expressions and stream pipelines, each intermediate
operation (filter, map, sorted) should be describable in plain language
before it is included. An AI can generate a syntactically valid pipeline in
seconds, but a pipeline that cannot be explained step by step will become a
maintenance liability as the application grows.
Appropriate Uses
✓ Use an AI to explain the exception hierarchy (Throwable → Exception
→ RuntimeException) if the class diagram is unclear.
✓ Use an AI to verify whether a specific exception is checked or
unchecked, then confirm the answer against the official JavaDoc.

--- PAGE 123 ---
102 Exception Handling
✓ Use an AI to show the anonymous inner class equivalent of a lambda
expression, to verify that the transformation is understood.
Practices to Avoid
× Do not ask an AI to fix a compile error caused by an unhandled checked
exception without first understanding why the compiler requires it.
× Do not allow an AI to write the catch block for a network call without
specifying which failure modes the application must distinguish.
× Do not use an AI-generated stream pipeline without being able to de-
scribe the purpose of each intermediate and terminal operation.
The pattern-recognition developed through lambda expressions and
streams in this chapter makes it substantially safer to evaluate AI-generated
code in subsequent chapters, where these constructs reappear in increas-
ingly complex contexts such as concurrent task management and test fixture
construction.

seven-day period in a line chart.

A custom API service has been developed specifically for this case study. The
service is implemented using a technology stack orchestrated by Amazon W eb Ser-
vices (A WS, 2026). This API is composed of several A WS components, including
A WS Lambda, Amazon S3, Amazon EventBridge, and the A WS API Gateway. De-
tailed information regarding the API’ s implementation is provided in Appendix B
of this coursebook. The following is a guide to using this Currency Exchange API.
2.4.1 Currency exchange API
The API endpoint provides historical exchange rate data between two specified cur-
rencies across a designated time frame. It can be accessed utilizing the Hypertext
Transfer Protocol (HTTP) GET method athttps://cmu.to/SE233currencyapi
Parameters
The following parameters are accepted via the HTTP GET method upon querying
the API endpoint:
• base (str): The base currency for the exchange rate (for instance, THB). This
parameter is mandatory and must be chosen from the supported currencies
list.

--- PAGE 126 ---
2.4. Case Study 105
• symbol (str): The target currency for the exchange rate (for instance, EUR).
This parameter is mandatory and must be chosen from the supported cur-
rencies list.
• start_date (str): The starting date for the exchange rate history (for instance,
2026-05-01). This parameter is mandatory and should be in YYYY-MM-DD
format.
• end_date (str): The ending date for the exchange rate history (for instance,
2026-05-07). This parameter is mandatory and should be in the YYYY-MM-
DD format.
Constraints
• The maximum date range for the query is 30 days.
• The earliest allowable start date is 90 days prior to the query date.
• Both the start_date and end_date cannot exceed the current date.
Supported currencies
Table 2.2: Supported Currency Codes and Names
Code Currency Name Code Currency Name
AUD Australian DollarINR Indian Rupee
CAD Canadian Dollar JPY Japanese Y en
CNY Chinese Y uan MYR Malaysian Ringgit
EUR Euro PHP Philippine Peso
GBP British Pound SterlingSGD Singapore Dollar
HKD Hong Kong DollarTHB Thai Baht
IDR Indonesian Rupiah USD US Dollar
Return value
The API endpoint responds with a JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) object com-
prising the following fields:

--- PAGE 127 ---
106 Exception Handling
• message (str): Indicates the status of the request. It will beSuccess if the
request was successful, otherwise, it will contain an error message.
• base (str): The base currency (identical to the input parameter).
• symbol (str): The target currency (identical to the input parameter).
• start_date (str): The starting date (identical to the input parameter).
• end_date (str): The ending date (identical to the input parameter).
• rates (dict): A dictionary wherein the keys are dates (YYYY-MM-DD) and
the values are the corresponding exchange rates for the specified base and
symbol currencies.
Example query and response
For example, a query for the exchange rate between THB and EUR for the period of
2026-05-01 to 2026-05-07 would be constructed using the following Uniform Re-
source Locator (URL):https://cmu.to/SE233currencyapi?base=THB&symbol=
EUR&start_date=2026-05-01&end_date=2026-05-07
A successful request to this URL returns a JSON object with the following struc-
ture and data:
{
"message": "Success",
"base": "THB",
"symbol": "EUR",
"start_date": "2026-05-01",
"end_date": "2026-05-07",
"rates": {
"2026-05-01": 38.274600982666016,
"2026-05-02": 38.274600982666016,
"2026-05-03": 38.274600982666016,
"2026-05-04": 38.274600982666016,
"2026-05-05": 38.28959655761719,
"2026-05-06": 38.01139831542969,
"2026-05-07": 37.92490005493164
}
}

+ 26 watchString.setStyle("-fx-font-size: 14;");
+ 27 if (this.currency != null) {
+ 28 exchangeString.setText(String.format("%s: %.4f",this.currency.getShortCode
(),this.currency.getCurrency().getRate()));
+ 29 if(this.currency.getWatch() == true) {
+ 30 watchString.setText(String.format("(Watch @%.4f)",this.currency.
getWatchRate()));
+ 31 }
+ 32 }
+ 33 currencyInfoPane.getChildren().addAll(exchangeString,watchString);
+ 34 return currencyInfoPane;
+ 35 }
+ 36 private HBox genTopArea() {

2.4.5 The controller components
TheLauncher class is subsequently modified to assemble and display all previously
implemented view components. TheInitialize class, presented in Script2.38, is
responsible for creating and initializing the primaryCurrency object used through-
out the application. Script2.39 presents the updatedLauncher class. As shown in
Line 16, theinitMainPane() method is invoked to initialize theTopPane andCur-
rencyPane instances. At this juncture, the only remaining view component to be
implemented is theGraphPane.
Script2.40 illustrates a class namedDrawGraphTask responsible for generat-
ing the visual elements of the graph,. This class implements the JavaCallable
interface, a standard interface that allows an object’ s logic to be executed as a back-
ground task concurrently with the main application thread. The fundamentals of
background processes and threads will be explained in the subsequent chapter. The
call() method serves as the entry point for this background task. The method it-
erates through the currency data points stored in thehistorical variable of the
Currency instance (Lines 20-24). Subsequently, it plots these data points onto the
graph according to their calculated (x, y) coordinates (Line 29). The resulting graph
is then added to theGraphPane (Line 31), which is subsequently returned to the
caller (Line 32). The invocation of theDrawGraphTask’ scall() method is man-
aged by therefreshPane() method, as shown in Script2.41.

+ 30 }

Upon the completion of this step, the application will render the GUI depicted
in Figure2.6, with only the Refresh button functionality implemented. However,
for the purposes of this case study, a hypothetical scenario is introduced wherein a
client requests several functional enhancements. Specifically, whereas the current
version permits monitoring only a single currency, a more practical requirement
is the concurrent monitoring of multiple currencies. T o accommodate this, the
Change button is to be replaced with dedicated Add and Delete buttons. Further-
more, the implementation must satisfy all remaining unfulfilled requirements from
the initial specification. Upon the integration of these new requirements, the final
application will feature an updated GUI that reflects this enhanced functionality.

--- PAGE 143 ---
122 Exception Handling
Figure 2.6: GUI of the Currency Watcher Case Study Application Af-
ter Completing the Refresh Button Functionality
2.4.7 Requirement change handling
In response to the new requirement to handle multiple currency objects concur-
rently, a modification of the underlying data structure is necessary. The transition
from representing the model as a single object to a collection is required to support
the dynamic addition and removal of currencies. For this purpose, anArrayList
is an appropriate data structure.
First, the change from a single currency object to aList (the superinterface
ofArrayList) is propagated to theInitialize class to ensure data model consis-
tency, as shown in Script2.45.
The management of these multiple currency objects introduces the require-
ment to dynamically update the view. This involves assembling the various view
sub-components for each currency into a single, cohesive presentation. Because
theList can store multiple currencies, the application must be capable of display-
ing multipleCurrencyPane instances concurrently. A dedicated controller com-
ponent is therefore required to manage this collection ofCurrencyPane objects.
Script2.46 introduces such a controller class.
Finally, theLauncher class is refactored to use theList data model and incor-
porate the new controller. As illustrated in Script2.47, theLauncher now orches-
trates the creation and management of the collection of currency panes, completing
the transition to a multi-currency architecture.

2.4.8 Background process
In addition to the event handler for the W atch button, the watch functionality re-
quires a background process to periodically refresh the application’ s data. For ex-
ample, to monitor the watch rate, the application must be updated at a regular in-
terval, e.g., every five seconds. This requires the implementation of a recurring task
on a background thread that executes the following sequence during each cycle:
1. Pauses execution for a specified interval (e.g., five seconds).
2. Invokes a refresh of all application components to fetch the latest data.
3. Compares the current exchange rates against the user-defined watch rates
and issues a notification if the condition is met.
TheRefreshTask class, shown in Script2.54, contains the logic for this recur-
ring background process. It implements a loop that manages the five-second inter-
val and subsequently invokes therefreshPane() method in theLauncher class to
trigger a full application refresh.

--- PAGE 152 ---
2.4. Case Study 131
Script 2.54: controller/RefreshTask.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class RefreshTask extends Task<Void> {
+ 3 @Override
+ 4 protected Void call() throws InterruptedException {
+ 5 for (;;) {
+ 6 try {
+ 7 Thread.sleep((long) (60 * 1 e3));
+ 8 } catch (InterruptedException e) {
+ 9 System.out.println("Encountered an interrupted exception");
+ 10 }
+ 11 Platform.runLater(new Runnable() {
+ 12 @Override
+ 13 public void run() {
+ 14 try {
+ 15 Launcher.refreshPane();
+ 16 List<Currency> allCurrency = Launcher.getCurrencyList();
+ 17 String found = "";
+ 18 for(int i = 0 ; i < allCurrency.size() ; i++) {
+ 19 if (allCurrency.get(i).getWatchRate()!= 0 && allCurrency.get(i).
getWatchRate() > allCurrency.get(i).getCurrency().getRate()) {
+ 20 if(found.equals(""))
+ 21 found = allCurrency.get(i).getShortCode();
+ 22 else
+ 23 found = found + " and " + allCurrency.get(i).getShortCode();
+ 24 }
+ 25 }
+ 26 if (!found.equals("")) {
+ 27 Alert alert = new Alert(Alert.AlertType.WARNING);
+ 28 alert.setTitle(null);
+ 29 alert.setHeaderText(null);
+ 30 if(found.length()>3) {
+ 31 alert.setContentText(String.format("%s have become lower than
the watch rate!",found));
+ 32 } else {
+ 33 alert.setContentText(String.format("%s has become lower than the
watch rates!",found));
+ 34 alert.showAndWait();
+ 35 }
+ 36 }
+ 37 } catch (Exception e) {
+ 38 e.printStackTrace();
+ 39 }
+ 40 }
+ 41 });
+ 42 }
+ 43 }
+ 44 }

--- PAGE 153 ---
132 Exception Handling
Subsequently, theLauncher class is updated as shown in Script2.55. This mod-
ification involves initializing and starting a new thread that executes an instance of
theRefreshTask class. ThePlatform.runLater() method is employed to ensure
that any GUI updates originating from the background thread are safely executed
on the main JavaFX Application Thread, thereby preventing potential concurrency
issues.

A remaining user experience issue is that the background thread continues to
run after a watch notification is triggered, resulting in redundant alerts. T o resolve
this, the background task is modified to pause until the notification is dismissed by
the user. This is achieved by transferring the notification logic from theRefresh-
Task class to a newWatchTask class that implements thejavafx.
concurrent.Task interface, as shown in Scripts2.56 and2.57.
The completion of these modifications brings the application close to fulfill-
ing the initial requirements. Remaining functionalities are intentionally left unad-
dressed and are proposed as end-of-chapter exercises.

+ 58 System.out.println("Encountered an execution exception");
+ 59 }
...

The use of rawThread objects andPlatform.runLater() is sufficient for sim-

2.6. Discussion and Summary 137
2.6 Discussion and Summary
This chapter has addressed two complementary topics. The first is exception han-
dling, which provides a structured mechanism for managing anomalous runtime
conditions. The discussion began with the categorization of exceptions into checked
and unchecked types, proceeded through the mechanics oftry-catch-finally
blocks and exception propagation viathrow andthrows, and concluded with the
design of custom exception classes. During the application design phase, all po-
tentialException types must be anticipated, and a strategy must be formulated to
determine where and how each exception should be handled. This process consti-
tutes a form of risk management, guided by the principle that user-triggered excep-
tions should prompt corrective input, sensible defaults should be assumed where
possible, and informative error messages should guide subsequent actions. The
consistent practice of exception handling encourages the separation of normal and
exceptional program flows, which is a critical skill for both academic coursework
and professional development.
The second topic is lambda expressions and streams, introduced as a more
concise and expressive syntax for patterns frequently encountered in event-driven
GUI programming. Lambda expressions reduce the verbosity of anonymous in-
ner classes when implementing functional interfaces, while the stream API enables
declarative data processing through pipelines of intermediate and terminal opera-
tions. The map-reduce paradigm, applied to aggregate and transform collections,
provides a foundation for parallel data processing that extends naturally into the
concurrency concepts presented in Chapter3.
The case study synthesized both topics into a functional Currency W atcher ap-
plication. Exception handling was applied to manage network failures and invalid
API responses, while lambda expressions simplified the event handler implementa-
tions. The introduction of a background refresh task within the case study further
demonstrated how these concepts interact in a realistic, multi-component applica-
tion.
Knowledge Outcomes
Upon the completion of this chapter, a student should demonstrate the ability to:

--- PAGE 159 ---
138 Exception Handling
• Classify a given Java exception as checked or unchecked, and explain the im-
plications of each category for method signatures and caller responsibilities.
• Trace the execution flow through atry-catch-finally block, including the
ordering of multiplecatch clauses from most specific to most general.
• Explain the role of thethrows keyword in method declarations and describe
how exception propagation transfers responsibility to calling methods.
• Justify the design decision to create a custom exception class rather than
reusing a standard Java exception type.
• Define a lambda expression in terms of its relationship to a functional inter-
face and explain how the compiler resolves the target type.
• Describe the distinction between intermediate operations (filter, map, sorted)
and terminal operations (reduce, collect, forEach) in a stream pipeline.

rence versus propagating them to a centralized handler, using the Currency
W atcher API integration as a concrete example.

presented in this chapter.

M
odern commodity computers are commonly equipped with mul-
ticore central processing units (CPUs), facilitating the concurrent
execution of multiple tasks. This capability is evident in common
computing scenarios, where activities such as application develop-
ment and media playback occur simultaneously on a single machine. Moreover,
multicore CPUs can be leveraged to execute computationally demanding tasks in
parallel, such as graphic rendering or simulations. However, the utilization of this
parallel processing power is not an automatic feature of software; it requires the de-
liberate design of concurrent programs to effectively manage hardware resources
and achieve performance improvements.
Conventional programming instruction often focuses on a sequential program-
ming model, in which instructions are executed in a single, linear sequence. While
effective for many applications, this sequential approach fails to utilize the parallel

--- PAGE 166 ---
Concurrency 145
processing capabilities of modern hardware, typically leaving most CPU cores idle.
Concurrent programming offers an alternative paradigm, enabling applications to
harness the power of multiple cores for improved performance and responsiveness.
An analogy can be used to illustrate the benefits and challenges of concurrency.
Consider a group academic project. Ideally, the division of labor allows for faster
completion than if one person performed all the work, potentially by a factor equal
to the number of group members. However, this ideal speedup may not always
be realized. Excessive communication overhead, such as frequent meetings, can
reduce overall project efficiency. Conversely, insufficient communication can lead
to integration errors that are discovered late in the process, causing further delays.
Similarly, achieving optimal speedup with concurrency in software develop-
ment is challenging due to the inherent overhead of inter-thread communication or
synchronization. If a computational problem can be partitioned into independent
subtasks that require no inter-communication, a near-linear performance speedup
may be attainable. Therefore, achieving an optimal balance between task decom-
position and communication overhead is a crucial aspect of concurrent program
design.
The fundamental units of execution in concurrent programming are processes
and threads. A process is an instance of a running program that operates within
an isolated memory space with dedicated resources; the concurrent execution of
multiple processes is known as multiprocessing. A thread, by contrast, is a more
lightweight unit of execution that exists within a process and shares its resources.
This model, where a single process executes multiple instruction streams concur-
rently, is known as multithreading. The primary distinction between these two
mechanisms lies in memory allocation. Because threads share the same memory
space, inter-thread communication is typically less resource-intensive, and thread
creation has lower overhead compared to process creation. This shared memory
model generally affords multithreading a significant performance advantage for
many applications.

--- PAGE 167 ---
146 Concurrency
3.1 Purpose
3.1.1 Concurrency and GUI programming
Figure 3.1: A JavaFX Application
Demonstrating Concurrency Prin-
ciples
Multithreading is a fundamental technique
for developing responsive and efficient graph-
ical user interfaces (GUIs). By allowing mul-
tiple threads to execute concurrently, multi-
threading enables an application to perform
background tasks while maintaining an in-
teractive user interface (UI). This capability
is particularly important for computationally
intensive operations, during which the UI
must remain responsive to user input.
A key advantage of multithreading in UI
design is the ability to facilitate inter-thread communication and interruption. For
instance, consider a video editing application where a rendering process is initiated
on a separate thread. This design allows the user to continue interacting with the
UI without interruption. If the user chooses to cancel the operation, a separate
thread can signal the rendering thread to terminate, demonstrating the flexibility
afforded by this concurrent approach.
In contrast, a single-threaded application would become unresponsive during
such time-consuming operations. Figure3.1 illustrates this problem: if a compu-
tationally intensive task is triggered by a button click and executed on the same
thread that handles GUI events, the application will appear to freeze until the task
completes. This lack of responsiveness significantly degrades the user experience,
particularly in applications that require continuous interaction.
T o mitigate the responsiveness issues inherent in single-threaded GUI appli-
cations, a standard design pattern employs at least two types of threads: the Event
Dispatch Thread (EDT) and one or more worker threads. The EDT is dedicated
exclusively to processing all GUI-related events, ensuring the prompt handling of
user input. W orker threads, by contrast, are responsible for executing the long-
running tasks triggered by these events.
T o maintain UI responsiveness, all operations that modify the GUI must be

--- PAGE 168 ---
3.1. Purpose 147
executed on the EDT . Therefore, a fundamental principle of concurrent GUI pro-
gramming is to delegate any time-consuming operations, such as file input/output
(I/O), network requests, or complex calculations, to separate worker threads. By
following this principle, developers can prevent the GUI from becoming unrespon-
sive during these operations and ensure a smooth, interactive user experience.
3.1.2 Concurrency and accelerated computing
Multiprocessing is frequently employed for computationally intensive tasks where
increased processing speed is the primary objective. In such scenarios, the operat-
ing system can allocate dedicated CPU cores and isolated memory spaces to each
process, which can simplify development by reducing the need for explicit synchro-
nization mechanisms. Multithreading, by contrast, operates on a shared memory
model. This requires developers to explicitly manage resource access to prevent
concurrency hazards such as data races, introducing additional implementation
complexity. Despite this added complexity, the benefits of multithreading often
extend beyond raw performance improvements.
A common consideration in concurrent programming is the magnitude of per-
formance improvement that can be achieved. The degree of performance gain de-
pends on several factors, most notably the nature of the algorithm and the available
hardware resources. For computationally intensive tasks, such as those common
in data science, the impact of concurrency can be substantial.
For example, a study by Phannachitta and Matsumoto (2019) involved con-
ducting thousands of experiments to compare the performance of various ma-
chine learning algorithms on an empirical software engineering problem. Execut-
ing these experiments on a standard dual-core computer required nearly a month
of computation time. However, by migrating the workload to a render farm equipped
with dual 8-core Intel XEON CPUs, the total execution time was reduced to less
than one week.
A subsequent, larger-scale experiment was conducted as part of research by
Phannachitta (2020). By leveraging high-performance computing resources avail-
able through the Amazon W eb Services (A WS) Cloud Credits for Research Pro-
gram, this more complex experiment was completed in several days. Further tests
utilizing a high-performance A WS instance (c5.24xlarge) demonstrated that a large-

--- PAGE 169 ---
148 Concurrency
scale data processing project, which would have required months of computation
on a standard machine, could be completed in approximately one day. However,
the significant operational cost of such high-performance instances raises concerns
regarding their cost-effectiveness for applications where the return on investment
is uncertain. For example, at the time of the study, the on-demand rental cost for a
c5.24xlarge instance could exceed one million baht per year.
Synchronization
The fundamental principle of synchronization in concurrent programming is to
coordinate the execution of multiple threads to prevent interference, particularly
when accessing shared resources. This coordination is crucial for maintaining data
integrity and consistency. Without effective synchronization, race conditions can
occur, leading to unpredictable and erroneous program behavior.
Script 3.1: Illustrating a Race Condition with Unsynchronized Bank Transactions
+ 1 int withdraw (int account, int amount) {
+ 2 double balance = readBalance(account);
+ 3 double balance = balance –amount;
+ 4 updateBalance(account, balance);
+ 5 return balance;
+ 6 }
+ 7 int deposit (int account, int amount) {
+ 8 double balance = readBalance(account);
+ 9 double balance = balance + amount;
+ 10 updateBalance(account, balance);
+ 11 return balance;
+ 12 }
Consider a scenario involving two threads operating on a shared bank account:
one depositing funds and the other withdrawing, a scenario illustrated by the pseu-
docode in Script3.1. Without synchronization, both threads could read the initial
balance concurrently. If the deposit thread adds funds and writes the new balance,
its update could be immediately overwritten by the withdrawal thread, which was

--- PAGE 170 ---
3.1. Purpose 149
operating on the stale, initial balance. This would result in a final balance that in-
correctly reflects only the withdrawal, compromising the integrity of the data.
Script3.2 illustrates this problem through a minimal, executable example. In
this script, two threads concurrently increment a sharedcounter variable 100,000
times. Because thecounter++ statement is not an atomic operation, it necessitates
three distinct steps: reading the current value, incrementing that value by one, and
writing the result back to memory. Consequently, the execution of these steps by
the two threads can interleave. A thread may read the variable’ s value before the
competing thread has completed its write operation, resulting in the silent loss of
increments. Executing this program reliably produces a final count significantly
below the expected 200,000, with the exact numerical result varying between exe-
cutions.
Script 3.2: Data Race Two Threads Incrementing a Shared Counter Without Syn-
chronization
+ 1 static int counter = 0;
+ 2
+ 3 public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
+ 4 Runnable task = () -> {
+ 5 for (int i = 0; i < 100 _000; i++) {
+ 6 counter++;
+ 7 }
+ 8 };
+ 9 Thread t1 = new Thread(task);
+ 10 Thread t2 = new Thread(task);
+ 11 t1.start(); t2.start();
+ 12 t1.join(); t2.join();
+ 13 System.out.println("Counter = " + counter);
+ 14 // Expected: 200000 | Actual: varies, e.g. 137422
+ 15 }

--- PAGE 171 ---
150 Concurrency
Synchronized code blocks provide a solution by ensuring the consistent state of
shared data objects. When a thread enters a synchronized block or method asso-
ciated with an object, it acquires a lock on that object. This lock grants the thread
exclusive access, preventing other threads from executing synchronized code on
the same object. Upon exiting the block, the lock is released, and any modifica-
tions to the shared data are made visible to other threads, thereby ensuring data
consistency.
Essentially, synchronization and concurrency represent opposing design con-
siderations. Synchronization prioritizes data integrity by enforcing controlled, se-
quential access to shared resources, while concurrency aims to maximize perfor-
mance by enabling the parallel execution of threads. The primary challenge is
achieving an optimal balance between these two goals. Excessive synchronization
can degrade performance by creating unnecessary contention and, in severe cases,
deadlocks. Conversely, insufficient synchronization leads to data corruption due
to uncoordinated access to shared resources.
This trade-off can be illustrated with an analogy. Consider a school hallway
during a change of classes. Without any rules of passage, students might collide
or create bottlenecks, delaying everyone. In a multithreaded program, synchro-
nization mechanisms function like these rules. They direct the flow of threads and
manage access to shared resources to prevent conflicts. Just as rules might dictate
that students keep to one side of the hallway, synchronization primitives, such as
locks, allow only one thread to modify a shared resource at a time. This manage-
ment of access prevents data corruption and helps maintain the integrity of shared
data structures.
In the context of GUI programming, multithreading is an essential technique
for maintaining application responsiveness. By utilizing multiple threads, a GUI
application can execute long-running operations in the background without inter-
rupting the user’ s ability to interact with the interface. Without this concurrent ap-
proach, the application would become unresponsive during time-consuming tasks,
leading to a poor user experience. Consequently, GUI frameworks typically em-
ploy a dedicated thread for all UI updates, often called the Event Dispatch Thread
(EDT). All long-running operations, such as network I/O or intensive computa-
tions, should be delegated to separate worker threads to prevent blocking the EDT
and ensure a fluid, interactive application.

--- PAGE 172 ---
3.2. The JavaFX Concurrent Framework 151
3.2 The JavaFX Concurrent Framework
TheDrawGraphTask class examined in Chapter 2 provided an initial examination
of background task execution. By implementing theCallable interface and utiliz-
ing aFutureTask, the application maintained GUI responsiveness while render-
ing currency graphs. The JavaFX concurrent framework formalizes this pattern
through the provision of higher-level abstractions, specificallyTask, Service, and
ScheduledService. These abstractions integrate directly with observable proper-
ties and the JavaFX Application Thread.
The framework is primarily composed of four core classes:Task, Service,
ScheduledService, andWorkerStateEvent. The Task, Service, andScheduled-
Service classes all implement theWorker interface, enabling their instances to be
executed on background threads. TheWorker.State enum represents the distinct
states of aWorker object during its lifecycle, all of which can be safely observed
from the main application thread.
The primary distinction betweenTask, Service, and ScheduledService lies
in their reusability and scheduling capabilities. An instance of theTask class is
designed for a single execution and is not reusable. In contrast, instances ofSer-
vice and ScheduledService represent reusable background tasks. TheSched-
uledService class, which extends theService class, is specifically designed for
tasks that must be executed repeatedly at specified intervals. Finally, an instance
of theWorkerStateEvent class represents an event that is triggered in response to
a state change in aWorker object. Event handlers can be registered to respond to
these state-change events.
3.2.1 Components
The Worker interface
TheWorker<V> interface defines the contract for an object that performs work on
a background thread. The generic parameterV specifies the data type of the task’ s
return value. A key feature of theWorker interface is that its state is observable,
and any state changes are published on the main JavaFX Application Thread. This

--- PAGE 173 ---
152 Concurrency
design facilitates communication with the JavaFX Scene Graph and allows for the
safe modification of UI components from background threads.
A Worker object transitions through a set of distinct states during its lifecycle,
from creation to completion. These states are represented by theWorker.State
enum, which includes the following predefined constants:READY, SCHEDULED, RUN-
NING, SUCCEEDED, FAILED, andCANCELLED.
Upon its creation, aWorker is in theREADY state. It transitions to theSCHEDULED
state just prior to execution and enters theRUNNING state once execution begins. If
the task completes without errors, its final state becomesSUCCEEDED. Conversely, if
an unhandled exception occurs during execution, the state transitions toFAILED.
TheCANCELLED state indicates that theWorker’ s execution was intentionally termi-
nated before completion.
The Task class
TheTask<V> class is a concrete, one-time-use implementation of theWorker<V>
interface. It is designed to encapsulate the logic for a background operation. T o
implement aTask, developers must override the abstractcall() method, which
contains the code to be executed on the background thread.
Like anyWorker, the state of aTask instance can be monitored throughout its
lifecycle. In addition to the standardWorker states, theTask class provides several
observable properties that offer more detailed feedback, such asprogress, mes-
sage, and value. These properties are particularly useful for updating the UI to
reflect the ongoing status of a background operation, such as displaying a progress
bar.
The Service and ScheduledService class
TheService<V> class is designed for reusable background operations. Unlike a
Task, which can only be executed once, aService can be restarted multiple times.
Internally, aService creates and managesTask instances to perform its work. The
ScheduledService<V> class extendsService<V> to provide functionality for tasks
that must be executed repeatedly according to a defined schedule. It can be con-

--- PAGE 174 ---
3.2. The JavaFX Concurrent Framework 153
figured to restart automatically after a successful completion or even after a failure,
based on a specified delay.
3.2.2 Example: JavaFX with concurrent threads
Figure 3.2: Graphical Representation of the FxConcurrentExample Application
Script3.3 illustrates a multithreaded JavaFX application. Line 7 of the script
presents thecall() method, which contains the logic to be executed on a back-
ground thread (Lines 8-14). The program’ s functionality is to increment a counter
from 1 to 50, with a one-second delay between increments, which is controlled
by the Thread.sleep(1000) command in Line 12. This command requires ex-
ception handling, as discussed in the previous chapter. For brevity, this example
uses athrows Exception declaration; however, atry-catch-finally block is the
recommended practice for production code. Line 10 updates the progress bar to
inform the user of the task’ s current execution status. For example, the progress
bar will indicate 50% completion after 25 seconds. Line 11 updates a label on the
GUI with the current value of the counter.
Outside the background task logic, the application’ s control elements are de-
clared in Lines 17-22. These consist of a progress bar, two labels, and two buttons.
The code from Line 23 onward defines the GUI layout. Figure3.1 shows the dis-
played GUI of this example application.

+ 35 }

3.3 Example project: Download manager
The preceding analysis has illustrated two distinct applications of multithreading:
enhancing GUI responsiveness and accelerating computational tasks. The follow-
ing example integrates both principles within a single application. The project ma-
terials are available athttps://github.com/mhrimaz/JavaFXWorkshop_06, and
the application’ s GUI is depicted in Figure3.3a. This project demonstrates the pro-
cess of enhancing an existing application with new functionality.

--- PAGE 177 ---
156 Concurrency
(a) GUI of the Initial Single-Threaded Down-
load Manager
(b) GUI of the Enhanced Multithreaded Down-
load Manager
Figure 3.3: Comparison of Download Manager GUIs
The original version of the application is a basic download manager. Its core de-
sign involves dispatching a download task to a background thread when theDown-
load button is clicked. The main application thread then monitors the download’ s
progress and provides real-time updates to the GUI. The initial application is com-
posed of three Java files:Downloader.java, FXMLDocumentController.java, and
JavaFXWorkshop_06.java. The Downloader class is responsible for establishing
the Uniform Resource Locator (URL) connection, reading the file’ s content into
a buffer using aByteArrayOutputStream, and writing the buffered data to local
storage. TheFXMLDocumentController class instantiates the various UI control
elements and registers their corresponding event handlers. Finally, theJavaFX-
Workshop_06 class serves as the main application launcher.
This example demonstrates a practical utilization of threads in GUI program-
ming, enhancing application performance by introducing multithreaded file down-
load functionality. Because the target file is partitioned into distinct segments, or
chunks, the download qualifies as an embarrassingly parallel task wherein each as-
signed thread executes independently. Specifically, this modification employs two
concurrent downloader tasks to retrieve these segments simultaneously, culminat-
ing in the final state illustrated in Figure3.3b. T o minimize synchronization over-
head during this concurrent execution, the application adheres to the following
sequence of operations:
1. The total size of the target file is obtained.

--- PAGE 178 ---
3.3. Example project: Download manager 157
2. The file size is divided by the number of download threads to determine the
size of each data segment.
3. A fixed-size thread pool is created, and all download tasks are submitted for
execution.
4. The main thread waits for the completion of all download tasks.
5. The downloaded data segments are merged into a single file.
The required modification involves refactoring theDownloader class to sup-
port multipart downloading and introducing a newMerger class responsible for
combining the downloaded file segments.
From the original project, theDownloader.java andFXMLDocument.fxml files
are retained, with the latter being renamed tomain-view.fxml. The JavaFXWork-
shop_06.java and FXMLDocumentController.java files are no longer required
and can be removed. Their functionality is replaced by two new files:MainCon-
troller.java andLauncher.java.

The modifiedDownloader class is presented in Script3.6. The variable dec-
larations up to Line 13 introduce three new instance variables:startByte, end-
Byte, anddoneSignal. ThestartByte andendByte variables define the byte range
that a specific downloader instance is responsible for retrieving. For example, in
a two-downloader implementation, thestartByte for the first downloader would
be zero. ThestartByte for the second downloader would be set to the midpoint
of the file, ensuring that each downloader operates on a distinct segment.
ThedoneSignal variable, of typeCountDownLatch, provides a simple yet effec-
tive synchronization mechanism. This latch is used to signal theMerger class upon
the completion of all download tasks. A controller class initializes the latch with
a count equal to the number of downloader tasks (two, in this example). Upon
completing its individual download task, each downloader thread decrements the
latch’ s count by one. When the latch’ s count reaches zero, it releases any waiting
threads, signaling to theMerger instance that all file segments have been down-
loaded and are ready for merging.
The implementation of theMerger class is presented in Script3.7. The primary
function of theMerger class is to wait for a start signal, which is implemented using
theawait() method of theCountDownLatch in Line 18. Upon receiving the signal,
it iterates through the downloaded file segments. The segments are processed in
sequential order, which is determined by the numeric suffix of each part’ s filename
(Line 22). After each segment is read and appended to the final output file, the
temporary segment file is deleted. This process is repeated until all segments have
been merged.

+ 16 File f = null;
+ 17 try {
+ 18 startSignal.await();
+ 19 out = new FileOutputStream(this.fileName);

+ 21 int toWrite;

+ 26 }

cute the two downloader tasks, which are instantiated in Lines 26-27 and submitted
for concurrent execution in Lines 34-36. After all download tasks have completed,
the thread pool is terminated (Line 37). This sequence concludes the entire multi-
threaded download and merge operation.

3.4 Case Study
AI Advisory
Concurrency defects are among the most difficult software issues to re-
produce and diagnose, and AI tools are consistently unreliable sources of
threading advice. Thread-safety errors, such as updating a JavaFX node
from a background thread or accessing a shared data structure without syn-
chronization, do not always produce immediate, reproducible crashes. In-
stead, these errors frequently produce intermittent failures that may not sur-
face until hours or days later. The prevention of such defects requires a deep
comprehension of the threading model and the ability to reason about exe-
cution flow prior to writing any concurrent code.
Recommended Approach
When working through the concepts in this chapter, the generation of a
Task implementation via an AI tool must be avoided. Instead, the devel-
opment process should begin by identifying which data is shared between
threads and which specific operations must be confined to the JavaFX Ap-
plication Thread. TheTask subclass must be constructed manually, en-
suring thatPlatform.runLater() is utilized for every scene-graph update
originating from a worker thread. Furthermore, potential race conditions
must be tested by executing the application repeatedly under varying com-
putational loads. Only after the implementation is functioning correctly,
should an AI tool be consulted for code review or optimization suggestions.
Appropriate Uses
✓ Utilize an AI tool to clarify the distinctions betweenTask, Service, and
ScheduledService after consulting the framework documentation pro-
vided in this chapter.

--- PAGE 186 ---
3.4. Case Study 165
✓ Employ an AI tool to explain the specific function ofPlatform.run-
Later() and the circumstances under which it is required, after observ-
ing its usage within the codebase.
✓ Utilize an AI tool to verify that thread-unsafe operations have been an-
notated or guarded correctly, but only after the implementation is com-
plete and functioning.
Practices to Avoid
× Do not request an AI tool to automatically refactor code to execute on a
background thread. Such refactoring involves non-trivial architectural
decisions regarding shared data and synchronization requirements.
× Do not accept an AI suggestion to add asynchronized keyword with-
out first independently identifying the critical section and the specific
shared state requiring protection.
× Do not employ an AI tool to debug aConcurrentModificationExcep-
tion without first manually tracing which thread is modifying the col-
lection and identifying the originating call site.
× Do not delegate the implementation of anExecutorCompletionSer-
vice to an AI tool, as the order in which concurrent results arrive holds
significant semantic importance within this chapter’ s case study.
This chapter details the implementation of an inverted index creation applica-
tion. When a set of Portable Document Format (PDF) files is placed into the ap-
plication’ s designated drop zone, the application extracts all words, calculates their
frequencies, and generates an inverted index. The terms within the index are then
sorted in descending order based on their frequency. The resulting inverted in-
dex facilitates the rapid location of documents that contain specific search terms.
The inverted index is a fundamental component of modern search engines, en-
abling the efficient retrieval of relevant documents or webpages in response to user

--- PAGE 187 ---
166 Concurrency
queries. Additionally, the sorted frequency count can serve as an indicator of a
word’ s relative importance within a document corpus.
This case study follows a systematic, step-by-step implementation process, be-
ginning with the GUI design. Subsequently, the implementation of the file-drop
functionality and the core indexing logic are detailed. Finally, the application is
enhanced through the integration of concurrency to improve performance. Figure
3.4 provides a visual representation of the final application GUI.

3.4. Case Study 173
3.4.3 Pdf file extraction
The generation of an inverted index first requires the extraction of all words from
the provided PDF files. The data structures for storing the document informa-
tion are defined in theFileFreq andPdfDocument classes (Scripts3.15 and3.16).
Accessor and mutator methods should be implemented for both classes, follow-
ing the approaches demonstrated in previous chapters. With the model and view
structures established, the subsequent step is to implement the application’ s core
logic. This logic primarily involves the word-processing procedure, which encom-
passes two main steps: (1) extracting all words from each PDF file and counting
their intra-document frequencies, and (2) merging these frequency counts across
all documents to create the final index. TheWordCountMapTask class is responsible
for the initial extraction and frequency counting, while theWordCountReduceTask
class is responsible for merging these counts. These two classes are presented in
Scripts3.17 and3.18, respectively.
Script 3.15: model/FileFreq.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class FileFreq {
+ 3 private String name;
+ 4 private String path;
+ 5 private Integer freq;
+ 6 public FileFreq(String name, String path, Integer freq) {
+ 7 this.name = name;
+ 8 this.path = path;
+ 9 this.freq = freq;
+ 10 }
+ 11 @Override
+ 12 public String toString() {
+ 13 return String.format("{%s:%d}",name,freq);
+ 14 }
+ 15 }

+ 3 private String name;
+ 4 private String filePath;
+ 5 private PDDocument document;
+ 6 private LinkedHashMap<String, List<FileFreq>> uniqueSets;
+ 7 public PdfDocument(String filePath) throws IOException {
+ 8 this.name = Paths.get(filePath).getFileName().toString();
+ 9 this.filePath = filePath;
+ 10 File pdfFile = new File(filePath);
+ 11 this.document = Loader.loadPDF(new RandomAccessReadBufferedFile(pdfFile));

Script 3.17: controller/WordCountMapTask.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class WordCountMapTask{
+ 3 private Map<String, FileFreq> wordCount;
+ 4 public WordCountMapTask(PdfDocument doc) throws IOException {
+ 5 PDFTextStripper reader = new PDFTextStripper();
+ 6 Pattern pattern = Pattern.compile(" ");
+ 7 String s = reader.getText(doc.getDocument());
+ 8 this.wordCount = pattern.splitAsStream(s)
+ 9 .map(word -> word.replaceAll("[^a-zA-Z]", "").toLowerCase().trim())
+ 10 .filter(word -> word.length() > 3)
+ 11 .map(word -> new AbstractMap.SimpleEntry<>(word, 1))
+ 12 .collect(toMap(e -> e.getKey(), e -> e.getValue(), ( v1, v2) -> v1 + v2))
+ 13 .entrySet()
+ 14 .stream()
+ 15 .filter(e -> e.getValue() > 1)
+ 16 .collect(Collectors.toMap(e -> e.getKey(), e-> new FileFreq(doc.getName
(),doc.getFilePath(),e.getValue())));
+ 17 }
+ 18 }

--- PAGE 196 ---
3.4. Case Study 175
Script 3.18: controller/WordCountReduceTask.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class WordCountReduceTask {
+ 3 private LinkedHashMap<String, List<FileFreq>> uniqueSets;
+ 4 public WordCountReduceTask(Map<String, FileFreq>[] wordMap) {
+ 5 List<Map<String, FileFreq>> wordMapList = new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(
wordMap));
+ 6 this.uniqueSets = wordMapList.stream()
+ 7 .flatMap(m -> m.entrySet().stream())
+ 8 .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
+ 9 e->e.getKey(),
+ 10 Collector.of(
+ 11 () -> new ArrayList<FileFreq>(),
+ 12 (list, item) -> list.add(item.getValue()),
+ 13 (current_list, new_items) -> {
+ 14 current_list.addAll(new_items);
+ 15 return current_list; })
+ 16 ))
+ 17 .entrySet()
+ 18 .stream()
+ 19 .sorted(Map.Entry.comparingByKey())
+ 20 .collect(Collectors.toMap(e->e.getKey(),e->e.getValue(),
+ 21 (v1,v2)->v1, () -> new LinkedHashMap<>()));
+ 22 }
+ 23 }
These two classes utilize the Stream API and lambda expressions (Oracle, 2026e),
which significantly reduces the amount of required implementation code. The
stream pipeline within theWordCountMapTask class, beginning on Line 8, performs
the following sequence of operations:
1. The input text is split into a stream of words using whitespace as the delimiter.
2. Each word is normalized by removing all non-alphabetic characters and con-
verting it to lowercase.
3. Each normalized word is then mapped to an integer value of 1, representing
a single occurrence.

--- PAGE 197 ---
176 Concurrency
4. A reduce operation is performed to collect and sum the occurrences of each
unique word within the document.
5. A new stream is created from the resulting map of words and their frequen-
cies.
6. The stream is filtered to retain only words with a frequency count greater
than one.
7. A final map is generated, associating each word with a tuple containing the
filename, file path, and frequency count.
In theWordCountReduceTask class, the code in Lines 8-16 creates a stream from
the word-frequency maps of all processed documents. A reduce operation is then
applied to aggregate the frequency counts for each word across all documents. The
code in Lines 17-21 then sorts the aggregated results to produce the application’ s
final output. At this stage of the implementation, the output is sorted alphabetically
by word; a subsequent modification will change the sorting order to be based on
frequency.
With the individual model, view, and controller classes prepared, the final step
is their integration. Script3.19 presents the modifiedMainViewController, which
replaces the previous file-name printing logic with the new word-processing tasks.
In Lines 37-38, an array ofWordCountMapTask instances and aMap data structure
are created. These are utilized within thefor-loop (Lines 39-53), where each PDF
file is processed individually to extract its word-frequency data. The output of this
mapping phase is stored in thewordMap variable. This collection of maps is then
passed to aWordCountReduceTask instance for the final reduction and merging
step (Line 42). The final, merged result is then passed to the GUI for display.
