3.4.5 Multithreaded indexing implementation
Indexing tasks are typically performed on large document corpora, an operation
that often demands substantial computational power. This example leverages mul-
tithreading to create concurrent indexer tasks. T o enable a class to be executed
concurrently in JavaFX, a common approach is to have it implement theCallable
interface. This requires the implementation of acall() method, which serves as
the entry point for the background task. An instance of such a class can then be
submitted for execution on a background thread. Scripts3.25 and3.26 modify the
WordCountMapTask andWordCountReduceTask classes accordingly. The submis-
sion of these tasks is initiated by the event handler for thestartButton within the
MainViewController class.
The final version of theMainViewController class is presented in Script3.27.
In this implementation, two service instances are declared: anExecutorService
and anExecutorCompletionService (Lines 59-60). The ExecutorCompletion-
Service is introduced here for the first time. This class decorates anExecutorSer-
vice to assist in managing asynchronous tasks, providing a way to retrieve results
as they become available. This service allows for the retrieval of results from com-
pleted tasks in the order they finish, facilitating further processing.
As shown in Line 71, the updatedWordCountMapTask instance is submitted to
theExecutorCompletionService for each PDF file that requires processing. The
results from these completed map tasks are then retrieved within afor-loop (Lines
76-83). Within this loop, thetake() method of the completion service is used,
which returns aFuture object representing the result of the next completed task.
A Future represents the result of an asynchronous computation; it provides meth-
ods to check if the computation is complete and to retrieve its result once it is.
Similarly, theWordCountReduceTask is submitted to anExecutorService (Line
86), and aFuture is used to retrieve the final result of this reduction task. Over-
all, the modifications to these classes exemplify a common approach to managing
concurrent data processing tasks in a JavaFX application.

3.4.6 Incorporating a Loading Indicator
Providing feedback to the user during computationally intensive operations, such
as the inverted index creation in this application, is an essential aspect of responsive
design. In this example, this feedback is provided by a loading indicator, specifically
a spinner, which is displayed within theStart Indexing button. It is important to
note that, by default, JavaFX event handlers execute on the main JavaFX application
thread. Consequently, if a long-running task is executed directly within an event
handler, the application thread will be blocked, preventing any GUI updates until
the task completes. This makes the application appear unresponsive. Therefore, a
specific technique is required to perform the long-running task on a background
thread while updating the loading indicator on the main application thread.
A standard approach for this is to encapsulate the time-consuming logic within
aTask object, which can be executed on a background thread. Script3.28 presents
the modifiedMainViewController class that incorporates this loading indicator.
The code in Lines 51-58 defines thecall() method for theTask, into which the
existing event handler logic has been relocated. AProgressIndicator instance is
introduced to serve as the visual loading indicator and is associated with the button.
The code in Lines 98-100 declares an event handler that binds the visibility of the
loading indicator to the running state of the background task. Finally, the code
in Lines 101-103 creates and starts a new background thread to execute theTask
when the button is clicked.
At this final stage of implementation, the application allows a user to drop one
or more PDF files onto its interface. The application then lists the paths of these files
for confirmation. The user can then initiate the indexing process by clicking the
Start Indexing button. Upon this action, the computationally intensive indexing
tasks are executed on background threads. While these tasks are running, a load-
ing indicator is displayed to inform the user that processing is underway. Upon
completion, the final inverted index is displayed in the right-hand pane. Users can
interact with this result list; clicking an entry opens the corresponding PDF file
associated with the selected term.

3.5 Exercise
1. Modify the sorting logic for the resultListView. The entries, which are
currently sorted alphabetically, should instead be ordered by their total fre-
quency count in descending order. For words that appear in multiple doc-
uments, the total frequency is the sum of their frequencies across all docu-
ments.
2. Enhance the display format for words that appear in multiple PDF files. The
frequency counts from each document should be displayed individually and
in descending order. For example, if the wordabout appears in three separate
documents with frequencies of 1, 4, and 2, the corresponding entry in the
ListView should be formatted as:about (4, 2, 1) .
3. Refactor the inputListView (the file drop zone) to display only the filename
for each dropped file, rather than the full file path, which can be difficult to
read.
4. Incorporate aMenuBar at the top of the application window. This menu bar
must contain aFile menu with, at a minimum, aClose option that terminates
the application.
5. Implement functionality to close the document-view window that appears
after clicking a term in the result list. This should be accomplished by regis-
tering an event handler that listens for theEscape (ESC) key press.
3.6 Discussion and Summary
This chapter has introduced the principles and practical mechanisms of concurrent
programming within the JavaFX framework. The discussion began by address-
ing the necessity of delegating long-running computations to background threads
to preserve GUI responsiveness. The JavaFX concurrent framework, specifically
Task, Service, and ScheduledService, was examined as the standard abstrac-
tion for managing these operations. This was followed by an exploration ofEx-
ecutorService andExecutorCompletionService for coordinating multiple asyn-

--- PAGE 212 ---
3.6. Discussion and Summary 191
chronous tasks. Furthermore, the chapter analyzed the fundamental tension be-
tween parallelism and data integrity, illustrating how unsynchronized access to
shared mutable state produces data races. Finally, the case study synthesized these
concepts through an inverted-index builder that processes PDF files in parallel uti-
lizing a map-reduce pattern.
Knowledge Outcomes
Upon the completion of this chapter, a student should demonstrate the ability to:
• Explain how the single-threaded rendering model of modern GUI frame-
works necessitates the delegation of long-running computations to back-
ground threads to prevent interface freezing.
• Differentiate between the three levels of abstraction in the JavaFX concurrent
framework: one-shotTask operations, reusableService components, and
periodicScheduledService executions.
• Bind observable properties ofWorker objects (such asprogress andmes-
sage) directly to user interface controls to maintain responsiveness without
relying on manual callbacks.
• Manage thread pools utilizing anExecutorService and process asynchronous
results in their order of completion via anExecutorCompletionService.
• Identify data race conditions caused by unsynchronized thread access to
shared mutable variables, and explain the resulting non-deterministic be-
haviors.
• Apply synchronization primitives, includingsynchronized blocks and atomic
variables, to enforce mutual exclusion while evaluating the architectural trade-
offs between coarse-grained and fine-grained locking.
Points for Discussion
1. The case study processes each dropped PDF file utilizing a separate thread
submitted to an executor. Under what specific circumstances might adding

--- PAGE 213 ---
192 Concurrency
more threadsdecrease overall throughput, and how does the ratio of CPU-
bound work to I/O-bound work influence the optimal thread pool size?
2. Synchronization successfully eliminates data races but introduces the pos-
sibility of a deadlock condition, wherein two threads each hold a lock that
the other requires. What specific strategies, such as strict lock ordering or
timeout-based acquisition, can prevent deadlocks without sacrificing the safety
guarantees provided by synchronization?
3. The inverted-index builder in the case study employs a map-reduce pattern,
where each thread independently indexes one document (the map phase),
and the results are subsequently merged into a single data structure (the re-
duce phase). How does this pattern generalize to computational problems
outside of text processing, and what specific properties must a problem pos-
sess to benefit from this decomposition strategy?
Suggested Extensions and Project Ideas
1. Replace the rawsynchronized counter in the data-race example with a
java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger, and compare the result-
ing code clarity and performance characteristics.
2. Incorporate a cancellation button into the case-study indexer that invokes
ExecutorService.shutdownNow(), and observe how each runningTask re-
sponds to an interrupt signal during the PDF parsing phase.
3. Convert the one-shot indexing operation into aScheduledService that re-
scans a designated folder at a fixed interval, automatically detecting newly
added PDF files and updating the result list.
4. Introduce a sharedConcurrentHashMap as the merge target during the re-
duce phase, and measure whether concurrent merging improves overall wall-
clock execution time compared to the current sequential merge implemen-
tation.

F
aults and errors are an inherent part of the software development and
production lifecycle. A method may produce an incorrect value, or an
application may terminate unexpectedly due to exceptions. The poten-
tial causes of errors are numerous, including data input errors, incorrect
parameter naming, improper variable type declarations, misuse of operators or
operands, flawed computational implementation, and inappropriate algorithm se-
lection. In essence, a fault is defined as a condition causing a software application
to fail to perform its intended function, while an error refers to the difference be-
tween the actual output and the expected output. The occurrence of a fault or error
indicates the presence of a defect within the source code that requires identification
and correction. The process of identifying, localizing, and correcting these defects
is known as debugging.

--- PAGE 218 ---
4.1. Debugging Methodologies 197
4.1 Debugging Methodologies
Faults and errors are common occurrences during software development. While
some issues, such as syntax errors, are readily identified by the compiler, defects
related to program logic or algorithmic flaws are often more challenging to diag-
nose. In most cases, the diagnosis of such defects requires an examination of the
application’ s behavior with specific input data to identify patterns of incorrectness.
Upon identifying a potential defect, the relevant software components must be iso-
lated and examined individually. Debugging in practice involves a diverse range of
tools and methodologies. This chapter will focus on three categories that are widely
employed in the industry.
4.1.1 Probing
A fundamental debugging method is known asprobing. This technique involves
observing the runtime state of a variable or the execution flow of a method by in-
serting print statements into the source code. For example, to ascertain the exact
value of a variablex, the code snippetSystem.out.println(x) is inserted into
the program adjacent to the line of code under scrutiny. Additional print state-
ments can subsequently be added to observe how the state ofx changes during
execution. This approach is frequently utilized due to its simplicity, particularly
when the developer possesses a strong hypothesis regarding the location of the de-
fect. However, this practice exhibits significant limitations when compared to more
comprehensive debugging techniques.
The primary drawbacks of this technique include its inherent lack of flexibil-
ity and the inefficient process of relocating print statements to various investiga-
tion points. Furthermore, observing multiple variables simultaneously can become
highly cumbersome. Crucially, these diagnostic code fragments must be manually
removed after the defect is resolved, as their continued presence can introduce un-
intended side effects or performance degradation. In summary, probing serves as
a straightforward initial approach to troubleshooting but rapidly becomes unman-
ageable as code complexity increases.
These limitations are significantly amplified in graphical user interface (GUI)
applications. Because a JavaFX application executes event handlers and background

--- PAGE 219 ---
198 Debugging
tasks on separate threads, concurrentSystem.out.println output from multiple
threads can arrive in an unpredictable order, making it exceedingly difficult to re-
construct the actual sequence of events. The console output quickly becomes dis-
organized when print statements are scattered across controllers, model classes,
and worker threads, particularly because they lack any indication of severity or
origin. Furthermore, probing offers no mechanism to selectively enable or disable
output for a specific component; every statement is either executed uncondition-
ally or must be manually commented out. Perhaps most critically, developers fre-
quently neglect to remove print statements prior to packaging an application for
release, resulting in diagnostic messages persisting in production environments,
where they become visible to end users or silently consume input/output (I/O) re-
sources. These cumulative shortcomings necessitate the adoption of a structured
logging framework. Such a framework provides level-based filtering, timestamped
output, configurable destinations, and the capacity to remain in the codebase per-
manently without interfering with production output.
Script 4.1: Using System.out.println() to Verify Application Loading
1 public class HelloApplication extends Application {

4 FXMLLoader fxmlLoader = new FXMLLoader(HelloApplication.class.getResource(

approach can be beneficial when a fault occurs without an immediately obvious
point of failure.
4.1.2 Logging
Logging is the recommended alternative to simple probing, offering significant im-
provements in both functionality and practicality. It is widely recognized as a best
practice in modern software development, facilitating future maintenance and sup-
port. The practice of logging involves recording messages during program execu-
tion to a persistent or observable location, such as a file, a database, or the console.
Each log entry typically comprises essential information, including a timestamp,
severity level, and a formatted message containing contextual information.
This logging mechanism enables the reporting and persistence of informational,
error, and warning messages, which can be later retrieved and analyzed. These cap-
tured log data are increasingly regarded as valuable assets in software development.
In essence, logging allows for a comprehensive understanding of an application’ s
behavior in production through the examination of log entries. More broadly, log-
ging assets are utilized in mining software repositories, a process that analyzes data
generated during software development to uncover meaningful and actionable in-
formation about software systems and projects. These assets play an important

--- PAGE 221 ---
200 Debugging
role in maintaining high levels of collaboration and ensuring quality in numerous
open-source software projects, including Mozilla Firefox, Eclipse, and LibreOffice.
In the Java ecosystem, logging is typically implemented using dedicated appli-
cation programming interfaces (APIs), such as Log4j (2026) or SLF4J (2026). The
essential components of a logging framework include:
• Logging frameworks;
• Level;
• Logger;
• Appender;
• Layout;
• Configuration;
Logging frameworks
The implementation of logging in a Java application requires the use of a logging
framework. Java provides a built-in framework in thejava.util.logging pack-
age, which contains the essential components for creating and persisting log mes-
sages. These components encompass objects, methods, and configuration options.
However, this built-in framework is generally considered less capable and is less
frequently used in production systems compared to third-party alternatives such
asLog4j2 andSLF4J.
In modern Java development, the combination ofSLF4J as a facade with a
Log4j2 binding has emerged as a predominant logging architecture. This architec-
ture provides extensive flexibility, allowing the underlying logging implementation
to be changed without modifying the application code.
Scripts4.3 and4.4 demonstrate the integration of these logging techniques into
theHello application. The use ofSystem.out.println() commands is replaced
with logging calls. Line 3 of each script demonstrates the logger declaration. Subse-
quent calls to methods such aslogger.info() andlogger.debug() illustrate the
replacement ofSystem.out.println() statements with structured logging calls. A
more comprehensive discussion of the configuration details for theLog4j2 frame-
work is presented in the case study section of this chapter.

Logging levels
Logging levels are standardized categories assigned to log entries to indicate their
severity or importance. In a production environment, messages with high severity
often signify unexpected application behavior that could lead to negative conse-
quences, such as data corruption or service unavailability. Clearly defined levels
are crucial for enabling developers and operators to quickly assess the urgency
of a given log entry. For instance, a log entry designated as a severe error re-
quires immediate attention, whereas an entry containing routine informational
data may only be relevant for certain types of analysis. A set of standard levels
is widely adopted across numerous programming languages. The following list
presents these common log levels, ordered from most to least severe:
• FATAL: Represents a critical error condition that will likely lead to the ter-
mination of the application. Due to their high severity, FATAL-level events
require immediate intervention.
• ERROR: Represents a significant error that, while not necessarily causing
application termination, prevents a primary business function from being
fulfilled. The application may continue to operate but in an erroneous or
degraded state. This level should be used judiciously to avoid diluting the
significance of critical error reports.
• W ARN: Indicates a potentially harmful situation or an unexpected event
from which the application can recover. This level is appropriate for non-
critical errors where a fix can be postponed or where the system is attempting
a recovery action, such as reconnecting to a service.
• INFO: Represents messages that highlight the routine progress and state
transitions of the application. This information is valuable for tracking nor-
mal application behavior in both development and production environments,
such as user login events or major service initializations.
• DEBUG: Provides fine-grained diagnostic information useful for debugging
application logic. DEBUG-level logs are primarily intended for use by devel-
opers during the development and troubleshooting phases.

--- PAGE 224 ---
4.1. Debugging Methodologies 203
• TRACE: Provides the most granular level of diagnostic information, offering
more detail than DEBUG. In many practices, TRACE-level logs are used for
highly detailed, step-by-step execution analysis.
Two additional pseudo-levels,OFF andALL, are also available.OFF is used to
disable all logging, whileALL enables the recording of all messages regardless of
their level.
The operational principle of logging levels is based on a configurable thresh-
old. During runtime, the application code generates logging requests, each with an
associated severity level. The logging framework is configured with a specific log
level that acts as this threshold. A logging request is processed only if its severity
level is equal to or greater than the configured threshold level; otherwise, the re-
quest is discarded. The standard hierarchical order of severity is:TRACE < DEBUG <
INFO < WARN < ERROR < FATAL. For instance, if the logging framework’ s threshold is
set toERROR, only logging requests with a level ofERROR orFATAL will be recorded.
Loggers
Loggers are named entities responsible for capturing log events. After applying a
level-based filter, they forward the qualifying log events to the appropriate Appen-
ders. They provide developers with fine-grained, runtime control over which log
statements are processed and their destinations.
Appender
Appenders are components responsible for directing formatted log events to their
final output destinations. These destinations can range from the console and local
files to more complex systems like databases or remote servers. The following list
outlines some widely used Appenders:
• ConsoleAppender: Appends log events to the console by passing format-
ted entries toSystem.out orSystem.err, withSystem.out as the default
target.System.err can be used alternatively, often to segregate error mes-
sages. While frequently used during development due to its simplicity, it is
generally less practical in production environments.

--- PAGE 225 ---
204 Debugging
• FileAppender: Appends log events to a file at a predefined location. This
is a common choice for production environments, as file-based logs can be
archived, retrieved, and analyzed post-execution. This is crucial when ad-
dressing software defects, as timestamps can aid in identifying when a prob-
lem occurred.
• RollingFileAppender: A FileAppender variant that “rolls over” log files
when certain conditions are met (e.g., the file reaches a specific size). This
is widely used in production systems to manage log file growth and simplify
archival.
• DailyRollingFileAppender: A time-based variation ofRollingFileAppen-
der that creates a new log file at a scheduled interval, typically daily.
• SMTP Appender: Formats log events as emails, typically used to provide im-
mediate notification forERROR orFATAL level events. This concept of real-
time notification has been adapted for modern communication platforms,
with custom appenders that send alerts to services like instant messaging
applications.
• JDBCAppender: Appends log events by persisting them to a relational da-
tabase. This structured storage facilitates complex querying and analysis of
log data.
Layout
Layouts are components responsible for formatting a log event into a specific string
representation before it is written by an Appender. Logging frameworks typically
offer layouts for various formats, including plain text, HTML, and XML. In prac-
tice, a useful log entry includes not only the message but also contextual metadata
(e.g., timestamp and thread name) that aids in diagnostics.
A common layout used in Java isPatternLayout, which allows for a highly
customizable log format based on a conversion pattern string. An example format
string is%dyyyy/MM/dd HH:mm:ss [%-5p] %c1 - %m%n , which produces log en-
tries such as2025/01/01 08:00:00 [DEBUG] HelloWorld - Message Logged.

--- PAGE 226 ---
4.1. Debugging Methodologies 205
Understanding the conversion specifiers is essential for interpreting aPatternLay-
out. The following list outlines some key specifiers:
• %d...: Formats the timestamp of the log event. The pattern inside the braces
(e.g.,yyyy/MM/dd HH:mm:ss ) follows standard date formatting rules.
• %p: Displays the severity level of the log event (e.g., DEBUG, INFO, ERROR).
Modifiers like-5 can be used for padding.
• %t: Displays the name of the thread that generated the log event.
• %c...: Displays the name of the logger that generated the event. The number
in braces specifies the precision (e.g.,1 shows only the simple class name).
• %M: Displays the name of the method where the logging request was issued.
• %L: Displays the line number of the source code where the logging request
was issued.
• %m: Outputs the application-supplied log message.
• %n: Outputs the platform-specific line separator.
Configuration
The variety of available logging frameworks has led to the development of diverse
configuration formats. This section focuses on the configuration format for the
Log4j2 framework, a widely adopted solution. ConfiguringLog4j2 typically in-
volves creating alog4j2.properties file within the project’ s resource folder. Script
4.5 provides an illustrative example of such a file. In this example, the root logger’ s
level is set toDEBUG, and a singleConsoleAppender is configured as the output des-
tination.
During program execution, when a logging request is made, the logging frame-
work uses this configuration to determine the appropriate action. If the request’ s
level meets the configured threshold, the framework formats the log message ac-
cording to the specifiedPatternLayout and forwards it to all configured appen-
ders.

--- PAGE 227 ---
206 Debugging
Script 4.5: resources/se233/chapter4/log4j2.properties
+ 1 name = PropertiesConfig
+ 2 appender.console.type = Console
+ 3 appender.console.name = consoleLogger
+ 4 appender.console.layout.type = PatternLayout
+ 5 appender.console.layout.pattern = [%5 p] % d{yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm:ss.SSS} [% t] % c{1} %
msg%n
+ 6
+ 7 rootLogger.level = debug
+ 8 rootLogger.appenderRef.stderr.ref = consoleLogger
4.1.3 Debugging
Debugging is the systematic process of identifying and resolving defects within
a running program. In contrast to the static analysis of stack traces, interactive
debugging requires a dedicated debugger tool, which is essential when the source
of an error is not immediately apparent. Such cases often involve subtle defects
in logic or algorithms that may not manifest until a specific runtime condition is
met, long after the faulty code has executed. Resolving these types of issues can be
particularly challenging.
Figure 4.1: Establishing a Breakpoint in In-
telliJ IDEA
The central principle of interactive
debugging is the ability to inspect the
program’ s state at any point during its
execution. This is accomplished by
setting breakpoints, which pause the
program’ s execution at specific lines of
code, allowing for the analysis of vari-
able values. A debugger thus enables
a detailed examination of how vari-
able values change as the program pro-
gresses from one statement to the next.
This capability facilitates the identification of unexpected state changes that may
indicate the root cause of a defect.
T o illustrate the debugging process, consider a simple scenario. Script4.6 presents
a program intended to calculate the sum of integer values provided as command-

--- PAGE 228 ---
4.1. Debugging Methodologies 207
line arguments. The program compiles and executes without errors; however, it
produces an incorrect result. For an input of1 2 3 4 5 , the expected output is 15,
but the program produces 0. In this situation, the first step is to form a hypothesis
about the probable source of the error. Because this is a logical error rather than a
compilation error, it must be investigated at runtime.
Script 4.6: Example Class for Demonstrating Debugging Summation.java
+ 1 public class Summation {
+ 2 public static void main(String[] args) {
+ 3 double sum = calculateSum(args);
+ 4 System.out.println("The summation is " + sum);
+ 5 }
+ 6 private static double calculateSum(String[] input) {
+ 7 double result = 0;
+ 8 for (String s : input) {
+ 9 result *= Integer.parseInt(s);
+ 10 }
+ 11 return result;
+ 12 }
+ 13 }
Figure 4.2: Configuring Program Argu-
ments for Debugging in IntelliJ IDEA
Using a debugger, a breakpoint can
be set at any line of code. This break-
point instructs the debugger to pause
program execution at that specific line,
allowing for an examination of the cur-
rent values of all declared variables.
Typically, a breakpoint is set just be-
fore the line of code where a defect
is suspected. The following example
demonstrates this process in the Intel-
liJ IDEA IDE. Initially, a breakpoint is set at Line 3 of Script4.6. This is typically
done by clicking in the area next to the line number, which places a red circle icon
at that location, as shown in Figure4.1. The program is then executed in debug
mode.

--- PAGE 229 ---
208 Debugging
T o execute the program in debug mode within IntelliJ IDEA, the run configu-
ration must first be edited by selectingRun > Edit Configurations... from the
main menu. In the configuration window, the program arguments that produced
the incorrect result (1 2 3 4 5 ) are entered. Figure4.2 provides a visual illustra-
tion of this step.
Figure 4.3: Debugger Tool Window in IntelliJ
IDEA
The debug execution mode is
then initiated via theDebug but-
ton, typically located next to the
Run button. This action starts a
debugging session, and program
execution pauses at the prede-
fined breakpoint. The Debug tool
window, illustrating the current
debugging process, is displayed
in Figure 4.3. As shown in the
tool window, because execution is
paused before the invocation of thecalculateSum() method, its local variables
have not yet been initialized.
From this paused state, the debugger can be used to execute the code statement
by statement, observing the state of all variables after each step. This action is typ-
ically performed using theStep Into command. Stepping through each iteration of
thefor-loop reveals that theresult variable consistently remains0.0. This ob-
servation indicates that the defect is related to either the initialization of theresult
variable (Line 7) or the operation within the loop (Line 9). A closer inspection re-
veals that the multiplication assignment operator (*=) in Line 9 is the root cause of
the defect. Replacing this with the addition assignment operator (+=) corrects the
logic, and re-executing the program yields the correct output of15.
TheStep Into command executes the current line and, if it contains a method
call, moves the execution point to the first line inside that method. If the internal
execution of a method is not of interest, theStep Over command can be used in-
stead. This command executes the entire method call at once and pauses on the
subsequent line in the current method.
Another powerful feature available in many modern debuggers, including In-
telliJ IDEA ’ s, is the ability toEvaluate Expression. This tool allows a developer to

--- PAGE 230 ---
4.2. Extra Topic: Sprite Animation 209
execute arbitrary code expressions within the current execution context, which is
useful for testing hypotheses without altering the source code and recompiling.
This feature is typically accessed via a dedicated button or context menu option
in the debugger tool window. Within this tool, arbitrary code expressions can be
evaluated, and their results are displayed, reflecting the current state of the paused
application.
4.2 Extra Topic: Sprite Animation
A simple game featuring sprite animation has been chosen as the case study for this
chapter’ s discussion of logging and debugging. The numerous state changes inher-
ent in game animation make it a suitable example for illustrating the challenges of
maintaining consistency and correctness during program execution. Prior to ex-
amining the case study, a concise overview of game loops and sprite animation is
provided.
Fundamentally, a game can be conceptualized as a combination of logic loops
and rendering loops. Typically, at least two main loops are implemented: one for
game logic computations and another for rendering visual effects. These loops of-
ten operate at different frequencies. The rendering loop should refresh at a high
rate, typically 60 times per second (60 Hz) or more, to ensure fluid visual ani-
mation. In contrast, the logic loop may refresh less frequently, for example, 5 to
10 times per second, as update frequencies exceeding this rate offer no perceptual
benefit for game logic.
The implementation of animation components introduces considerations be-
yond standard application programming, as the timing of drawing operations must
be synchronized with human perception. Movements that are rendered too slowly
or too quickly can result in an undesirable user experience. For example, con-
sider the object movement implemented in Script4.7. Although the code is free
of errors, the resulting movement is imperceptible because its speed exceeds the
threshold of human visual processing; it does not constitute animation. The solu-
tion is to control the refresh rate of the drawing loop. Script4.8 presents an updated
implementation,redrawShape.java, where aThread.sleep() call is introduced
into the loop. The value1000/60 corresponds to a target refresh rate of 60 frames

--- PAGE 231 ---
210 Debugging
per second, with each frame having a duration of approximately 16.7 milliseconds.
Script 4.7: Drawing Objects at High Speed
+ 1 for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {
+ 2 x++;
+ 3 y++;
+ 4 c.redraw();
+ 5 }
Script 4.8: Implementing Movement with Thread.sleep for Smoother Animation
1 for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {
2 x++;
3 y++;
4 c.redraw();
+ 5 try {
+ 6 Thread.sleep(1000/60);
+ 7 } catch(Exception e) {
+ 8 e.printStackTrace();
+ 9 }
10 }
In programming terms, animation is synonymous with time-based motion,
wherein an image is drawn, erased, and redrawn at a new coordinate within each
iteration of the drawing loop. This process occurs at a refresh rate optimized for
human perception. The refresh rate must strike a balance, as excessively slow rates
result in a laggy experience, while overly fast rates create the illusion of teleporta-
tion rather than smooth movement. Broadly speaking, animation can be viewed
as the time-based alteration of graphical objects through changes in their states,
locations, sizes, and orientations (Haase & Guy, 2007). The essential components
for implementing basic animation include drawing, clocks, movement, collision
handling, and interpolation.

--- PAGE 232 ---
4.2. Extra Topic: Sprite Animation 211
4.2.1 Drawing
In JavaFX, objects intended for animation are typically implemented as custom
components. This can be achieved by extending a class such asjavafx.scene.lay-
out.Pane, which allows the custom component to be treated as a standard JavaFX
node and added to the scene graph. A key consideration in 2D graphics program-
ming is the coordinate system. In contrast to conventional Cartesian coordinate
systems where the origin (0, 0) is at the bottom-left, the JavaFX coordinate system
places the origin at the top-left corner of the drawing surface.
4.2.2 Clock
A clock, in the context of animation and game development, is a mechanism for
synchronizing time-dependent operations. Similar to how a master clock synchro-
nizes various devices in a complex system to prevent signal interference, a central
timing loop in an application ensures that all animations and logic updates occur
in a coordinated and consistent manner. In practice, afor orwhile loop, often
referred to as the main game loop, is implemented to serve this function. The pri-
mary purpose of this clock or loop is to ensure that all visual components update
their state based on a single, authoritative time reference. For the purposes of this
course, the main programming loops will be synchronized with a master clock that
“ticks” at a regular interval, such as every millisecond.
4.2.3 Movement
The implementation of object movement is primarily concerned with achieving a
high degree of realism. A common methodology is to apply principles from basic
physics. In this approach, a movable object is defined by its position, velocity, and
acceleration at any given time. Instead of directly manipulating an object’ s posi-
tion, developers can manipulate its acceleration, allowing changes to propagate to
its velocity and position according to physics-based formulas. The realism can be
further enhanced by incorporating concepts such as force and momentum. For
instance, keyboard events can be interpreted as forces applied to a game character
in a specific direction. The character then moves with a calculated acceleration up
to a predefined maximum velocity. When the key is released, the force is removed,

--- PAGE 233 ---
212 Debugging
and the character decelerates until its velocity reaches zero. In this model, the posi-
tion and velocity are not directly set but are emergent properties of the underlying
physics simulation.
4.2.4 Collision handling
Collision handling, or collision detection, is the process of detecting when two or
more graphical objects intersect. Because these objects are virtual, their reactions
to such intersections must be explicitly defined in the program logic. This compo-
nent of a game or simulation often involves significant mathematical calculations.
Common responses to a detected collision include halting movement, modifying
object attributes (e.g., reducing health points), triggering visual effects, or updating
a score.
Script 4.9: Example Implementation of a Basic Game Loop LogicLoop.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class LogicLoop {

Before discussing interpolation, a summary of the game loop components is
provided. Scripts4.9 and4.10 present the structures of the logic and rendering
loops, respectively. Lines 5 and 6 of each script define their respective refresh rates;
in this example, the logic loop operates at 10 Hz (10 times per second) and the
rendering loop operates at 60 Hz. The logic loop (Script4.9) updates the state of
game objects and performs collision checks once per tick (every 100 milliseconds).
The rendering loop (Script4.10), by contrast, ticks more frequently (every 16.7
milliseconds). Each iteration is responsible for redrawing all visual components
based on the current game state.

4.2.5 Interpolation
The final component of animation discussed here is sprite sheet animation. This
technique creates the illusion of movement by rapidly displaying a sequence of
static images, or frames. These frames are often consolidated into a single image file
known as a sprite sheet, which improves organization and memory management.
Figure4.4 illustrates an example of a sprite sheet.
T o utilize a sprite sheet, a viewport is defined. The viewport acts as a sliding
window that selects a single frame from the sheet to be displayed at any given time.
With each tick of the rendering loop, the viewport’ s position is updated to select the
next frame in the animation sequence. Initially, only the top-left Rockman image
is displayed. After a frame’ s duration (e.g., 16.7 ms), the viewport moves to the
next image in the same row. This process of sequentially displaying sub-images
from a sprite sheet to create animated movement is a fundamental technique for
2D character animation. When the sequence reaches its end, it can loop back to
the first frame. This technique enhances the perceived realism of moving objects.
Script4.11 demonstrates a code fragment that uses a viewport for sprite sheet
animation. Thetick() method determines which frame to display during the cur-
rent rendering cycle, based on thecurIndex variable. Thecolumns androws vari-
ables represent the dimensions of the sprite grid on the sheet. Theinterpolate()
method in this context sets the coordinates of the viewport on the sprite sheet,
wherex andy represent the top-left corner of the target frame. For the first frame
(curIndex = 0), bothx andy are zero. For the next frame, the value ofx is incre-
mented by the width of a single sub-image. A key requirement for this technique
is that all frames within a sprite sheet must have uniform dimensions. Table4.1

--- PAGE 236 ---
4.2. Extra Topic: Sprite Animation 215
Script 4.11: Example Implementation of Sprite Sheet AnimationInterpolation.java
+ 1 public void tick() {
+ 2 curColumnIndex = curIndex % columns;
+ 3 curRowIndex = curIndex / columns;
+ 4 curIndex = ( curIndex+1) % ( columns * rows);
+ 5 interpolate();
+ 6 }
+ 7 protected void interpolate() {
+ 8 final int x = curColumnIndex * width + offsetX;
+ 9 final int y = curRowIndex * height + offsetY;
+ 10 this.setViewport(new Rectangle2D(x, y, width, height));
+ 11 }
illustrates the relationship betweencurIndex, curColumnIndex, andcurRowIndex
for the sprite sheet in Figure4.4, which is organized as a grid of five columns and
two rows.
Table 4.1: Relationship between curIndex, curColumnIndex, and curRowIndex for the
Sprite Sheet in Figure 4.4.
curIndex curColumnIndex curRowIndex
0 0 0
1 1 0
2 2 0
3 3 0
4 4 0
5 0 1
6 1 1
7 2 1
8 3 1
9 4 1

4.3 Case Study
AI Advisory
Debugging is a skill that AI tools can assist but cannot replace. Utilizing
an AI tool as the primary resort when a defect emerges, such as pasting a
stack trace and requesting an immediate solution, bypasses the diagnostic
reasoning that this chapter is designed to cultivate. The ability to formu-
late a hypothesis, isolate the relevant code path, and verify the hypothesis
through observation constitutes a core professional competency that im-
proves exclusively through deliberate practice.
Recommended Approach
When a defect emerges, a structured methodology must be followed: first,
formulate a hypothesis regarding the root cause; second, establish a break-
point in the debugger to test this hypothesis; and third, consult a log file if
the defect is timing-related or difficult to reproduce interactively. Only af-
ter a specific, testable hypothesis has been established should an AI tool be
consulted to confirm or challenge the underlying reasoning. Employing an
AI tool as a hypothesis generator, such as requesting potential causes for an
observed symptom, is highly productive. Conversely, utilizing an AI tool as
an automatic defect resolver bypasses the learning process entirely.
Appropriate Uses
✓ Utilize an AI tool to interpret an unfamiliar line within a stack trace,
such as explaining the representation ofjavafx.application.Appli-
cation$1.run in a thread dump.
✓ Employ an AI tool to suggest Log4j2PatternLayout syntax options
only after the basic pattern format is fully understood.

--- PAGE 238 ---
4.3. Case Study 217
✓ Utilize an AI tool to generate alog4j2.properties skeleton after each
field in the configuration file can be explained independently.
Practices to Avoid
× Do not paste a stack trace into an AI tool and request a resolution prior
to establishing a breakpoint and confirming the failure location inde-
pendently.
× Do not employ an AI tool to disable or silence excessive log output with-
out first determining the underlying cause of the excessive logging.
× Do not delegate game-loop implementation to an AI tool. The temporal
logic of frame interpolation and acceleration must be traced manually
to be fully comprehended.
The case study for this chapter focuses on the implementation of a simple 2D
platformer game in which character movement is controlled by the keyboard. The
project, namedPlatformer, and its associated assets are available athttps://cmu.
to/SE233CH4.
The implementation for this case study is notable for its complexity, particu-
larly due to the large number of state variables required. This complexity presents
an ideal scenario for demonstrating the use of a debugger as a tool to assist in the
development process. The use of a debugger for troubleshooting is highly recom-
mended when encountering any development issues throughout this case study.
The project’ s file structure is organized as follows:

4.3.1 Game loop
Before examining an implementation with separate logic and rendering loops, a
version of the game that utilizes a single, unified game loop will be explored. Script
4.15 illustrates a class namedGameLoop that implements theRunnable interface. Its
primary component is an infinite loop that executes on a background thread and
repeats at a frequency of 60 Hz (approximately every 16.7 milliseconds). Within
each iteration, therun() method sequentially invokes theupdate(), checkColli-
sions(), andpaint() methods.
T o examine the operation of thisGameLoop, theGameCharacter, GameLoop, and
Launcher classes are modified as shown in Scripts4.16, 4.17, and 4.18, respec-
tively. The updatedGameCharacter class in Script4.16 implements the logic for
the character to fall to the virtual ground. Two new state variables are introduced
in Lines 19-20: yVelocity, which determines the rate of descent, andisFalling,
a boolean flag indicating the character’ s falling state. Three new methods are also
added:moveY() to update the character’ s y-coordinate,checkReachFloor() to de-
tect when the character has reached the virtual ground, andrepaint() to redraw
the character on the platform.

4.3.2 Keys
Keyboard input is used to control the movement of the game character. For ex-
ample, thea, w, andd keys could be mapped to leftward movement, jumping, and
rightward movement, respectively. The binding of these keys requires the imple-
mentation of event listeners to handle the corresponding keyboard events. Script
4.19 presents a newKeys class that functions as a centralized input manager. This
class tracks which keys are currently pressed during each game loop tick, enabling
the application to respond to keyboard state. TheKeys class is designed to be in-
dependent of theGameCharacter class to enhance modularity; for example, this
separation would allow different key mappings for multiple players in a local co-
operative mode. AHashMap is employed within theKeys class to efficiently track
the state of multiple, simultaneous key presses, which is a common requirement
for complex character control.
The integration of theKeys class requires modifications to theGameStage,
Launcher, GameLoop, and GameCharacter classes, as shown in Scripts4.20, 4.21,
4.22, and 4.23, respectively. The modifications in Script4.20 integrate theKeys
class with the main view component,GameStage.
In Script4.21, two anonymous classes (Lines 16 and 17) are used to create event
handlers. These handlers capture keyboard press and release events and forward
them to theKeys object managed by theGameStage. This action updates the state of
theHashMap, adding an entry when a key is pressed and removing it upon release.

4.3. Case Study 231
The logic for this boundary check is straightforward. It verifies if the charac-
ter’ s updated x-coordinate is either less than zero or greater than the width of the
game stage. If a boundary is exceeded, the character’ s x-coordinate is adjusted to
the boundary value itself. This correction occurs before theGameLoop invokes the
repaint() method, ensuring the character is rendered in the correct position.
4.3.4 Jumping
When the designated key for upward movement (e.g.,w) is pressed, a force is ap-
plied to the character to initiate a jump. Subsequently, the character should de-
scend under the influence of gravity until it makes contact with the ground. The
downward motion occurs at a non-constant speed due to gravitational accelera-
tion. Consequently, the character’ s vertical velocity increases throughout its de-
scent. However, a complete physics-based implementation of this jumping mech-
anism is overly complex for an initial example.
T o simplify the initial implementation, a model is considered that represents
the jump using only basic force, velocity, and position. Modifications begin with
theGameCharacter class, as shown in Script4.26. Three new variables are intro-
duced:canJump, isJumping, andhighestJump. The first two are boolean flags that
track whether the character is permitted to jump and whether a jump is currently
in progress, respectively. These flags are essential for preventing the user from ini-
tiating a new jump while the character is already airborne. While thecanJump state
is false, subsequent presses of the jump key will have no effect until the charac-
ter has returned to the ground. ThehighestJump variable defines the maximum
vertical displacement the character can achieve relative to the ground.
In this update, conditional logic for upward movement is added to themoveY()
method (Lines 42-44). Additionally, two new methods,jump() andcheckReach-
Highest(), are introduced (Lines 53-66). Thejump() method is invoked when
the jump key is pressed, but only if thecanJump state istrue. The checkReach-
Highest() method determines if the character has reached its maximum vertical
height and, if so, initiates the descent by settingisJumping tofalse andisFalling
totrue. ThecheckReachFloor() method resets the character’ s state upon landing
on the virtual ground. Specifically, it sets thecanJump state back totrue (Line 70),
allowing a new jump to be initiated.

--- PAGE 255 ---
234 Debugging
The current implementation demonstrates the fundamental mechanics of a
platform game. The character’ s movement is controlled via the keyboard, and the
application responds to user input for actions such as horizontal movement and
jumping. This state serves as a foundation for implementing the more advanced
topics discussed previously, including multiple game loops, sprite interpolation,
acceleration, and logging.
The first enhancement is the separation of rendering logic from the main game
loop. This is achieved by creating a newDrawingLoop class. Script4.28 presents this
new class, which encapsulates methods related to collision detection and graphics
rendering that were previously in theGameLoop. The originalGameLoop is subse-
quently modified (Script4.29) by reducing its refresh rate to 10 Hz (100 millisec-
onds per tick) and removing this rendering logic.
As discussed earlier, the drawing loop should only observe variable changes
to render the consequences on the screen, without performing any programming
logic computations. This necessitates adjustments to themoveLeft andmoveRight
methods in theGameCharacter class, detailed in Script4.30. The primary change
involves calculating movement on the x-axis similarly to the y-axis. Instead of di-
rectly modifying the location, external forces determine it, enhancing flexibility for
future upgrades to the movement simulation engine. The calculations for the char-
acter’ s position on both x and y axes are now independent of character rendering.
In the updatedGameCharacter class (Script4.30), the character’ s x-coordinate
is no longer modified directly within the input handling logic. Instead, the state
of the character’ s horizontal movement is controlled by boolean flags, and a new
moveX() method is responsible for applying this movement during the rendering
phase. Consequently, the character’ s movement on both axes is now governed by
four state flags:isMoveLeft, isMoveRight, isFalling, and isJumping. The in-
creased complexity of this state-based logic highlights the utility of a debugger for
verification and troubleshooting.

+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class DrawingLoop implements Runnable {
+ 3 private GameStage gameStage;
+ 4 private int frameRate;
+ 5 private float interval;
+ 6 private boolean running;
+ 7 public DrawingLoop(GameStage gameStage) {
+ 8 this.gameStage = gameStage;
+ 9 frameRate = 60;
+ 10 interval = 1000.0 f / frameRate; // 1000 ms = 1 second
+ 11 running = true;
+ 12 }
+ 13 private void checkDrawCollisions(GameCharacter gameCharacter) {
+ 14 gameCharacter.checkReachGameWall();
+ 15 gameCharacter.checkReachHighest();
+ 16 gameCharacter.checkReachFloor();
+ 17 }
+ 18 private void paint(GameCharacter gameCharacter) {
+ 19 gameCharacter.repaint();
+ 20 }
+ 21 @Override
+ 22 public void run() {
+ 23 while (running) {
+ 24 float time = System.currentTimeMillis();
+ 25 checkDrawCollisions(gameStage.getGameCharacter());
+ 26 paint(gameStage.getGameCharacter());
+ 27 time = System.currentTimeMillis() - time;
+ 28 if (time < interval) {
+ 29 try {
+ 30 Thread.sleep((long) ( interval - time));
+ 31 } catch (InterruptedException e) {
+ 32 }
+ 33 } else {
+ 34 try {
+ 35 Thread.sleep((long) ( interval - ( interval % time)));
+ 36 } catch (InterruptedException e) {
+ 37 }
+ 38 }
+ 39 }
+ 40 }
+ 41 }

4.3.5 Interpolation
The final enhancement to be discussed is sprite animation, which is used to cre-
ate more realistic character movement. An examination of the sprite sheet (e.g.,
MarioSheet.png) reveals a sequence of character poses. The rapid, sequential dis-
play of these individual images creates the illusion of fluid movement, which is the
foundation of 2D sprite animation.
Script 4.32: model/AnimatedSprite.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class AnimatedSprite extends ImageView {
+ 3 int count, columns, rows, offsetX, offsetY, width, height, curIndex,
curColumnIndex = 0, curRowIndex = 0;
+ 4 public AnimatedSprite(Image image, int count, int columns, int rows, int
offsetX, int offsetY, int width, int height) {
+ 5 this.setImage(image);
+ 6 this.count = count;
+ 7 this.columns = columns;
+ 8 this.rows = rows;
+ 9 this.offsetX = offsetX;
+ 10 this.offsetY = offsetY;
+ 11 this.width = width;
+ 12 this.height = height;
+ 13 this.setViewport(new Rectangle2D(offsetX, offsetY, width, height));
+ 14 }
+ 15 public void tick() {
+ 16 curColumnIndex = curIndex % columns;
+ 17 curRowIndex = curIndex / columns;
+ 18 curIndex = ( curIndex+1) % ( columns * rows);
+ 19 interpolate();
+ 20 }
+ 21 protected void interpolate() {
+ 22 final int x = curColumnIndex * width + offsetX;
+ 23 final int y = curRowIndex * height + offsetY;
+ 24 this.setViewport(new Rectangle2D(x, y, width, height));
+ 25 }
+ 26 }

--- PAGE 261 ---
240 Debugging
This functionality is encapsulated within a newAnimatedSprite class, as shown
in Script4.32. Its core methods,tick() andinterpolate(), use a viewport to se-
quentially select and display individual frames from the sprite sheet, with the frame
changing at each tick of the rendering loop. ThecurIndex variable tracks the cur-
rent frame in the animation sequence, while thecolumns androws variables define
the dimensions of the sprite grid.

4.3.6 Acceleration
The integration of sprite animation results in a more fluid running motion for the
game character. Overall, the application has undergone substantial visual and in-
teractive enhancements since its initial version. However, the character’ s move-
ment can be made more realistic. The current implementation, which uses con-
stant velocity, is a primary source of this unnatural movement.
The next enhancement introduces acceleration to the movement model. Move-
ment along the x-axis will be determined by an applied input force, while move-
ment along the y-axis will be influenced by both input force (for jumping) and
gravity. A simplified acceleration model is implemented to demonstrate the im-
provement in realism compared to the previous constant-velocity model.
Due to the modular design, the implementation of acceleration only requires
modifications to theGameCharacter class. For clarity, the updates to this class are
presented across two separate scripts. Script4.36 presents changes to variable dec-
laration and assignment, along with the updatedmoveX() andmoveY() methods.
Script4.37 details updates to thejump(), checkReachHighest(), andcheckReach-
Floor() methods. Collectively, these changes implement a basic model of projec-
tile motion by incorporating acceleration along both the x and y-axes.
The motion characteristics differ significantly between the two axes. On the
x-axis, horizontal movement initiates from zero velocity and accelerates to a max-
imum defined velocity. Conversely, vertical movement (a jump) begins with an
initial upward velocity that continuously decreases due to gravity until it reaches
zero at the apex of the jump. Subsequently, the character accelerates downward
until it makes contact with the virtual ground.

--- PAGE 264 ---
4.3. Case Study 243

58 if(falling) {
+ 59 yVelocity = yVelocity >= yMaxVelocity? yMaxVelocity : yVelocity+yAcceleration;
60 y = y + yVelocity;
61 }
62 else if(jumping) {
+ 63 yVelocity = yVelocity <= 0 ? 0 : yVelocity-yAcceleration;
64 y = y - yVelocity;
65 }

4.3.7 Logging
While interactive debugging has been instrumental in the development process
thus far, it is also appropriate to explore alternative diagnostic techniques such as
probing and logging. Scripts4.38 and4.39 demonstrate the probing technique ap-
plied to theGameCharacter andGameLoop classes, respectively.

--- PAGE 266 ---
4.3. Case Study 245
A trace() method is introduced to theGameCharacter class, which outputs
the character’ s current coordinates and velocities to the console. Within theGameLoop,
thistrace() method is invoked to allow for the observation of these values during
the character’ s horizontal movement.
However, as probing is often criticized as an overly simplistic technique, it is
recommended to replace it with a structured logging implementation. Logging
provides a more robust and configurable mechanism for tracing program state and
troubleshooting issues.

With the dependencies configured, the next step requires the creation of a
log4j2.properties configuration file. The contents of this file are presented in
Script4.43. This example configuration designates the console as the sole appen-
der and sets the root logging level toINFO. The output pattern is defined to include
the severity level, a detailed timestamp, the thread name, the class name, the log
message, and a line separator. Upon compiling and executing the application, the
console output will adhere to the newly configured log pattern.

--- PAGE 269 ---
248 Debugging
Script 4.43: resources/se233/chapter4/log4j2.properties
+ 1 name = PropertiesConfig
+ 2 appender.console.type = Console
+ 3 appender.console.name = consoleLogger
+ 4 appender.console.layout.type = PatternLayout
+ 5 appender.console.layout.pattern = [%5 p] % d{yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm:ss.SSS} [% t] % c{1} %
msg%n
6
+ 7 rootLogger.level = info
+ 8 rootLogger.appenderRef.stderr.ref = consoleLogger
Finally it is important to note that Logging is evidence, not just debug output.
Log files serve a purpose that extends well beyond development-time debugging.
In a deployed application, structured log output constitutes the primary source of
evidence when diagnosing failures that cannot be reproduced interactively. A well-
configured logging framework records timestamps, thread identifiers, severity lev-
els, and class origins automatically, information that is impossible to reconstruct
from memory or from the unstructured output ofSystem.out.println calls. Log-
ging statements should therefore be treated as permanent, first-class components
of the codebase, designed to remain in place across releases and configured through
severity levels rather than removed when debugging is complete.

day of the week.
4.5 Discussion and Summary
This chapter has examined three progressively capable strategies for identifying
and correcting software defects. The discussion began with probing, a technique
characterized by the insertion ofSystem.out.println statements to observe run-
time states. This approach exposed its inherent limitations in multi-threaded GUI
applications, where concurrent, unstructured output rapidly becomes unmanage-
able. Structured logging was subsequently introduced as a sustainable alternative.
A logging framework provides severity-based filtering, timestamped records, con-
figurable output destinations, and the capacity to remain permanently in the code-
base without interfering with production behavior. The chapter then progressed
to breakpoint-based debugging within the IntelliJ IDEA environment. This sec-
tion demonstrated how stepping through execution flows and inspecting variable
states yields significantly more precise diagnostic information than either prob-
ing or logging alone. Finally, the case study synthesized all three techniques by
applying them to a 2D platformer application, progressing from initial probing to
logging integration, and concluding with the interactive debugging of collision, in-
terpolation, and acceleration logic.

--- PAGE 271 ---
250 Debugging
Knowledge Outcomes
Upon the completion of this chapter, a student should demonstrate the ability to:
• Describe the limitations of print-statement probing, including the inability
to filter by severity, the absence of timestamps and thread identification, and
the risk of diagnostic code leaking into production releases.
• Explain the Log4j2 architecture in terms of its core components, specifically
the Logger, Appender, and Layout, and describe the role each component
plays in the processing pipeline from log event to output destination.
• Distinguish between the standard logging levels (TRACE, DEBUG, INFO, WARN,
ERROR, FATAL) and justify which level is appropriate for a given category of
diagnostic message.
• Explain how a debugger breakpoint differs from a print statement in terms
of execution interference, reproducibility, and the ability to inspect complex
object states without modifying source code.
• Describe the game-loop pattern, encompassing the update, render, and sleep
phases, and explain how frame interpolation smooths visual movement be-
tween discrete ticks.
• Explain why logging statements should be treated as permanent, first-class
code rather than temporary debugging aids, emphasizing the role of log files
as critical post-release diagnostic evidence.
Points for Discussion
1. The probing technique and structured logging both generate textual output;
however, probing is considered a temporary diagnostic measure, whereas
logging represents a permanent architectural component. What specific prop-
erties of a logging framework justify retaining log statements in production
code, and under what conditions might the computational overhead of log-
ging become a performance concern?

--- PAGE 272 ---
4.5. Discussion and Summary 251
2. A breakpoint suspends the entire application at a specific line of code, which
can alter the timing behavior of concurrent or animation-related operations.
How might this observer effect influence the diagnosis of timing-dependent
defects, and what alternative debugging strategies can be employed to miti-
gate this issue?
3. The case study integrates collision detection, interpolation, and acceleration
within a single game loop. If a rendering anomaly occurs during gameplay,
what systematic approach can be utilized to isolate the defect to one of these
three subsystems prior to implementing any code modifications?
Suggested Extensions and Project Ideas
1. Add aRollingFileAppender to the log4j2 configuration that rotates log files
daily and retains the seven most recent files, then verify the rotation be-
haviour by adjusting the system clock or using a scheduled test.
2. Implement a conditional breakpoint in IntelliJ IDEA that suspends execu-
tion only when a character’ s velocity exceeds a defined threshold, and use
this technique to diagnose an intermittent overshooting defect.
3. Extend the game loop by integrating a frame-rate counter displayed within
the application window. Cross-reference any observed frame drops with
WARN-level log entries to identify which subsystem consumes the greatest ex-
ecution time per tick.
4. Introduce a replay mechanism that reads a log file of timestamped input
events and re-executes them against the game loop, enabling deterministic
reproduction of defects that were originally observed only in live play.
