S
oftw aretesting is a fundamental process in software engineering that serves
to ensure the quality of a software product. Its purpose is to evaluate and
verify that a software application functions according to its specified re-
quirements. The benefits of a rigorous testing process include the pre-
vention of defects, reduced long-term maintenance costs, and enhanced overall
software performance.
The aim of software testing is to determine whether an application can operate
correctly when subjected to conditions designed to induce failure. These condi-
tions are introduced through systematically designed test cases, which enable the
observation of the software’ s behavior under specific circumstances. T est cases are
typically designed to cover a broad spectrum of potential issues that may arise in
a production environment. Common software defects include incorrect calcula-
tions, erroneous data manipulation, flawed data matching, inaccurate search re-
sults, faulty processing of data relationships, incorrect implementation of business
rules, and inadequate performance, among others.

--- PAGE 278 ---
5.1. Basic Software Testing 257
5.1 Basic Software Testing
According to Kaner (2009), software testing is defined as a systematic, technical
investigation conducted to provide stakeholders with information about the qual-
ity of the product or service under test. The key terms in this definition require
further clarification. Systematic refers to a methodical approach derived from ex-
perimentation, experience, and observation. T echnical denotes the application of
specific skills and practical knowledge. Investigation signifies a detailed inquiry
or systematic examination. In essence, software testing employs specialized tech-
nical skills and knowledge to provide an evidence-based assessment of a software
project’ s quality.
5.1.1 Importance
As software applications increase in complexity, encompassing more features and
supporting diverse platforms, achieving a defect-free state becomes increasingly
challenging. Defects, in any form, can compromise software functionality and, in
critical cases, lead to severe system malfunctions. The history of software engineer-
ing includes numerous examples of severe consequences resulting from software
defects, ranging from substantial financial losses to physical harm.
For instance, five nuclear power plants in the United States were temporarily
shut down due to a flaw in a simulation program used for designing earthquake-
resistant nuclear reactors (Lin, 1985). The root cause was the use of an arithmetic
sum of a set of numbers instead of their absolute values. An F-18 fighter aircraft
crashed due to a missing else clause in a conditional statement, a scenario that was
erroneously assumed to be impossible (Neumann, 1986). The Ariane 5 spacecraft
exploded during launch due to an integer overflow error (Le Lann, 1997). More
recently, T oyota was required to recall over four million vehicles due to unintended
acceleration caused by software defects (Austen-Smith et al., 2017). These cases
underscore the potential for rigorous testing to have prevented such incidents.
The optimal level of effort and expenditure on testing has been a subject of
debate. The justification for a given level of testing often depends on the potential
cost savings achieved by preventing critical defects. In other words, the decision is
determined by the potential impact of a failure relative to the cost of testing (i.e.,

--- PAGE 279 ---
258 Unit Testing
the risk-to-impact ratio). Furthermore, intangible consequences, such as customer
dissatisfaction and damage to brand reputation, can be prohibitively expensive.
A fundamental principle in software engineering is that defects found later in
the development cycle are more expensive to fix. The cost to resolve a defect in-
creases substantially, often exponentially, as it progresses through the development
lifecycle. For example, the effort required to correct a defect in the requirements
specification that could be fixed in one person-hour during the design phase may
increase exponentially if it is not discovered until after the software has been de-
ployed. These increased costs are attributable to rework in development, additional
meetings, re-testing, and other related activities.
5.1.2 Sources of bugs
As previously established, the cost to correct a defect is significantly lower the ear-
lier it is identified and resolved in the development lifecycle. A study by Perry and
Stieg (1993) revealed that defects introduced during the implementation and test-
ing phases constitute approximately 50 percent of all defects found. This finding
emphasizes the importance of defect detection in the earlier phases of develop-
ment, as the cost and complexity of correction increase significantly for defects
that persist into later stages.
During the requirements phase, defects can be introduced through an inad-
equate specification process. These often manifest as erroneous, incomplete, or
inconsistent requirements, frequently attributable to insufficient diligence during
the requirements gathering and elicitation stages. During the design phase, defects
can arise from flaws in the system’ s architecture or detailed design. A notable exam-
ple is a design flaw discovered in the communication protocol of some Bluetooth-
enabled devices, which created a security vulnerability (Zuo et al., 2019). In the
implementation and testing phases, defects can be introduced directly during cod-
ing or can arise from issues with the configuration of the testing environment.
5.1.3 T esting methods
The V-Model, depicted in Figure5.1, is a widely recognized conceptual model that
illustrates the software testing process in relation to development phases (Java T

--- PAGE 280 ---
5.1. Basic Software Testing 259
Point, 2020). It outlines two primary phases: verification and validation. V erifi-
cation is the process of evaluating work products to determine if they meet their
specified requirements, often involving static analysis of components. V alidation,
by contrast, is the process of evaluating the final software product during or at the
end of development to determine if it satisfies customer expectations. In essence,
verification asks,Are we building the product right?, while validation inquires,Are
we building the right product?
The left side of the V-Model represents the development phases and their cor-
responding work products. The right side enumerates the testing activities that
correspond to each development phase. A brief explanation of the entries on the
left-hand side is provided below:
Figure 5.1: The V-Model of Software Testing (Java T Point 2020)
• Business requirement specification: This phase focuses on defining the
high-level goals and expectations of the customer. Clear communication is
crucial to ensure that the development team fully understands these require-
ments.
• System requirement specification: This stage involves detailing the specific
features, functions, and behaviors of the system required to meet the business
needs.

--- PAGE 281 ---
260 Unit Testing
• High-level design: This phase defines the overall system architecture, in-
cluding the relationships between major components, modules, and data
systems. It describes high-level data flows and data structures.
• Low-level design: This phase involves the detailed design of individual soft-
ware components, often including the creation of class diagrams that specify
variables and methods.
• Implementation: This phase involves the writing of source code to build the
software application according to the design specifications.
The right side of the V-Model illustrates the validation phases, where each level
of testing corresponds to a level on the development (verification) side:
• Unit testing: Corresponding to the Low-Level Design phase, unit testing
focuses on verifying the correctness of the smallest testable parts of an ap-
plication, such as individual methods or classes. These tests ensure that each
unit functions correctly in isolation.
• Component testing: This level of testing, corresponding to the High-Level
Design phase, validates the functionality of individual software components,
which are typically composed of multiple, related units.
• Integration testing: Following component testing, integration testing veri-
fies the interaction and data flow between integrated components to ensure
they function correctly as a combined group.
• System testing: Corresponding to the System Requirement Specification
phase, system testing evaluates the complete and integrated software system
to ensure it meets all specified functional and non-functional requirements.
This is often conducted by a dedicated Quality Assurance (QA) team in an
environment that simulates production.
• Acceptance testing: Corresponding to the Business Requirement Specifi-
cation phase, this is a crucial final stage where the system is evaluated by
end-users or client representatives to confirm that it meets their business

--- PAGE 282 ---
5.1. Basic Software Testing 261
needs and is acceptable for deployment. It focuses on validating the system’ s
functionality and usability in a real-world context.
5.1.4 T esting objectives
The primary objectives of software testing are to identify defects as early as possible
in the development lifecycle and to verify that these defects are resolved. T o achieve
these goals, testers design and implement test cases, which are scripted procedures
or separate programs executed with several intentions: (1) to uncover defects, (2)
to verify that the system meets its specified requirements in its target environment,
(3) to validate that the system is suitable for its intended purpose, and (4) to ensure
the system performs as expected.
The following list outlines common testing objectives:
• Alpha testing: A form of internal acceptance testing conducted by in-house
teams (e.g., developers, QA) to identify defects before the software is released
to external users.
• Beta testing: A form of external acceptance testing where the software is
released to a limited audience of real users outside of the development orga-
nization to obtain feedback and identify defects in a real-world environment.
• Conformance T esting:V erifies that a software product conforms to specific
standards, conventions, or regulations.
• Performance testing: Evaluates a system’ s responsiveness, stability, and scal-
ability under a particular workload.
• Recovery testing: Assesses a system’ s ability to recover gracefully from fail-
ures, such as hardware crashes or network outages.
• Stress testing: Evaluates a system’ s robustness by testing it at or beyond the
limits of its specified operational capacity.
• Usability testing: Evaluates how easy and intuitive a software application is
to use from the perspective of an end-user.

--- PAGE 283 ---
262 Unit Testing
5.2 Unit Testing
This course will focus on unit testing as it serves as the foundation for other testing
methodologies. T o illustrate the concept of software unit testing, an analogy can
be drawn to the process of troubleshooting electronic devices. Consider a scenario
in which a stereo system is faulty, with sound produced by only one channel. The
diagnostic process would involve isolating each major component (e.g., speakers,
amplifier, source) and testing their interactions. This level of testing is analogous to
software integration testing. If the amplifier is suspected as the source of the fault,
its internal components can be tested individually, for instance, by using a multi-
meter to measure voltage. This systematic examination of the smallest functional
parts corresponds to unit testing. Although potentially time-consuming, this gran-
ular approach enables the precise isolation of faulty components. This mirrors the
practice of unit testing in software development, where individual units of code are
tested in isolation to verify their correct functionality.
In a software context, a unit test is therefore a piece of code that exercises a
small, specific unit of functionality, such as a single method within a class. When
a unit test verifies that a code segment performs as expected, that unit can be inte-
grated into the larger system with a higher degree of confidence in its correctness.
A primary challenge in unit testing is the management of external dependen-
cies, such as file systems or databases, which a unit of code may rely upon. T o over-
come this challenge and maintain isolation, testers often use mock objects. These
are simulated objects that mimic the behavior of real dependencies in a controlled
manner. For example, when testing a method that makes a Hypertext Transfer
Protocol (HTTP) request to an external API, the test can be isolated from the net-
work by using a mock object. This mock would not make a real network request
but would instead return a predefined response (e.g., from a local JavaScript Ob-
ject Notation (JSON) file) that mimics the data from the actual API. This approach
provides complete control over the test environment and allows for the predictable
and repeatable verification of the method’ s behavior.

--- PAGE 284 ---
5.2. Unit Testing 263
5.2.1 Objective of unit testing
A well-designed unit testing strategy provides developers with greater confidence
in their code. It is widely recognized that as a software system grows, the likelihood
of defects increases. This is due to both the increased functional scope and the
complex interactions between components. A well-structured suite of unit tests
facilitates the early detection and localization of these defects. By writing unit tests
concurrently with feature development, the number of defects introduced into the
codebase can be minimized. This, in turn, reduces the need for complex debugging
efforts.
It is important to emphasize that the defining characteristic of unit tests is their
isolation. A well-designed unit test must be autonomous and not rely on external
dependencies such as databases, APIs, or network resources. This isolation ensures
that the test exclusively verifies the behavior of the unit under test, which enhances
the reliability and repeatability of the test results.
5.2.2 Example scenario
In the context of an e-commerce application, the shopping cart is a critical compo-
nent that requires rigorous testing to ensure its reliability. Several key test scenarios
are essential, including: adding an item (verifying correct addition and state up-
dates), removing an item (confirming removal and state updates), modifying item
quantities (validating quantity and price changes), and calculating the total price
(verifying correctness across various item combinations, discounts, and updates).
Each unit test focuses on a specific method or function within the shopping
cart’ s source code. For example, a test for theaddItemToCart(Item item, int
quantity) method would invoke it with various inputs to verify that items are
added correctly and that the cart’ s internal state is updated accurately. Similarly,
dedicated unit tests would be created for methods such asremoveItemFromCart(),
updateItemQuantity(), and calculateTotalPrice() to verify their individual
functionality.
Unit testing is of significant importance in this scenario as it allows for the iso-
lation and verification of the smallest functional units of code. By testing individ-
ual components in isolation, unit tests facilitate the rapid identification and correc-

--- PAGE 285 ---
264 Unit Testing
tion of defects within the shopping cart’ s underlying logic. This proactive approach
helps prevent runtime problems such as inaccurate price calculations or item man-
agement failures. Ultimately, a comprehensive suite of unit tests contributes to the
development of a resilient and dependable shopping cart feature, enhancing the
user experience and mitigating potential financial losses from operational failures.
This example emphasizes that unit testing is focused on the granular examina-
tion of individual code components to ensure their correct behavior in isolation.
It is essential to recognize that unit tests, by design, do not evaluate complex inter-
actions between different functions or modules, nor do they test the integration of
business logic with the GUI. These higher-level interactions are assessed through
other testing methodologies, such as integration testing and system testing.
5.2.3 JUnit 5
JUnit (2026) is the predominant framework for conducting unit testing in Java. Its
library provides a comprehensive set of tools that streamline and systematize the
unit testing process. By convention, for a given class namedFoo, a corresponding
test class namedFooTest is created. This test class contains methods designed to
test the public methods ofFoo at the unit level. Each method withinFooTest de-
fines a specific test case that verifies an expected outcome and determines whether
the test passes or fails.
JUnit provides a suite ofassert methods that form the core of a unit test’ s
verification logic. The underlying principle is to invoke an assertion method within
the body of a test method, providing a condition that is expected to evaluate to
true. For example, a test could verify that an emptyArrayList has a size of two
after two elements have been added. This expected outcome would be expressed as
a condition within an assertion. If the condition evaluates tofalse, the assertion
fails, which in turn causes the entire test case to fail.
Script5.1 illustrates an example of aJUnit test class structure.JUnit employs
annotations, such as@Test, to identify test methods. When the test suite is exe-
cuted, theJUnit framework automatically discovers and runs all methods marked
with the@Test annotation, evaluating the assertions within them to determine the
test results.

--- PAGE 286 ---

Script 5.1: Example JUnit 5 Test Case NameTest.java
+ 1 import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
+ 2 import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertTrue;
+ 3
+ 4 class ShoppingCartTest {
+ 5
+ 6 @Test
+ 7 void addItemToCart_givenAnItemAdd_cartShouldContainItemEntity() {
+ 8 ShoppingCart cart = new ShoppingCart();
+ 9 Item item = new Item("Java Textbook", 399.00);
+ 10 cart.addItem(item);
+ 11 assertTrue(cart.getItems().contains(item), "Item not added to cart");
+ 12 }
+ 13 }
Assertion methods
Assertion methods are fundamental tools in unit testing frameworks used to verify
the expected behavior of the code under test. BothJUnit 4 andJUnit 5 offer a
comprehensive suite of static assertion methods, facilitating the evaluation of spe-
cific conditions during test execution. These methods compare the actual outcome
of a variable or expression against a predefined expected value, ensuring it aligns
with the expected behavior. If the assertion condition is not met, the method sig-
nals a test failure by throwing anAssertionError or a similar exception.
In JUnit 4 , the assertion methods typically begin with the keywordassert.
For instance,assertTrue("message", condition) fails the test if the provided
condition evaluates tofalse, optionally displaying a message to provide context
for the failure.JUnit 5 , on the other hand, encapsulates its assertion methods
within theorg.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions class. While the functionality
largely mirrors that ofJUnit 4 , the method names and usage show slight varia-
tions. Other common methods address a variety of assertion requirements. Com-
monly usedJUnit 5 assertion methods include:
• Assertions.assertTrue(condition, message) : Fails if the booleancon-
dition evaluates tofalse.

--- PAGE 287 ---
266 Unit Testing
• Assertions.assertFalse(condition, message) : Fails if the booleancon-
dition evaluates totrue.
• Assertions.assertEquals(expected, actual, message) : Fails if theex-
pected andactual values are not equal.
• Assertions.assertSame(expected, actual, message) : Fails if theexpected
andactual variables do not refer to the same object.
• Assertions.assertNotSame(expected, actual, message) : Fails if theex-
pected andactual variables refer to the same object.
• Assertions.assertNull(object, message) : Fails if theobject is not null.
• Assertions.assertNotNull(object, message) : Fails if theobject is null.
• Assertions.fail(message): Unconditionally fails the test with the provided
message.
• Assertions.assertThrows(expectedType, executable, message) : Fails if
theexecutable (e.g., a lambda expression) does not throw an exception of
theexpectedType.
Script5.2 presents a simpleCalculator class used for demonstratingJUnit
tests. The corresponding test class,CalculatorTest, is illustrated in Script5.3
and is designed to evaluate theCalculator’ s functionality. It contains two distinct
test methods, each preceded by the execution of a setup method. ThesetUp()
method, annotated to run before each test, instantiates theCalculator object. The
first test method,add_givenTwoPositiveNumbers_thenSum() (Line 11), invokes
theadd() method with values 3 and 4 and subsequently verifies that the result is 7
using the assertion in Line 13. The second test method,subtract_givenTwoPos-
itiveNumbers_thenDifference(), follows a similar structure, invoking thesub-
tract() method with values 9 and 5 and using an assertion (Line 18) to confirm
the result matches the expected value of 4.

JUnit naming conventions
JUnit promotes the use of descriptive naming conventions for both test classes and
test methods. A common convention for test classes is to append the suffixTest to
the name of the class being tested. For instance, the test class in Script5.3 is named
CalculatorTest, which clearly indicates that it is designed to test theCalculator
class.
Script 5.3: JUnit Test Class with Two Test Methods CalculatorTest.java
+ 1 import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;
+ 2 import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
+ 3
+ 4 public class CalculatorTest {
+ 5 private Calculator calculator;
+ 6 @BeforeEach
+ 7 void setUp() {
+ 8 calculator = new Calculator();
+ 9 }
+ 10 @Test
+ 11 void add_givenTwoPositiveNumbers_thenSum() {
+ 12 int result = calculator.add(3, 4);
+ 13 assertEquals(7, result);
+ 14 }
+ 15 @Test
+ 16 void subtract_givenTwoPositiveNumbers_thenDifference() {
+ 17 int result = calculator.subtract(9, 5);
+ 18 assertEquals(4, result);
+ 19 }
+ 20 }

--- PAGE 289 ---
268 Unit Testing
T est method names should be self-explanatory, allowing a developer to under-
stand the test’ s purpose and the specific behavior under test without needing to
examine the method’ s implementation. This is crucial because when a test suite
is executed, the names of all executed test methods are typically listed along with
their pass or fail status. A descriptive method name allows a developer to quickly
identify a failed test and understand the context of the failure.
In contrast to standard Java method naming conventions, which often favor
brevity, test method names benefit from being verbose and highly descriptive. Sev-
eral naming conventions have emerged inJUnit testing to clearly convey a test’ s
purpose and scope. The following are some common formats:
1. method_givenCondition_thenExpectedResult: This format explicitly states
the method being tested, the condition or input scenario, and the expected
outcome, e.g.,
• add_givenTwoPositiveNumbers_thenSum
• subtract_givenNegativeNumber_thenDifference
2. testMethodName: This format is simple and straightforward, beginning with
the wordtest followed by the name of the method being tested, e.g.,
• testAdd
• testSubtract
3. shouldExpectedBehavior_whenStateUnderTest: This format emphasizes the
expected behavior and the specific state or scenario under which the test is
performed, e.g.,
• shouldReturnSum_whenTwoNumbersArePositive
• shouldReturnDifference_whenSubtractingSmallerFromLarger
4. whenCondition_thenExpectedResult: This format highlights the condition
and the expected outcome, e.g.,
• whenAddingTwoPositiveNumbers_thenSumIsCorrect
• whenSubtractingSmallerNumber_thenDifferenceIsPositive

--- PAGE 290 ---
5.2. Unit Testing 269
5. givenCondition_whenAction_thenExpectedResult: This format is highly
descriptive, explicitly stating the condition, the action being taken, and the
expected result, e.g.,
• givenTwoPositiveNumbers_whenAdded_thenSumIsCorrect
• givenNegativeNumber_whenSubtracted_thenDifferenceIsCorrect
Script5.4 demonstrates the implementation of test methods that follow several
of these naming conventions. These descriptive conventions effectively communi-
cate the expected behavior being tested, often obviating the need to examine the
implementation details of the test itself. For clarity and consistency, this course-
book will adopt themethod_givenCondition_thenExpectedResult format for all
subsequent test method examples.
Unit testing tips
A commonly recommended practice in unit testing is to design tests that are trust-
worthy and focused. T o achieve this, complex test cases are often decomposed into
multiple, smaller tests, with each test case ideally containing only a single assertion.
This approach ensures that each test method verifies a single, specific aspect of the
unit’ s functionality, which makes failures easier to diagnose.
Furthermore, to maintain test clarity, it is advisable to avoid complex program-
matic logic within test cases. The use of control flow statements such asif-else,
switch, for, andtry-catch can introduce complexity that obscures the test’ s pri-
mary objective. By minimizing such constructs, tests remain concise and focused,
which improves their readability and maintainability.
Exhaustive testing of every possible input is often impractical, particularly for
methods that accept a large or infinite range of values (e.g., real numbers). There-
fore, a strategic approach is to select a limited set of test cases that are most likely to
uncover defects. A common technique for this is boundary value analysis, which
prioritizes the testing of values at the edges of an input domain. For example, when
testing a method that operates on a numeric range, the boundary values (e.g., min-
imum, -1, 0, 1, and maximum) are more likely to expose defects than values in
the middle of the range. Thorough testing of these boundary conditions often pro-

5.2. Unit Testing 271
vides sufficient confidence in the unit’ s correctness without requiring the testing of
numerous other values within the range.
Other scenarios prone to off-by-one errors and other defects include opera-
tions on the boundaries of arrays or collections (e.g., the first and last elements).
This can be a common source of error, particularly for developers accustomed to
different programming languages that may use 1-based instead of 0-based index-
ing. Additionally, inconsistent handling of different representations for empty or
invalid values (e.g.,0, -1, null, NaN, or empty collections) can also introduce de-
fects.
Consider a scenario where the applicable age range for an insurance package is
18 to 65, inclusive. A concise unit test can be constructed usingJUnit to verify if a
given age falls within this specified range. An efficient and comprehensive testing
approach involves selecting test cases from three partitions: ages below 18, ages
within the 18-65 range, and ages above 65. If time and resources permit, testing
the exact boundary values of 18 and 65 can provide additional confidence in the
implementation’ s correctness. T esting additional values within these partitions is
generally considered redundant, as they are not expected to reveal new types of
defects.
For this insurance eligibility scenario, using exceptions and theassertThrows
method provides several advantages over returning a simple boolean value. By
throwing anIllegalArgumentException for invalid ages, the error condition is
made explicit, which offers greater insight into the nature of a test failure. This
approach also allows for distinguishing between different invalid input cases (e.g.,
age too young vs. age too old), which enhances the diagnostic value of the tests.
Furthermore, usingassertThrows strengthens test assertions. It verifies not
only that an exception is thrown but also that the thrown exception is of the ex-
pected type and optionally contains a specific message. This approach creates more
robust tests that can be adapted as the eligibility logic evolves, facilitating the testing
of various invalid input scenarios.
Additionally, modifying theisEligibleForInsurance() method to throw ex-
ceptions rather than returning boolean values follows the design principle of ex-
plicit error signaling, which enhances the overall robustness and maintainability of
the codebase.

+ 34 }

JUnit test suites
T o assess the comprehensive functionality of an application, multiple test classes
are typically executed together.JUnit provides a test suite mechanism to facilitate
this process, which allows for the aggregation of multiple test classes into a single
executable suite. Executing the test suite then invokes all tests from all included
classes in a single, unified run.
In JUnit 5 , creating a test suite requires theorg.junit.platform:junit-platform-
suite-engine dependency. It can be integrated into thepom.xml file as demon-

freshed, a test suite class can be implemented, as shown in Script5.8. This script
demonstrates aJUnit 5 test suite that aggregates tests from theCalculatorTest
andInsuranceEligibilityTest classes. This aggregation is specified in Line 6
using the@SelectClasses annotation.

276 Unit Testing
Mocking
Mocking is a technique used in unit testing to simulate the behavior of real objects,
particularly complex external dependencies such as web services, databases, or file
systems. Because unit testing requires the isolation of the component under test,
the use of live external dependencies would violate this principle. Therefore, mock-
ing is an indispensable technique for testing code that interacts with these external
systems. This approach mitigates the impact of factors such as network latency or
external service availability, ensuring that unit tests are reliable, fast, and focused
exclusively on the functionality of the unit under test. Mocking is particularly use-
ful in the following scenarios:
• When the code under test has dependencies that are difficult or impractical
to set up in a test environment.
• When specific scenarios, such as error conditions or particular data responses,
must be simulated, which may be difficult to reproduce with a live depen-
dency.
• When the code under test must be completely isolated from external factors
to ensure that test failures are attributable only to defects within the unit
itself.
A widely used framework for creating mock objects in Java isMockito (2026). It
enables the isolation of code by allowing developers to substitute real dependencies
with mock objects that have a defined, controlled behavior. This facilitates focused
testing by ensuring the predictable behavior of dependencies and allowing for the
verification of interactions between the code under test and its collaborators. Its
intuitive API simplifies the process of defining the behavior of mock objects, which
promotes the creation of clear and maintainable unit tests.
Script5.9 presents theFetchData class and itsfetchRange() method, which is
a modified version of the method implemented in the case study of Chapter 2. The
purpose of thisfetchRange() method is to retrieve currency data from an external
API for a given currency symbol and a specified number of days. The method is
expected to return an array of date-rate pairs in JSON format. Because this method

reliability and execution speed of the test.

ment before each test case. It initializes a mock for the staticIOUtils.toString()
method from the Apache Commons IO library. It definesmockJsonData, a JSON

The use of mocking ensures that this unit test verifies only the logic within the
fetchRange() method itself, isolating it from external factors like network latency
or API availability. This approach enhances the robustness and portability of the
test, providing high confidence in the method’ s logical correctness.
Reflection
Reflection is a feature that enables the examination and modification of an applica-
tion’ s runtime behavior. It allows a program to inspect its own objects, classes, and
methods at runtime and, if necessary, modify their behavior. A common use case
for reflection in Java is the unit testing ofprivate methods, which are not other-
wise accessible from outside the class. Without reflection, the alternative would be
to change the method’ s visibility topublic for testing purposes, an approach that
is considered an anti-pattern as it violates the principle of encapsulation.
Reflection, by contrast, provides a mechanism to bypass standard access con-
trol, allowingprivate methods to be invoked as if they werepublic. This elimi-
nates the need to modify the source code of the class under test. Specifically, the
reflection API enables a program to perform the following actions at runtime:

(Line 23). By invoking this method with specific arguments (e.g., 3 and 4), the out-
put can be directly tested. In this case, the expected result of 5.0 verifies the correct
implementation of the Pythagorean theorem.
While reflection is a powerful tool, it should be used with caution. Its improper
use can lead to code that is less readable, less maintainable, and more difficult to de-
bug. Furthermore, reflection typically introduces performance overhead and can
create security vulnerabilities if used improperly. Therefore, a careful evaluation of
its trade-offs is necessary before incorporating reflection into an application.

5.3 Test-Driven Development (TDD)
TDD is a fundamental methodology in modern software engineering. This method-
ology reverses the traditional development sequence by requiring the creation of
automated tests prior to the implementation of the corresponding production code.
This paradigm enforces the writing of the minimal amount of production code
necessary to satisfy specific test requirements, resulting in a more robust, tightly
focused architectural design. The standard TDD process executes through the fol-
lowing sequence:
1. Decompose tasks: The overall system requirements are partitioned into smaller,
prioritized logical units.
2. Create a failing test: A single unit is selected, and a test case is authored to
verify its intended functionality. Because no production code exists, this new
test must fail. This deliberate failure confirms that the test itself functions
correctly and accurately targets the unimplemented behavior.

--- PAGE 303 ---
282 Unit Testing
3. W rite production code:The minimal amount of production code is imple-
mented exclusively to satisfy the new test.
4. Run all tests: The comprehensive test suite is executed to verify that the new
implementation satisfies the immediate requirement without introducing re-
gressions into existing functionality.
5. Refactor and repeat: The implementation is restructured to improve archi-
tectural design without altering observable behavior. This cycle repeats se-
quentially until all system requirements are fulfilled.
Authoring test cases before production code enforces strict architectural disci-
pline. Developers must explicitly define the design of the solution, the flow of infor-
mation, expected output parameters, and potential edge cases before any functional
logic is implemented.
The essence of this discipline is formalized in the three rules of TDD established
by Robert C. Martin (Martin, 2007):
1. Production code may only be written to make a failing unit test pass.
2. A unit test may only be written up to the point of failure, with compilation
failures qualifying as legitimate test failures.
3. Production code may only be written up to the point sufficient to make the
currently failing unit test pass.
These three constraints impose a rigorous micro-cycle on the development pro-
cess. The first rule strictly prohibits speculative implementation, ensuring produc-
tion code exists solely to satisfy a concrete test expectation. The second rule pre-
vents the test from over-specifying behavior in a single step, forcing incremental
growth. The third rule guards against over-engineering by limiting the implemen-
tation to the absolute minimum required to satisfy the immediate test. Collectively,
these rules enforce a highly structured cycle wherein test code and production code
evolve in short, verifiable increments.
Although TDD necessitates a substantial initial time investment, this method-
ology produces software characterized by higher quality, fewer runtime defects,

--- PAGE 304 ---
5.3. Test-Driven Development (TDD) 283
and significantly improved long-term maintainability. Furthermore, the rigorous
test-first process ensures a precise translation of system requirements into func-
tional code.
5.3.1 Benefits
TDD offers several distinct advantages in the software development process. By
writing tests before the corresponding production code, developers receive imme-
diate feedback on their implementation. This feedback is provided by a compre-
hensive suite of tests that covers both new and existing functionality. This allows
for continuous regression testing, ensuring that new additions do not negatively
impact existing functionality.
The TDD workflow naturally encourages the decomposition of complex prob-
lems into smaller, testable units. This provides a structured context for making
low-level design decisions. By focusing on writing only the code necessary to pass
a specific test, developers are guided away from overengineering, resulting in a
more focused and efficient codebase.
Because each unit test has a limited and well-defined scope, the required re-
work to fix a failing test is typically less complex compared to debugging failures in
larger, more integrated tests. Furthermore, TDD produces a comprehensive suite
of automated tests as a natural byproduct of the development process. This test
suite can be executed at any time to validate the correctness of the application and
detect regressions introduced by recent changes.
In summary, development teams that follow TDD principles can potentially
achieve higher code quality, increased productivity, and a comprehensive test suite
with greater overall coverage. A concrete demonstration of TDD will be presented
in the second case study of this chapter.
5.3.2 Red-Green-Refactor: A Worked Example
The TDD cycle is commonly referred to as thered-green-refactor loop (Beck, 2003).
T o make this cycle concrete, consider a minimalCalculator class that must pro-
vide anadd method accepting two integers and returning their sum. The following

--- PAGE 305 ---
284 Unit Testing
three steps trace the full loop from a failing test through a passing implementation
to a refactored design.
Step 1. Red: Write a Failing T est. Before any production code exists, a JUnit test
class is created that describes the expected behaviour of theadd method. Because
theCalculator class has not yet been implemented, the project will not compile,
and any attempt to run the test suite will produce a failure. This deliberate failure
is thered phase: it confirms that the test itself is functional and that the expected
behaviour is not yet satisfied.
Script 5.13: Failing Test forCalculator.add() CalculatorTest.java
+ 1 import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
+ 2 import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;
+ 3
+ 4 class CalculatorTest {
+ 5
+ 6 @Test
+ 7 void add_givenTwoIntegers_shouldReturnTheirSum() {
+ 8 Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
+ 9 int result = calculator.add(3, 7);
+ 10 assertEquals(10, result);
+ 11 }
+ 12
+ 13 @Test
+ 14 void add_givenNegativeOperands_shouldReturnCorrectSum() {
+ 15 Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
+ 16 assertEquals(-5, calculator.add(-2, -3));
+ 17 }
+ 18
+ 19 @Test
+ 20 void add_givenZero_shouldReturnTheOtherOperand() {
+ 21 Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
+ 22 assertEquals(4, calculator.add(0, 4));
+ 23 assertEquals(4, calculator.add(4, 0));
+ 24 }
+ 25 }

--- PAGE 306 ---
5.3. Test-Driven Development (TDD) 285
Script5.13 contains three test methods. The first verifies the straightforward
addition of two positive integers. The second covers the boundary condition of
negative operands. The third checks the identity property of zero. T ogether, these
tests establish a specification for theadd method before any implementation exists.
Attempting to compile at this point fails because theCalculator class is undefined.
Step 2. Green: Write the Minimum Code to Pass. The objective of thegreen
phase is to make all tests pass with the least amount of production code. Over-
engineering is intentionally avoided; the focus is on satisfying the test assertions
and nothing more.
Script 5.14: Minimal Calculator Implementation Calculator.java
+ 1 public class Calculator {
+ 2
+ 3 public int add(int a, int b) {
+ 4 return a + b;
+ 5 }
+ 6 }
Script5.14 provides the simplest implementation that satisfies all three tests.
Running the test suite at this point produces three passing tests, confirming that
the specification is met. The project is now in thegreen state.
Step 3. Refactor: Improve the Design Without Changing Behaviour. With a
passing test suite acting as a safety net, the code can be restructured to improve
readability, reduce duplication, or prepare for future extension. In this example, the
test class contains a repeated instantiation ofCalculator in every test method. This
duplication can be eliminated by extracting the object creation into a@BeforeEach
setup method, as demonstrated in Script5.15.
After refactoring, the test suite is executed once more to verify that all tests still
pass. This confirmation is essential: the refactoring must not alter the observable
behaviour of the code. The@BeforeEach annotation causes thesetUp() method
to execute before each test method, guaranteeing that every test operates on a fresh
Calculator instance. This pattern becomes increasingly valuable as the number

--- PAGE 307 ---
286 Unit Testing
of test methods grows, as it centralises object construction and reduces the main-
tenance burden of modifying initialisation logic.
The red-green-refactor cycle then repeats for the next piece of functionality.
For instance, asubtract method would begin with a new failing test (red), proceed
through the minimal implementation (green), and conclude with any necessary
restructuring (refactor). Over successive iterations, the production code and the
test suite grow incrementally, each addition validated by the full suite of existing
tests.
Script 5.15: Refactored Test with @BeforeEach CalculatorTest.java
+ 1 import org.junit.jupiter.api.BeforeEach;
+ 2 import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
+ 3 import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;
+ 4
+ 5 class CalculatorTest {
+ 6 private Calculator calculator;
+ 7
+ 8 @BeforeEach
+ 9 void setUp() {
+ 10 calculator = new Calculator();
+ 11 }
+ 12
+ 13 @Test
+ 14 void add_givenTwoIntegers_shouldReturnTheirSum() {
+ 15 assertEquals(10, calculator.add(3, 7));
+ 16 }
+ 17
+ 18 @Test
+ 19 void add_givenNegativeOperands_shouldReturnCorrectSum() {
+ 20 assertEquals(-5, calculator.add(-2, -3));
+ 21 }
+ 22
+ 23 @Test
+ 24 void add_givenZero_shouldReturnTheOtherOperand() {
+ 25 assertEquals(4, calculator.add(0, 4));
+ 26 assertEquals(4, calculator.add(4, 0));
+ 27 }
+ 28 }

5.4. Case Study (Part 1) 287
5.4 Case Study (Part 1)
AI Advisory
AI tools can generate unit tests rapidly, but tests generated without explicit
comprehension tend to verify trivial logic and overlook meaningful edge
cases. Generating a passing test suite is fundamentally distinct from design-
ing a suite that guards against regressions, documents contractual behavior,
and reveals boundary-condition defects. T est design is a rigorous reason-
ing task that requires domain-specific knowledge beyond the capability of
statistical language models.
Recommended Approach
AI assistance is highly appropriate for boilerplate construction, such as
generating a JUnit 5 test class skeleton, producing the@BeforeEach setup
method, or scaffolding a standardassertEquals pattern, provided that the
expected behavior is already completely understood. However, deciding
what to test, identifying which inputs constitute critical boundary cases,
and determining which execution paths are most prone to runtime errors
must remain an analytical, human-driven design activity. Following the in-
dependent design of a test, an AI tool may be consulted to suggest additional
edge cases for a pure function, but each suggestion must be critically eval-
uated against the actual contract of the function.
Crucially, the V-Model presented earlier in this chapter establishes that ev-
ery unit test must trace its origin directly to a specific requirement on the
left-hand side of the V . A test that merely confirms an implementation de-
tail, such as verifying that a method returns the exact value it was previously
assigned, provides no assurance that the underlying requirement is satisfied.
AI-generated tests frequently exhibit this specific weakness, as they tend to
replicate the syntax of the production code rather than the specification the
code was authored to fulfill. Effective test design necessitates examining the
core requirement, explicitly identifying the acceptance criteria, and subse-

--- PAGE 309 ---
288 Unit Testing
quently constructing test cases designed tofail if those criteria are violated.
This requirement-driven practice fundamentally distinguishes a robust test
suite from an ineffective suite that achieves high coverage metrics while fail-
ing to guard against unintended side-effects.
Appropriate Uses
✓ Utilize an AI tool to generate a JUnit 5 test class skeleton once the class
under test and its expected behavior are fully comprehended.
✓ Consult an AI tool for Mockito syntax regarding a specific mocking sce-
nario after the test architecture has been designed independently.
✓ Request an AI tool to suggest edge cases for a pure function, subse-
quently evaluating each suggestion against the documented contract of
the function prior to adoption.
✓ Employ an AI tool to generate repetitive assertion patterns for data-
driven tests where the underlying assertion logic has already been vali-
dated manually.
Practices to Avoid
× Do not request an AI tool to “write tests for this class” without explicitly
specifying the behavior to be verified. The resulting tests will frequently
pass without validating any meaningful functionality.
× Do not delegate test-coverage targets or test-design decisions to an AI
tool. Coverage is a metric of thoroughness, not correctness; only the
developer can accurately determine which execution paths carry archi-
tectural risk.
× Do not accept AI-generated Mockito stubs without verifying that the
mock behavior precisely matches the actual interface contract of the de-
pendency being replaced.

--- PAGE 310 ---
5.4. Case Study (Part 1) 289
× Do not use an AI tool to interpret a failing test before reading the failure
message independently and formulating a concrete hypothesis regard-
ing the root cause.

In the current implementation, two distinct characters are animated using sep-

--- PAGE 312 ---
5.4. Case Study (Part 1) 291
Collisions detected along the x-axis are resolved by reverting both characters
to their positions from the preceding frame. This logic prevents the characters
from overlapping or passing through each other horizontally. For collisions along
the y-axis, the method determines which character is positioned above the other.
This differentiation allows for distinct outcomes based on the nature of the vertical
collision. For instance, a future implementation might award points to the charac-
ter on top, while the character below enters a “defeated” state and is subsequently
respawned at its initial coordinates.
Therespawn() method (Lines 134-144) is responsible for resetting a charac-
ter’ s state variables to their initial values. Several new variables are introduced to
support this respawn mechanism. Specifically, thestartX andstartY variables
store the character’ s initial coordinates, allowing the character to be returned to its
original position by reassigning these values to the currentx andy coordinates.
The updatedDrawingLoop class, presented in Script5.17, incorporates the logic
for character collision detection. The conditional statement in Line 27 uses the
getBoundsInParent().intersects() method to determine if the bounding boxes
of the two character sprites are overlapping. If an overlap is detected, the corre-
spondingGameCharacter objects invoke their respectivecollided() methods to
handle the collision, as shown in Lines 28 and 29.

5.4.2 Death scene rendering
With the current implementation, the application provides a more complete game-
play experience. However, further refinements can be made to improve the overall
presentation. The next update introduces a simple animation for the character’ s
“defeated” state, as the current instantaneous respawn upon being stomped can be
visually jarring and disrupt gameplay.
Script5.18 introduces modifications to thecollided() method of theGameChar-
acter class and adds a new method namedcollapsed(). Specifically, when a char-
acter is stomped, it will now enter a “collapsed” animation state. After a brief delay
of 300 milliseconds, the character will be respawned at its initial coordinates. This
enhancement adds a clear visual indicator for a character’ s defeat, which makes the
gameplay more intuitive and visually polished.

5.4.3 Scoreboard
The final feature to be implemented for this platformer game is the scoreboard. The
scoring logic requires that each character begins with a score of zero, and their score
is incremented by one each time they successfully stomp on the opposing character.
Unlike the previous enhancement, implementing the scoreboard requires the cre-
ation of a new view class to display the players’ scores. This new view component is
then integrated into the mainGameStage. Script5.19 presents the implementation
of this scoreboard component.

--- PAGE 316 ---
5.4. Case Study (Part 1) 295
The required modifications to the application logic are detailed in Scripts5.20,
5.21, and5.22. TheGameStage class (Script5.20) is updated to include instances of
the newScore view component. TheGameCharacter class is updated with a new
instance variable,score, which is initialized to zero and incremented each time the
character successfully stomps on an opponent.
TheGameLoop acts as the controller for this scoreboard functionality. On each
tick of the game loop, it synchronizes the score displayed in the view with the cur-
rent score value stored in eachGameCharacter instance. With the implementation
of these final updates, the case study application is complete.
Figure5.4 illustrates the completed version of the game from this case study.
Common implementation errors that may be encountered include the absence of
required accessor methods (Getters), missing exception handling logic, or incom-
plete dependency configurations.

300 Unit Testing
The@BeforeAll annotation on Line 14 designates a method that is executed
once before any tests in this class are run. Similarly, the@BeforeEach annotation
on Line 18 designates a method that is executed before each individual test method.
These annotations are useful for preparing shared resources, such as initializing an
object instance, prior to the execution of tests. The lambda expression on Line 16
initializes the JavaFX toolkit, which is a prerequisite for testing JavaFX components.
In this example, thesetUp() method creates aGameCharacter instance that is
used by the test method. The single test case in this script (Lines 22-32) verifies
that the values passed to theGameCharacter constructor are correctly assigned to
their corresponding instance variables. Upon execution, this test should pass.
5.4.5 Unit testing with reflection
The following section demonstrates the testing of the character movement logic.
The primary challenge in testing this functionality is the need to verify the state of
private fields, which are not directly accessible from an object instance. As previ-
ously discussed, the reflection API provides a mechanism to address this situation.
The updatedGameCharacterTest class, shown in Script5.24, utilizes reflec-
tion to inspect the internal state of theGameCharacter object. By declaringField
objects forxVelocity, yVelocity, and yAcceleration, the test class prepares to
access these private fields. ThesetUp() method initializes theGameCharacter in-
stance and then uses reflection to make theseprivate fields accessible for the dura-
tion of the test. The method is declared to throwNoSuchFieldException to handle
potential discrepancies between the test code and the implementation, ensuring
that the test setup fails gracefully if a specified field does not exist.
The test methods, located at Lines 43, 50, and 59, each verify a specific behav-
ior of theGameCharacter class. They are declared to throwIllegalAccessExcep-
tion to handle potential issues when accessing theprivate fields. For example, the
moveX_givenMoveRightOnce_thenXCoordinateIncreasedByXVelocity() test (Line
43) verifies that invoking the character’ s move method correctly updates its x-coordinate
based on its x-velocity. Similarly, the other tests verify that vertical velocity in-
creases as expected (Line 50) and that vertical acceleration remains constant (Line
59). Collectively, these tests verify that the internal motion logic of theGameChar-
acter class functions as expected.

5.5 Case Study (Part 2)
The second case study of this chapter demonstrates the TDD principle through the
implementation of a simplified variation of the classic snake game. Following the
TDD process, development begins with the decomposition of the project into its
essential features. The primary functional requirements for the snake game are as
follows:
• The snake must move one unit in its current direction per game tick.
• The snake’ s direction of movement must be controllable across four axes (up,
down, left, right).
• The snake must be able to collide with food items.
• Upon colliding with a food item, the snake’ s length must increase.
• After a food item is consumed via collision, a new food item must be respawned
at a different location.
• A collision between the snake’ s head and any part of its own body must result
in a “game over” state.
• A collision between the snake’ s head and the game boundaries must also
result in a “game over” state.

• The correct update of the snake’ s position after a single game tick, where it is

--- PAGE 326 ---
5.5. Case Study (Part 2) 305
Script5.26 presents anenum that represents the four cardinal directions of the
snake’ s movement. ThisDirection enumeration is utilized by theSnake class. The
initial version of theSnake class, presented in Script5.27, is implemented with
the minimal code required to pass the existing test cases. A snake is represented
by a List of 2D coordinates, where each coordinate in theList corresponds to a
segment of the snake’ s body.
Movement is calculated based on the snake’ s head. Therefore, a dedicated vari-
able is required to track the head’ s position in addition to theList representing the
body. Furthermore, the coordinates of the snake’ s tail are also tracked in a sepa-
rate variable. This variable is used to determine which coordinate to remove from
the bodyList each time the snake moves. Theupdate() method implements the
movement logic according to the following steps:
• Determine the new coordinate to which the snake’ s head will move.
• Remove the coordinate that currently represents the snake’ s tail from the
body List.
• After the move, add the new head coordinate to the bodyList.
The other methods included in the initial implementation are accessor methods
(Getters) for the variables required by theSnakeTest class.

5.5.3 Implementing snake death condition
The initial requirements specify two terminal conditions for the snake: collision
with the game boundaries and collision with its own body. Script5.33 addresses
these scenarios through the implementation of two test methods within theSnakeTest
class:checkDead_ifHitGameBorder_snakeWillDie andcheckDead_ifHitItself_-
snakeWillDie.
T o satisfy these test cases, a new method namedcheckDead() is introduced
to theSnake class, as shown in Script5.34. The checkDead_ifHitGameBorder_-
snakeWillDie() test case positions the snake near a boundary and sets its direction
towards that boundary. After a single update, a collision is guaranteed, and the test
then asserts that the snake’ s state is correctly set todead.
For thecheckDead_ifHitItself_snakeWillDie() test case, the snake’ s length
is first increased to a point where a self-collision is possible. The test logic then
directs the snake to turn back on itself, ensuring a collision with its own body.
Subsequently, the test asserts that the snake’ s state is updated todead as expected.
With these modifications implemented, the entire test suite can be executed to
verify that all eight test cases now pass.

5.5.4 The game loop
T esting the game loop presents a significant challenge due to its tight coupling with
theGameStage, Snake, andFood classes, which requires the interaction of multiple
components. This interdependence violates the principle of isolation required for
a true unit test and is more characteristic of an integration test. However, with
careful design, specific elements within the game loop can still be subjected to unit
testing.
Script 5.35: test/java/se233/chapter5part2/ReflectionHelper.java
+ 1 package se233.chapter5part2;
+ 2
+ 3 import java.lang.reflect.Field;
+ 4 import java.lang.reflect.Method;
+ 5
+ 6 public class ReflectionHelper {
+ 7 public static void setField(Object object, String fieldName, Object value)
throws Exception {
+ 8 Field field = object.getClass().getDeclaredField(fieldName);
+ 9 field.setAccessible(true);
+ 10 field.set(object, value);
+ 11 }
+ 12 public static Object getField(Object object, String fieldName) throws
Exception {
+ 13 Field field = object.getClass().getDeclaredField(fieldName);
+ 14 field.setAccessible(true);
+ 15 return field.get(object);
+ 16 }
+ 17 public static Object invokeMethod(Object object, String methodName, Class<?>[]
parameterTypes, Object... args) throws Exception {
+ 18 Method method = object.getClass().getDeclaredMethod(methodName,
parameterTypes);
+ 19 method.setAccessible(true);
+ 20 return method.invoke(object, args);
+ 21 }
+ 22 }

--- PAGE 334 ---
5.5. Case Study (Part 2) 313
Suitable candidates for unit testing include key processing logic, collision de-
tection algorithms, and rendering logic. These can be tested by focusing on the
GameLoop class while using mock objects to simulate the behavior of its collabo-
rators. For instance, key press events can be mocked to verify that the snake’ s di-
rection is updated correctly. Similarly, collision logic can be tested by using mock
Snake andFood objects to verify outcomes such as snake growth, food respawn-
ing, and the triggering of a “game over” state. The rendering logic can be verified by
inspecting the state of internal variables to confirm that the snake and food com-
ponents have been correctly updated for the next frame.
T o facilitate the assignment and retrieval ofprivate variable values, as well as
the invocation ofprivate methods during testing, a helper class namedReflec-
tionHelper is introduced (Script5.35).
Script5.36 illustrates theGameLoopTest class, designed to test theGameLoop
class. TheGameLoop implementation follows the structure from the previous case
study and consists of three primaryprivate methods:keyProcess(), checkCol-
lision(), andredraw(). Due to theirprivate visibility, the reflection technique
is employed to test them. WithinGameLoopTest, a helper method namedclock-
TickHelper() is implemented. This method simulates a single game tick by se-
quentially invoking the three mainprivate methods of theGameLoop class, which
improves the organization and maintainability of the test code.

+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class GameLoopTest {
+ 3 private GameStage gameStage;

+ 6 private GameLoop gameLoop;
+ 7 @BeforeAll
+ 8 public static void initJfxRuntime() {
+ 9 javafx.application.Platform.startup(() -> {
+ 10 });

+ 12 @BeforeEach
+ 13 public void setUp() {
+ 14 gameStage = new GameStage();
+ 15 snake = new Snake(new Point2D(0, 0));
+ 16 food = new Food(new Point2D(0, 1));
+ 17 gameLoop = new GameLoop(gameStage, snake, food);
+ 18 }
+ 19 private void clockTickHelper() throws Exception {
+ 20 ReflectionHelper.invokeMethod(gameLoop, "keyProcess", new Class<?>[0]);
+ 21 ReflectionHelper.invokeMethod(gameLoop, "checkCollision", new Class<?>[0]);
+ 22 ReflectionHelper.invokeMethod(gameLoop, "redraw", new Class<?>[0]);
+ 23 }

+ 26 ReflectionHelper.setField(gameStage, "key", KeyCode.RIGHT);
+ 27 ReflectionHelper.setField(snake, "direction", Direction.DOWN);
+ 28 clockTickHelper();
+ 29 Direction currentDirection = ( Direction) ReflectionHelper.getField(snake, "
direction");
+ 30 assertEquals(Direction.RIGHT, currentDirection);
+ 31 }

+ 33 public void collided_snakeEatFood_shouldGrow() throws Exception {
+ 34 clockTickHelper();
+ 35 assertTrue(snake.getLength() > 1);
+ 36 clockTickHelper();
+ 37 assertNotSame(food.getPosition(), new Point2D(0, 1));
+ 38 }
+ 39 @Test
+ 40 public void collided_snakeHitBorder_shouldDie() throws Exception {
+ 41 ReflectionHelper.setField(gameStage, "key", KeyCode.LEFT);
+ 42 clockTickHelper();
+ 43 Boolean running = ( Boolean) ReflectionHelper.getField(gameLoop, "running");
+ 44 assertFalse(running);
+ 45 }
+ 46 }

5.5. Case Study (Part 2) 315
The test class in Script5.36 contains three test methods that verify the key pro-
cessing and collision logic. However, directly testing theredraw() method with
JUnit alone is challenging, as it requires verifying that a method was invoked on
a collaborator object rather than checking a return value. For this type of inter-
action testing, a mocking framework such as Mockito is the recommended tool.
Script5.37 provides an example of how mocking can be utilized for this purpose.
Script 5.37: test/java/se233/chapter5part2/GameLoopTest.java
1 //Imports are omitted
...
16 public class GameLoopTest {
...
+ 60 @Test
+ 61 public void redraw_calledThreeTimes_snakeAndFoodShouldRenderThreeTimes() throws
Exception {
+ 62 GameStage mockGameStage = Mockito.mock(GameStage.class);
+ 63 Snake mockSnake = Mockito.mock(Snake.class);
+ 64 Food mockFood = Mockito.mock(Food.class);
+ 65 GameLoop gameLoop = new GameLoop(mockGameStage, mockSnake, mockFood);
+ 66 ReflectionHelper.invokeMethod(gameLoop, "redraw", new Class<?>[0]);
+ 67 ReflectionHelper.invokeMethod(gameLoop, "redraw", new Class<?>[0]);
+ 68 ReflectionHelper.invokeMethod(gameLoop, "redraw", new Class<?>[0]);
+ 69 verify(mockGameStage, times(3)).render(mockSnake, mockFood);
+ 70 }
71 }
The corresponding production code to be implemented is theGameLoop class.
An examination of the test classes (Scripts5.36 and5.37) indicates that theGameLoop
class must contain instances of theGameStage, Snake, andFood classes. Script5.38
presents the full implementation of theGameLoop class, including theupdate(),
checkCollision(), andredraw() methods, which is designed to pass all test cases
defined in theGameLoopTest class.
T o use Mockito, themockito-core library must be added as dependencies; the
required pom.xml configuration is shown in Script5.39.

316 Unit Testing
Script 5.38: controller/GameLoop.java
+ 1 //Imports are omitted
+ 2 public class GameLoop implements Runnable {
+ 3 private GameStage gameStage;
+ 4 private Snake snake;
+ 5 private Food food;
+ 6 private float interval = 1000.0 f / 10;
+ 7 private boolean running;
+ 8 public GameLoop(GameStage gameStage, Snake snake, Food food) {
+ 9 this.snake = snake;
+ 10 this.gameStage = gameStage;
+ 11 this.food = food;
+ 12 running = true;
+ 13 }
+ 14 private void keyProcess() {
+ 15 KeyCode curKey = gameStage.getKey();
+ 16 Direction curDirection = snake.getDirection();
+ 17 if (curKey == KeyCode.UP && curDirection != Direction.DOWN)
+ 18 snake.setDirection(Direction.UP);
+ 19 else if (curKey == KeyCode.DOWN && curDirection != Direction.UP)
+ 20 snake.setDirection(Direction.DOWN);
+ 21 else if (curKey == KeyCode.LEFT && curDirection != Direction.RIGHT)
+ 22 snake.setDirection(Direction.LEFT);
+ 23 else if (curKey == KeyCode.RIGHT && curDirection != Direction.LEFT)
+ 24 snake.setDirection(Direction.RIGHT);
+ 25 snake.move();
+ 26 }
+ 27 private void checkCollision() {
+ 28 if (snake.collided(food)) {
+ 29 snake.grow();
+ 30 food.respawn();
+ 31 }
+ 32 if (snake.checkDead()) { running = false; }
+ 33 }
+ 34 private void redraw() { gameStage.render(snake, food); }
+ 35 @Override
+ 36 public void run() {
+ 37 while (running) {
+ 38 keyProcess();
+ 39 checkCollision();
+ 40 redraw();
+ 41 try {
+ 42 Thread.sleep((long)interval);
+ 43 } catch (InterruptedException e) {
+ 44 e.printStackTrace();
+ 45 }
+ 46 }
+ 47 }
+ 48 }

5.6 Exercise (2 parts)
5.6.1 Part 1
1. Implement two test cases for thecheckReachGameWall() method in theGameChar-
acter class. The first test should verify the collision logic for the game bound-
ary on the left side, and the second should verify the logic for the game
boundary on the right side.
2. Implement two test cases for thejump() method in theGameCharacter class.
One test must verify the case where a jump can be successfully initiated, and
the other must verify the case where a jump is not permitted (e.g., the char-
acter is already airborne).
3. Implement two test cases for thecollided() method in theGameCharacter
class. The first should verify a horizontal (x-axis) collision, and the second
should verify a vertical (y-axis) collision. These tests will require the instan-
tiation of a secondGameCharacter object to act as the collision target.
4. Create a new test class for theKeys class to verify its key-press handling logic.
This class must include at least two test cases: one to verify the state change

--- PAGE 343 ---
322 Unit Testing
for a single key press and another to verify the correct state when multiple
keys are pressed simultaneously.
5.6.2 Part 2
1. The current implementation contains a defect where the game does not ter-
minate immediately upon a boundary collision. Use the debugger to identify
and correct this issue. After resolving the defect, implement a feature that
displays a “Game Over” message in a popup window when the snake’ s death
condition is met.
2. Following the TDD process, first create a test method, then implement the
corresponding production code for a scoring mechanism where consuming
a standard food item awards one point.
3. Following the TDD process, create a test and then the corresponding imple-
mentation for a special food item. This special food item should be visually
distinct (e.g., colored green) and should award five points when consumed.
4. Following the TDD process, develop a test and then the corresponding im-
plementation to enforce a new movement rule: the snake must be prevented
from reversing its direction (e.g., changing from moving right to moving left
without an intermediate turn).
5.7 Discussion and Summary
This chapter has presented software testing as a systematic, evidence-based dis-
cipline and has progressively built the technical foundation required to apply it in
practice. The discussion began with the V-Model, which established the correspon-
dence between each development phase and its associated testing activity, thereby
framing unit testing within the broader quality assurance lifecycle. The chapter
then introduced JUnit 5 as the primary testing framework, covering annotations,
assertion methods, naming conventions, test suites, and the mocking of external
dependencies using Mockito. Reflection was examined as a mechanism for testing

--- PAGE 344 ---
5.7. Discussion and Summary 323
private methods without compromising encapsulation. The treatment of TDD for-
malized thered-green-refactor cycle through Martin’ s three rules (Martin, 2007),
reinforcing the principle that production code should exist only to satisfy a con-
crete, failing test. Two case studies applied these techniques to interactive JavaFX
applications: the first extended the platformer game from Chapter4 with collision,
death-scene, and scoreboard logic validated by JUnit tests and reflection; the sec-
ond constructed a Snake game entirely through the TDD process.
Knowledge Outcomes
Upon the completion of this chapter, a student should demonstrate the ability to:
• Explain the V-Model of software testing, identifying how unit testing, inte-
gration testing, system testing, and acceptance testing each correspond to a
specific development phase.
• Describe the purpose of JUnit 5 annotations (@Test, @BeforeEach, @AfterEach,
@TestInstance) and explain how they control test lifecycle and instance
management.
• Justify the use of descriptive test-method naming conventions, such as the
Given-When-Then pattern, and explain how such names improve diagnostic
clarity when a test fails.
• Explain the role of mocking in unit testing, specifically how substituting live
dependencies with controlled mock objects preserves test isolation and re-
peatability.
• Describe how Java reflection bypasses access-control restrictions to invoke
private methods, and analyze the trade-offs in readability, performance, and
security that this technique introduces.
• State the three rules of TDD and explain how each rule constrains the rela-
tionship between test code and production code during thered-green-refactor
cycle.

--- PAGE 345 ---
324 Unit Testing
Points for Discussion
1. The V-Model pairs each development phase with a corresponding testing
activity, yet many agile teams iterate through multiple phases within a single
sprint. How can the principles of the V-Model be reconciled with an iterative
development workflow, and what risks arise if the correspondence between
development and testing phases is not maintained?
2. Reflection enables the testing of private methods without altering their vis-
ibility, but this approach creates a dependency on internal implementation
details that may change across refactoring cycles. Under what circumstances
does the diagnostic value of testing a private method outweigh the mainte-
nance cost of a reflection-based test, and when is it preferable to test the
private logic indirectly through public methods?
3. The third rule of TDD restricts production code to the minimum required to
pass the current failing test. In practice, developers frequently anticipate fu-
ture requirements and write code beyond what the immediate test demands.
What are the concrete consequences of violating this rule, and how does
strict adherence influence the long-term design quality of a codebase?
Suggested Extensions and Project Ideas
1. Integrate a code-coverage tool, such as JaCoCo, into the Maven build config-
uration and generate a coverage report for the platformer test suite. Identify
untested branches in thecollided() method and write additional test cases
to achieve full branch coverage.
2. Implement a parameterized test class using@ParameterizedTest and@Csv-
Source to replace repetitive assertion patterns in theCalculatorTest class,
and compare the resulting test readability and maintenance burden with the
original approach.
3. Extend the Snake game with a persistent high-score feature, developing the
entire implementation through the TDDred-green-refactor cycle. Begin with

--- PAGE 346 ---
5.7. Discussion and Summary 325
a failing test that asserts the correct serialization and deserialization of score
data, then build the minimal production code to satisfy each successive test.
Progress Checklist
□ Explain the V-Model of software testing and identify the testing activ-
ity that corresponds to each development phase.
□ Distinguish unit testing from integration testing and system testing in

ternal dependency and verify interaction behavior.

validate internal logic without modifying the visibility of the method
under test.
□ Apply the TDDred-green-refactor cycle to implement a feature, pro-
gressing from a failing test through minimal production code to a
refactored design.
