S
oftw arebuild management systems are essential components of Software
Configuration Management (SCM), a critical support discipline in the soft-
ware development process. SCM is primarily concerned with managing
changes, corrections, extensions, and adaptations made to a software ap-
plication during its lifecycle. In practice, SCM is implemented through a variety of
tools, such as Git (Git, 2026) for version control, Apache Maven (Apache Maven,
2026) for build automation, and Docker (Docker, 2026) for containerization. Key
SCM functionalities include build and release management, workspace manage-
ment, versioning, dependency management, and change management.
This chapter will primarily focus on two of these functionalities: software build-
ing and dependency management. Other SCM concepts will be introduced in more
advanced courses within the software engineering curriculum.

--- PAGE 350 ---
6.1. Built Tool and Dependency Management 329
6.1 Built Tool and Dependency Management
6.1.1 Build tool
A build tool is a software utility designed to automate the process of converting
source code files into a standalone, executable artifact. It acts as a central orches-
trator, managing tasks such as compilation, linking, packaging, and testing. This
automation not only streamlines the development workflow but also ensures con-
sistency and reproducibility across different environments. At its core, a build tool
comprises two fundamental components:
• Configuration: A build script or configuration file that defines the project’ s
structure, dependencies, and the sequence of tasks required to build the soft-
ware. This is often written in a declarative, domain-specific language (DSL).
• Execution engine: An executable program that parses the configuration file
and executes the defined tasks in the correct order. It invokes external tools
such as compilers and linkers to produce the final build artifact.
One of the defining characteristics of modern build tools is their platform inde-
pendence. They are designed to function seamlessly across various operating sys-
tems, minimizing the need for manual configuration and adaptation. This porta-
bility is essential in collaborative development environments where team members
may use different operating systems. The key responsibilities of a modern build tool
include the following:
• Incremental builds: The tool intelligently identifies which parts of the source
code have been modified since the last build and recompiles only those af-
fected files and their dependents. This incremental approach significantly
reduces build times, especially for large projects.
• Dependency management: The tool automates the process of downloading
and managing external libraries and frameworks (dependencies), ensuring
that the correct versions are available during the build process.

--- PAGE 351 ---
330 Build Management System
• Build profiles: Build tools allow developers to define different build config-
urations or profiles. For example, a project might have separate profiles for
development, testing, and production environments, each with its own set
of compiler flags, optimization settings, and resource configurations.
• Adaptability: As software projects evolve, so do their requirements and de-
pendencies. A build tool should be flexible enough to accommodate these
changes with minimal manual intervention. It should provide mechanisms
for easily updating dependencies, adding new build steps, or modifying ex-
isting ones.
• Automation: The primary goal of a build tool is to automate repetitive and
error-prone tasks. By automating the build process, developers can focus on
writing code and implementing features, rather than dealing with the intri-
cacies of compilation and deployment.
6.1.2 Dependency management
In practical software development, individual projects rarely exist in complete iso-
lation. They frequently rely on external libraries or components, creating a network
of dependencies. These dependencies, such as the libraries used for PDF extraction
or logging, require careful management to ensure compatibility and avoid version
conflicts. The manual management of these dependencies (a process that includes
downloading libraries, adding them to a project, and verifying compatibility) is
often cumbersome and error-prone, particularly in large-scale projects.
Dependency management tools automate this process. They can automatically
identify, download, and resolve the versions of required dependencies, ensuring
the seamless integration of external components. Modern build tools often incor-
porate dependency management as a core feature. This level of automation stream-
lines the development workflow and allows developers to focus on core application
logic rather than on managing third-party libraries.
Furthermore, modern build tools often provide comprehensive, automated build
pipelines that encompass tasks beyond dependency management, such as compi-
lation, testing, packaging, documentation generation, and deployment. These au-

--- PAGE 352 ---
6.2. Java Build Management System 331
tomated pipelines collectively enhance the efficiency and reliability of the entire
software development lifecycle.
6.2 Java Build Management System
There are three prominent build management systems for the Java platform: Apache
ANT with Ivy (Apache ANT+Ivy, 2017), Apache Maven (Apache Maven, 2026),
and Gradle (Gradle, 2026).
Introduced in 1999, ANT (Another Neat T ool) was the first widely adopted
Java build tool, recognized for its compact, extensible design and platform inde-
pendence. It provided users with fine-grained control over the build process. How-
ever, ANT’s reliance on Extensible Markup Language (XML) for its configuration
files, initially considered a strength, became a limitation as more concise notations
like domain-specific languages (DSLs) gained popularity. Additionally, the lack
of a predefined build lifecycle and integrated dependency management hindered
ANT’ s scalability in larger projects. While Apache later introduced Ivy to address
dependency management, this occurred after the release of Apache Maven, which
had already achieved significant adoption due to its native dependency manage-
ment capabilities.
Maven, which means accumulator of knowledge in Yiddish, is currently one
of the most popular Java build tools. Despite also using XML for configuration,
Maven’ s success can be attributed to two key features: its robust dependency man-
agement and its standardized build process. Maven simplifies the process of pack-
aging software and adding required dependencies to a project. Prior to Maven,
developers primarily relied on ANT , which was often criticized for requiring the
repetition of common build steps across different projects. Maven addressed this
by introducing the concept of a build lifecycle, which is composed of predefined
phases (e.g., compile, package) common to most development projects.
Gradle, released in 2007, is the most recent of the three build tools discussed. It
is designed with a focus on multi-project builds, a common requirement for mod-
ern, large-scale systems. Unlike ANT and Maven, Gradle employs a Groovy or
Kotlin-based DSL for its build scripts, which are typically more compact and read-
able than their XML-based counterparts.

--- PAGE 353 ---
332 Build Management System
This course focuses on Maven to demonstrate the components of a Java build
management system. Its structured, convention-based approach is well-suited for
introducing these concepts.
6.2.1 Apache Maven
Apache Maven is widely recognized for its standardized build lifecycle and its stream-
lined dependency management. A key advantage of Maven is that managing de-
pendencies can often be accomplished with minimal effort. For example, Maven’ s
dependency management eliminates the need to manually download and config-
ure project libraries. Instead, a developer simply declares the required dependency
coordinates in the project’ s configuration file, and Maven automatically handles the
process of downloading and linking the appropriate library.
Maven build lifecycle
The Maven build lifecycle is defined as a sequence of named phases, where each
phase represents a specific stage in the build process. Each phase consists of one or
more goals that are executed in order. For example, thepackage phase has the goal
of bundling the compiled code into a distributable format, such as a Java Archive
(JAR) file. Maven defines a default lifecycle that includes 23 phases, though only a
subset is commonly used for a typical project. The following list outlines common
phases along with their descriptions:
• clean: Deletes all artifacts generated by previous builds.
• validate: V alidates that the project is correct and all necessary information
is available.
• process-resources: Copies and processes resource files into the target di-
rectory.
• compile: Compiles the project’ s source code.
• process-test-resources: Copies and processes test resource files into the
test target directory.
• test-compile: Compiles the test source code.

--- PAGE 354 ---
6.2. Java Build Management System 333
• test: Executes unit tests.
• package: Packages the compiled code into its distributable format (e.g., a
JAR file).
• verify: Runs any checks to verify the package is valid and meets quality
criteria.
• install: Installs the package into the local Maven repository for use as a
dependency in other local projects.
• deploy: Copies the final package to a remote repository for sharing with
other developers and projects.
In practice, a developer invokes a single phase, and Maven executes that phase
along with all preceding phases in the defined lifecycle. For example, executing
the package phase will trigger validate, compile, test, and finally package. A com-
mon command used to build and install a typical Java project is mvn install, which
executes the entire lifecycle up to the install phase (McIntosh, Adams, & Hassan,
2012). The deploy phase is typically invoked separately to publish the final artifact
to a shared repository.
Maven standard directory layout
Maven employs a standard directory layout to enforce a consistent project struc-
ture, which facilitates automated builds. Without this convention, projects could
adopt disparate folder structures, complicating navigation and the onboarding pro-
cess for new developers.
Because this standard layout is independent of any specific IDE, Maven projects
can be seamlessly imported into various IDEs, such as IntelliJ IDEA and Eclipse,
without compatibility issues. A newly created Maven project adheres to the follow-
ing standard directory layout:

--- PAGE 355 ---
334 Build Management System
src
main
java
resources
test
java
resources
target
pom.xml
The root directory contains two primary subdirectories:src andtarget. The
src directory holds all source materials for the project, including source code and
resource files. It is further subdivided intomain for the primary application code
andtest for the testing code. Bothmain andtest contain ajava directory for
source code and aresources directory for other assets. Thejava directories follow
the standard package structure, while theresources directories contain files that
are bundled into the final build artifact.
A crucial component of this structure is thepom.xml file, which is the POM
for the project. This file serves as the central configuration point where developers
define project metadata, dependencies, and build instructions for Maven.
Project object model (POM)
POM is an XML file located at the root directory of a Maven project. This file con-
tains essential information about the project and the configuration settings used
for building it. When initiating a build, Maven reads the POM file to understand
the developer’ s intent and configure the build process accordingly. The informa-
tion commonly specified in a POM file includes project properties, dependencies,
plugins, goals, build profiles, project version, and project descriptions.
Before creating a POM file, it is necessary to define the project’ s group, name,
and version. Maven designates these identifiers asgroupId, artifactId, andver-
sion, respectively. These identifiers, along with the specified Maven model version,
constitute the minimum requirements for a valid POM file. An example of a min-
imal POM file is illustrated in Script6.1.

--- PAGE 356 ---
6.2. Java Build Management System 335
Script 6.1: Minimum Requirements for a pom.xml File pom.xml
+ 1 <project>
+ 2 <modelVersion>4.0.0</modelVersion>
+ 3
+ 4 <groupId>se233</groupId>
+ 5 <artifactId>chapter6</artifactId>
+ 6 <version>1.0-SNAPSHOT</version>
+ 7 <name>chapter6</name>
+ 8 </project>
The following provides detailed explanations for each tag in Script6.1:
• <project>: The root element of the POM file.
• <modelVersion>: The version of the POM model itself, which should be set
to 4.0.0 for Maven 2 and later.
• <groupId>: A unique identifier for the project’ s group, typically representing
the organization or project collection.
• <artifactId>: The identifier for the project, corresponding to the artifact
(e.g., JAR, W AR) name.
• <version>: The specific version of the project. This identifier is used in an ar-
tifact repository to distinguish between different releases. It is conventional
to append-SNAPSHOT to the version to indicate that the project is in an
active development phase.
Script6.2 expands upon the minimal POM file by incorporating additional
details concerning project packaging, properties, and dependencies. In this exam-
ple, the project specifies its source file encoding asUTF-8. Moreover, it declares
project dependencies, which are enumerated under the<dependencies> tag. Each
dependency follows the<groupId>:<artifactId>:<version> coordinate system,
mirroring the format used to identify the project itself. In this instance, the project
incorporates theorg.openjfx:javafx-controls andorg.openjfx:javafx-fxml
libraries, both at version 26.0.1.

A common question regarding dependency management is how to determine
the appropriate XML snippet to add to thepom.xml file for a required library. A
straightforward approach is to search for the library’ s name followed by the term
“Maven dependency” or “pom” in a search engine. Results often lead to a repository
website, such as MVNRepository (https://mvnrepository.com/). Figure6.1 il-

6.3 Continuous Integration (CI)
CI is a fundamental practice in modern software development that involves the
frequent merging of code changes into a shared repository, with a recommended
frequency of at least once per day. The primary objective of CI is to maintain a
consistently integratable codebase, ensuring that the software can be successfully
built, executed, and deployed at any time while passing all automated tests. By in-

--- PAGE 359 ---
338 Build Management System
Script 6.3: pom.xml File with Properties, Dependencies and Plugins pom.xml
1 <project>
2 <modelVersion>4.0.0</modelVersion>
...
11 <dependencies>
12 <dependency>
13 <groupId>org.openjfx</groupId>
14 <artifactId>javafx-controls</artifactId>
15 <version>26.0.1</version>
16 </dependency>
+ 17 <dependency>
+ 18 <groupId>org.apache.logging.log4j</groupId>
+ 19 <artifactId>log4j-core</artifactId>
+ 20 <version>2.25.4</version>
+ 21 </dependency>
22 </dependencies>
23 </project>
tegrating changes early and often, development teams can proactively identify and
resolve integration problems before they escalate, thereby fostering collaboration
and continuous improvement.
CI serves as a preventive measure against the accumulation of integration issues
that arise when developers work in isolation for extended periods. Without CI,
these issues may remain hidden until later stages, leading to a scenario commonly
termedintegration hell, where merging code from multiple developers becomes
an exceptionally complex and time-consuming undertaking. Frequent integration
allows developers to identify conflicts and incompatibilities quickly, resolving them
while the context of the changes is still recent.
Beyond preventing integration problems, CI facilitates faster feedback loops
through automated tests that execute with each integration, providing immediate
insights into the impact of any changes. This rapid feedback enables the early de-
tection and remediation of bugs, which is typically more efficient and economical.
Additionally, CI enhances collaboration by encouraging developers to share their
work regularly and communicate potential issues promptly.

--- PAGE 360 ---
6.3. Continuous Integration (CI) 339
Figure6.2 depicts a typical CI workflow, wherein code commits to a version
control system (e.g., Git) trigger an automated pipeline. This pipeline builds the
code, runs a suite of automated tests, and, if successful, can trigger a continuous
delivery (CD) pipeline for deployment. This automated process ensures that each
code change is thoroughly tested and integrated, reducing the risk of introducing
errors into the main codebase.
Figure 6.2: Example CI Workflow
Build tools play a crucial role in CI by automating the build process, which en-
sures consistency and repeatability. They manage tasks such as dependency resolu-
tion, code compilation, test execution, and software packaging. Popular build tools
for Java, such as Ant, Maven, and Gradle, offer features like predefined build lifecy-
cles, customizable build scripts, and integration with testing frameworks, making
them invaluable assets in CI pipelines.
CI is often coupled with CD, which automates the release of software to pro-
duction environments. By automating the entire software delivery pipeline, in-

--- PAGE 361 ---
340 Build Management System
cluding code integration, testing, and final deployment, CD enables organizations
to achieve a rapid time-to-market. This streamlined process eliminates manual in-
terventions, which reduces the risk of errors during deployments. Consequently,
new features, enhancements, and bug fixes can be promptly delivered to end-users.
The adoption of CD empowers development teams to respond swiftly to market de-
mands, thereby fostering a competitive advantage.
6.4 Case Study
AI Advisory
This chapter demonstrates how the deliberate, informed use of AI assistance
transitions from cautious experimentation to professional practice. Build
configuration is declarative, compositional, and immediately verifiable: a
correct POM compiles, whereas an incorrect one does not. These properties
make dependency declarations, plugin configurations, and CI pipeline defi-
nitions well-suited to AI-assisted completion, provided that every generated
suggestion is treated as a preliminary draft requiring verification rather than
as a finalized artifact.
Recommended Approach
When adding a dependency or configuring a CI workflow, begin by under-
standing the structural requirements of the task: the minimum POM ele-
ments, the applicable Maven lifecycle phase, or the GitHub Actions work-
flow syntax. CI platforms such as GitHub Actions use Y AML (Y AML Ain’t
Markup Language), a human-readable data-serialization format, to define
pipeline configurations. Use GitHub Copilot to accelerate the mechanical
aspects of the task, such as auto-completing coordinate triples, suggesting
plugin configuration blocks, or scaffolding a CI workflow definition. Subse-
quently, verify every generated value against an authoritative source before
committing it to the repository.

--- PAGE 362 ---
6.4. Case Study 341
Appropriate Uses
✓ Use Copilot to auto-complete repetitive POM dependency blocks after
thegroupId, artifactId, andversion structure is fully understood.
✓ Use Copilot to generate a starter GitHub Actions workflow for a Maven
project. Because CI workflow files utilize Y AML syntax that may be
unfamiliar, every line of the generated file must be reviewed and com-
prehended before it is committed to a repository.
✓ Use the Copilot chat panel to query quick-reference information, such
as “what Maven lifecycle phase runs unit tests?” , as a supplement to of-
ficial documentation.
✓ Use an AI tool to compare Maven and Gradle for a specific project sce-
nario after the Maven lifecycle has been studied independently.
✓ Treat every AI-generated version number as a suggestion; always verify
the version against the Maven Central Repository.
Practices to Avoid
× Do not accept an AI-suggested<version> for a security-sensitive li-
brary without verifying the Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures
(CVE) status of that specific release.
× Do not allow Copilot to generate an entirepom.xml file from scratch
without first comprehending the minimum POM requirements detailed
in this chapter.
× Do not use AI tools to bypass an understanding of the Maven build
lifecycle; the phases and their execution order constitute foundational
knowledge for professional Java development.

--- PAGE 363 ---
342 Build Management System
× Do not assume that a Copilot-generated CI workflow file will function
correctly for a specific project structure without systematically reading
and verifying every line of the configuration.
Reflecting across the six chapters, the AI advisory progression of this
coursebook follows the same instructional logic as the chapters themselves:
mastering the fundamentals, understanding the failure modes, and subse-
quently introducing tools that amplify existing capabilities. A developer
who has manually traced an MVC event flow, deliberately handled a net-
work exception, diagnosed a thread-safety issue using a breakpoint, and
authored a robust unit test suite is fully equipped to utilize GitHub Copi-
lot productively. This exact profile of technical competence defines the in-
tended state of the reader upon concluding this coursebook.
6.4.1 Managing Maven project
The case study from the previous chapter,chapter5part1, demonstrated the ease
with which a Maven project can be reused. For instance, the project was provided
as a zip file viahttps://cmu.to/SE233CH5P1. Because the project did not require
modifications to itspom.xml file, the only step needed to reuse it on a different ma-
chine was to copy thesrc directory. The project’ s existing configuration, including
itsproperties, dependencies, and plugins, facilitates this portability. Once the
project is opened in an IDE like IntelliJ IDEA and Maven is refreshed, the build
tool automatically configures the project based on thepom.xml file, eliminating the
need for further manual setup.
T o initiate the present case study, a new project,chapter6, is created by cloning
thechapter5part2 (snake game) project. This is accomplished by copying the en-
tiresrc directory and thepom.xml file fromchapter5part2 into the newchap-
ter6 project directory. Thepom.xml file is included to simulate a real-world sce-
nario where specific dependency versions are required to avoid package conflicts.
In this case,org.junit.platform:junit-platform-suite-engine:1.14.4 was
added as a project dependency.

--- PAGE 364 ---
6.4. Case Study 343
However, this action alone is insufficient for Maven to configure and run the
project. First, the project’ s coordinates,<groupId>:<artifactId>:<version>, must
be updated to match the new project’ s identity. Script6.4 provides the initial update
for thepom.xml file, setting the coordinates tose233:chapter6:1.0-SNAPSHOT. T o
rename the project’ s packages, the IDE’ s refactoring function can be utilized, as il-
lustrated in Figures6.3. By right-clicking on these233.chapter5part2 package,
selectingRefactor and thenRename, choosing the option to renameAll directo-
ries, and entering the new package namese233.chapter6, all package declarations
are updated accordingly. An additional manual edit is required for themodule-
info.java file, as shown in Script6.5. After completing these modifications, the
project should build and run successfully without errors.
Suppose logging functionality is to be integrated into the project. In prior
case studies, the instructor provided the<groupId>:<artifactId>:<version> co-
ordinates for stable versions of thelog4j-core, log4j-api, and log4j-slf4j-
impl libraries. However, retrieving these coordinates is a straightforward task.
T o do so, one can search for these libraries on a public repository such ashttps:
//mvnrepository.com/ and copy the provided Maven dependency XML for the
latest stable version into the project’ spom.xml file.
Script6.6 provides an example of the updatedpom.xml file demonstrating the

select the most recent stable versions available at the time of implementation.

Ordinarily, this JAR file can be exe-

--- PAGE 369 ---
348 Build Management System
manifest attribute in chapter6-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar(see Figure6.5) indicates a con-
figuration issue that must be resolved. T o address this error, two actions are re-
quired:
• Inclusion of Dependencies: All project dependencies must be bundled within
the JAR file.
• Specification of Main Class : The entry point, or main class, that is called
upon execution must be explicitly designated.
These requirements can be met by modifying thepom.xml file to include a build
plugin, such as theMaven shade plugin or theMaven assembly plugin . These
plugins automate the process of packaging dependencies and specifying the main
class, ensuring that the final JAR file is executable.

After rebuilding the project with the updated configuration, attempting to exe-
cute the JAR file may result in a different error:“Error: JavaFX runtime components
are missing and are required to run this application”. This issue, specific to JavaFX
applications, requires additional steps to resolve. A potential solution, outlined on
Stack Overflow athttps://stackoverflow.com/questions/52653836, indicates
a class loading problem and recommends creating a separate launcher class as a
workaround.
Following this suggestion, a new Java class namedJarLauncher must be cre-
ated with the content provided in Script6.10. The pom.xml file is then modified
to designate this newJarLauncher class as the main class for the executable JAR,
rather than the originalLauncher class. Script6.11 illustrates the updatedpom.xml
configuration reflecting this change.
After implementing these modifications and re-executing the Maven “clean”
and “package” lifecycle goals, the game should be launchable. The application can
be started either by double-clicking thechapter6-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar file or by
running the commandjava -jar chapter6-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar from the project’ s
target directory.
6.5 AI-Assisted Development and GitHub Copilot
Throughout the preceding chapters, each AI Advisory section has identified a spe-
cific category of task in which uncritical reliance on AI-generated code would prove
counterproductive: untraceable MVC integration in Chapter1, silenced exceptions
in Chapter2, unsafe threading in Chapter3, bypassed debugging in Chapter4, and
ineffective tests in Chapter5. In each of these instances, the inherent risk arose
from delegating tasks wherecontext and reasoning are strictly irreplaceable. Build
configuration, however, occupies a fundamentally different position on this spec-
trum.
POM declarations, Maven dependency blocks, and CI pipeline definitions are
largelydeclarative and compositional. The correct parameters are retrievable di-
rectly from official documentation, the structure is highly standardized, and the
failure mode, such as a build that fails to compile or a pipeline that aborts, is im-
mediate and unambiguous. Consequently, this is precisely the category of task in

--- PAGE 372 ---
6.5. AI-Assisted Development and GitHub Copilot 351
which AI-assisted development tools provide high-value assistance with compara-
tively low risk.
6.5.1 What is GitHub Copilot?
GitHub Copilot is an AI-powered code-completion tool developed by GitHub in
collaboration with OpenAI. It operates as an integrated plugin within the IDE, ana-
lyzing the surrounding code context, including open files, comments, and function
signatures, to generate inline suggestions that range from single-line completions
to multi-line code blocks. Copilot is trained on a large corpus of publicly available
source code and documentation, which enables it to produce syntactically correct
suggestions for a wide variety of languages, frameworks, and configuration for-
mats.
Unlike a general-purpose chatbot, Copilot is designed for in-editor use: it re-
sponds to the immediate editing context rather than to a conversational prompt.
This contextual awareness makes it particularly effective for repetitive, pattern-
based tasks such as writing boilerplate code, completing structured configuration
files, and generating standard code templates. However, the same mechanism that
enables rapid suggestion also introduces the risk of hallucinated dependencies, out-
dated version numbers, and configurations that appear plausible but do not match
the specific requirements of the project.
6.5.2 Integration with IntelliJ IDEA
Copilot integrates into IntelliJ IDEA as a plugin installable through the IDE plu-
gin marketplace. Once installed and authenticated with a GitHub account, the tool
provides real-time inline suggestions as development progresses. Suggestions ap-
pear as grayed-out text that can be accepted with a single keystroke or dismissed to
continue manual entry. The plugin also provides a chat panel for querying specific
details about the current file or project without requiring the developer to navigate
outside the IDE environment.
The integration is context-sensitive: when editing apom.xml file, Copilot uti-
lizes its knowledge of Maven conventions to suggest dependency blocks, plugin
configurations, and property declarations. When editing a CI pipeline configu-

--- PAGE 373 ---
352 Build Management System
ration file written in Y AML (a widely used data-serialization format for DevOps
tooling), Copilot can suggest workflow structures, job definitions, and common
step configurations tailored for Java and Maven projects. This specificity distin-
guishes Copilot from general search engines, as it generates suggestions that are
syntactically aligned with the specific file currently active in the editor.
6.5.3 Appropriate Use Cases in Build Configuration
The declarative nature of build and CI configuration files makes them well-suited to
AI-assisted completion. The following scenarios represent cases in which Copilot
can meaningfully accelerate the development workflow:
• Dependency declarations. When initiating a<dependency> block in a POM
file, Copilot can suggest the correct<groupId>, <artifactId>, and <ver-
sion> for widely used libraries such as JUnit, Log4j, or Mockito. The sug-
gested version must be verified against the Maven Central Repository
(mvnrepository.com) prior to acceptance, as the tool may generate an out-
dated or deprecated release.
• CI workflow scaffolding. For projects hosted on GitHub, Copilot can gener-
ate a starter GitHub Actions workflow file that checks out the repository, con-
figures a Java environment, and executesmvn test . The generated Y AML
typically adheres to the standard structure documented in the official GitHub
workflow templates, but must be reviewed to ensure compatibility with the
specific project layout and Java version.
• Plugin configuration. Maven plugins, such as themaven-compiler-plugin,
maven-surefire-plugin, and maven-shade-plugin, require specific con-
figuration blocks. Copilot can suggest these blocks based on the plugin name
and the surrounding POM context, minimizing the necessity to consult doc-
umentation for routine configurations.
• Repetitive structural patterns. In multi-module projects, the parent POM
and child POM structures follow a well-defined architectural pattern. Copi-
lot can replicate this pattern across modules, significantly reducing manual
duplication and adaptation efforts.

--- PAGE 374 ---
6.5. AI-Assisted Development and GitHub Copilot 353
6.5.4 Limitations and Verification Habits
Despite its effectiveness in declarative contexts, Copilot remains a statistical model
that generates suggestions based on patterns in its training data rather than on ver-
ified, domain-specific knowledge. The following limitations require active mitiga-
tion:
• Version accuracy. Copilot may suggest a library version that was current at
the time of its training but has since been superseded or found to contain
a security vulnerability. Every suggested<version> tag must be verified
against the Maven Central Repository, and for security-sensitive libraries,
the CVE database should be consulted.
• Project-specific configuration. Copilot lacks awareness of the specific mod-
ule structure, Java version, or custom plugin requirements of the project cur-
rently under development. A suggestion that compiles in isolation may fail
within the context of a multi-module build or conflict with existing plugin
configurations.
• CI environment assumptions. A generated CI workflow may assume a de-
fault operating system, Java distribution, or caching strategy that does not
align with the project’ s actual deployment environment. Each line of a gen-
erated CI configuration file must be reviewed and comprehended prior to
repository integration.
• Licensing and compliance. Code suggestions derived from publicly avail-
able repositories may inadvertently introduce code patterns that carry licens-
ing obligations. For production projects, the provenance of AI-generated
code segments should be reviewed against organizational compliance poli-
cies.
6.5.5 Lessons from This Course
The AI advisory progression of this coursebook follows the same structural logic
as the chapters themselves: mastering the fundamentals, understanding the failure
modes, and subsequently introducing tools that amplify existing capabilities. A

--- PAGE 375 ---
354 Build Management System
developer who has manually traced an MVC event flow (Chapter1), deliberately
handled a network exception (Chapter2), diagnosed a thread-safety issue using a
breakpoint (Chapter4), and authored a robust unit test suite (Chapter5) possesses
the requisite domain knowledge to evaluate whether an AI-generated suggestion is
correct, adequate, or misleading.
This foundational competence transforms the dynamic with AI assistance from
one of dependency to one of technical collaboration. Build configuration, as demon-
strated in this chapter, represents a clear example of a domain where AI tools op-
erate optimally: the tasks are highly structured, the correctness criteria are strictly
verifiable, and the consequence of an error is a failed build rather than a silent run-
time defect. The critical competency lies not in the mere operation of Copilot, but
rather in the ability to determinewhen the generated output is trustworthy and
when manual reasoning remains strictly necessary.

6.7. Discussion and Summary 355
6.7 Discussion and Summary
This chapter has positioned build management as a foundational practice within
SCM, progressing from the manual compilation of individual source files to the
fully automated build-test-deploy pipelines that define professional Java develop-
ment. The discussion began by distinguishing build tools from Build Management
Systems, establishing that the latter provide dependency resolution, lifecycle man-
agement, and plugin extensibility beyond simple compilation. Apache Maven was
examined in detail: its convention-over-configuration philosophy, the standard di-
rectory layout, the sequential phases of the default build lifecycle, and the structure
of the Project Object Model that governs every Maven project. Continuous Integra-
tion was then introduced as the practice of merging and verifying code changes at
least daily, utilizing automated pipelines that execute the full Maven lifecycle upon
each commit. The chapter concluded with an examination of AI-assisted develop-
ment through GitHub Copilot, demonstrating that declarative, pattern-based con-
figuration tasks, such as POM authoring, dependency declaration, and CI work-
flow scaffolding, represent the domain in which AI tools deliver optimal value,
provided that every generated suggestion is verified against authoritative sources.
Knowledge Outcomes
Upon completion of this chapter, a student should demonstrate the ability to:
• Explain the role of Software Configuration Management in the software de-
velopment lifecycle and identify how build management, version control,
and dependency management contribute to project integrity.
• Distinguish between a build tool that automates compilation and a Build
Management System that additionally manages dependencies, enforces a stan-
dard project structure, and provides an extensible plugin architecture.
• Describe the Maven standard directory layout and explain how the convention-
over-configuration principle minimizes the requirement for explicit build
instructions.

--- PAGE 377 ---
356 Build Management System
• Trace the execution order of the Maven default build lifecycle phases and
explain how each phase contributes to producing a tested, packaged artifact.
• Specify the purpose of Continuous Integration in a team-based workflow
and explain how automated build and test execution upon each commit pre-
vents the accumulation of integration defects.
• Evaluate the suitability of AI-assisted code completion for declarative con-
figuration tasks and explain why verification against official repositories and
documentation remains strictly necessary.
Points for Discussion
1. Maven enforces a single standard directory layout and a fixed lifecycle phase
order, while Gradle offers a flexible, script-based approach that allows devel-
opers to define custom build logic. What are the trade-offs between convention-
driven rigidity and script-driven flexibility, and under what project condi-
tions does each approach provide a net advantage?
2. Continuous Integration requires that all developers integrate their changes
into a shared repository at least once per day. In practice, developers some-
times delay integration to avoid exposing incomplete work. What strategies
can a team adopt to reconcile the CI requirement of frequent integration with
the reality that not every feature can be completed within a single day?
3. The chapter argues that build configuration is a domain where AI-assisted
tools provide high-value assistance with low risk, in contrast to the cautions
raised in earlier chapters. Under what circumstances could this assessment
prove incorrect; that is, in what scenarios might an AI-generated POM or CI
configuration introduce a defect that is not immediately caught by a failed
build?
Suggested Extensions and Project Ideas
1. Create a multi-module Maven project that separates a JavaFX application
into distinct modules for the model, view, and controller layers. Configure

--- PAGE 378 ---
6.7. Discussion and Summary 357
the parent POM to manage shared dependencies and plugin versions cen-
trally, and verify that themvn package command produces a deployable ar-
tifact from the aggregated modules.
2. Set up a GitHub Actions CI pipeline for one of the earlier coursebook projects
that automatically executes the full JUnit test suite on every push to themain
branch. Configure the pipeline to report test results as a build status check
and investigate the behavior when a deliberately failing test is committed.
3. Compare the Maven and Gradle build systems by migrating the case study
project from this chapter to a Gradle-based configuration. Document the
structural differences in project layout, dependency declaration syntax, and
build lifecycle, and measure the build execution time for both systems under
identical conditions.
4. Investigate the Maven dependency tree for a project that includes transitive
dependencies by executingmvn dependency:tree . Identify at least one case
of a transitive dependency that could introduce a version conflict, and con-
figure a<dependencyManagement> block to resolve it.

APPENDIX B
Appendix B
T
his chapter outlines the creation of the Currency Exchange Application
Programming Interface (API) developed for the case study in Chapter2.
This API application is constructed by integrating several services from
Amazon W eb Services (A WS, 2026). Specifically, the API is composed
of two distinct applications: the Exchange Rate Processor and the API Endpoint.
Exchange Rate Processor
The primary function of this module is to fetch and prepare historical currency
data from external sources. The architecture consists of a scheduled A WS Lambda
function coupled with Amazon S3 for storage. The architecture diagram, pre-
sented in FigureB.1, comprises four main components: Amazon EventBridge,
A WS Lambda, the internet, and an A WS S3 bucket.
Amazon EventBridge is configured with a rule that triggers an A WS Lambda
function on a schedule, such as daily, shortly after midnight Coordinated Univer-
sal Time (UTC). The A WS Lambda function serves as the core engine of this ap-
plication, executing code that retrieves currency data from a data provider over
the internet. In the past, developers could easily access various commercial finan-
367

--- PAGE 389 ---
368 Appendix B
Figure B.1: Data Processing Architecture Diagram
cial APIs to fetch historical and live currency exchange rates. However, in recent
years, these commercial data providers have become increasingly expensive, often
imposing high monthly subscription fees and strict limitations on the number of
requests allowed. This makes relying on commercial APIs highly impractical for
an academic project like that of this coursebook.
T o overcome this limitation, the Bank of Thailand (BOT) API (Bank of Thai-
land, 2026) serves as an excellent alternative. Although the BOT API provides daily
average exchange rates rather than real-time data, this frequency is more than ad-
equate to demonstrate the system architecture and serve the educational purposes
of this book. It should be noted that while the BOT provides this data for public ac-
cessibility, users must agree to their terms of service, which state that the BOT does
not guarantee the complete accuracy or timeliness of the data, and users assume all
responsibility for its use in their applications.
T o utilize the BOT API, registration on the portal is required. Once registered,
developers must create a new application. For example, an application named
SE233Exchange was created for this case study. This would generate an authen-
tication key (BOT_DATA_API_KEY) that will be later used in the source code to ac-
cess this API. The application must then be subscribed to the Exchange Rates Plan.
While this plan offers an unlimited quota, it strictly enforces a rate limit of 200 calls
per hour. Additionally, the API restricts data retrieval to a maximum of 31 days per
single request.
Once the exchange data are retrieved, the Lambda function selects and refor-

--- PAGE 390 ---
Appendix B 369
mats this data into a structure suitable for the use case. This processed data acts as
a cache for the API Endpoint application, which prevents end-users from directly
interacting with the BOT API and minimizes unnecessary processing of irrelevant
information. After preprocessing, the data is stored as a binary file in an Amazon
S3 bucket. While numerous storage options exist, ranging from flat files to special-
ized low-latency databases, S3 was chosen for its simplicity and cost-effectiveness.
With this architecture, the application can serve query responses in approximately
300 milliseconds, a timeframe deemed sufficiently fast for this educational context.
A more detailed discussion on storage choices is provided later in this chapter.
The BOT API’ s Average Exchange Rate endpoint provides daily historical data
for a wide range of currencies, inherently using the Thai Baht (THB) as the base cur-
rency. Although the API supports many more options, this case study intentionally
limits the retrieval to a specific subset of 13 major foreign currencies (AUD, CAD,
CNY , EUR, GBP , HKD, IDR, INR, JPY , MYR, PHP , SGD, and USD). Because the
31-day data limit requires multiple queries per currency to compile a multi-month
history, restricting the list of currencies minimizes the total number of requests.
This ensures the application stays well below the strict rate limit of 200 calls per
hour. The data refreshes at the end of every business day. When queried, the API
returns a JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) structure containing a nesteddata_-
detail array, with each daily record formatted similarly to the following example:
[
{
"period":"2024-01-04",
"currency_id":"USD",
"currency_name_th": "สหรัฐอเมริกา",
"currency_name_eng":"USA",
"buying_sight":34.12,
"buying_transfer":34.25,
"selling":34.50,
"mid_rate":34.375
},
{
"period":"2024-01-05",
"currency_id":"USD",
"currency_name_th": "สหรัฐอเมริกา",
"currency_name_eng":"USA",

--- PAGE 391 ---
370 Appendix B
"buying_sight":34.15,
"buying_transfer":34.28,
"selling":34.55,
"mid_rate":34.415
}
]
This JSON response contains information that is not strictly required for the
application’ s use case. For instance, the case study in Chapter2 only requires a
single daily value. While the API provides distinct buying and selling rates, the
mid_rate serves as a sensible and balanced choice for this purpose.
Furthermore, as mentioned earlier, the BOT API imposes a strict limitation:
each API request can only fetch a maximum of 31 days of historical data. Because
the case study requires data spanning over several months, a single API call is in-
sufficient. The application must dynamically break the required timeframe into
smaller, 31-day chunks and execute multiple sequential queries to compile the full
dataset.
Since the source data requires substantial pruning and multiple queries to con-
struct, it is highly beneficial to store the processed result in a format optimized
for its intended use. After benchmarking various data formats available in Python,
the Apache Arrow Inter-Process Communication (IPC) format was selected due to
its efficiency in both storage size and data access speed. The Arrow IPC format is
known for its efficient serialization and is designed for the high-performance trans-
fer of data between processes or systems. This facilitates effective data sharing in
data-intensive applications. For further details on Apache IPC, refer to the official
documentation (Apache Arrow, 2026).
Therefore, the Lambda function’ s role is to execute shortly after midnight, de-
termine the necessary date ranges, execute multiple queries to the BOT API to
retrieve the nested JSON data, extract themid_rate for each date, convert the data
into the cached Arrow IPC format, and store the resulting IPC file in an Amazon
S3 bucket for universal access.
T o create a Lambda function, navigate tohttps://aws.amazon.com/compute/
lambda and select “Sign in to the console” . If an account does not already exist, reg-
istration is required. Within the A WS Management Console, locate the Lambda
service. On the Lambda page, select “Create function” . FigureB.2 outlines the ba-

--- PAGE 392 ---
Appendix B 371
Figure B.2: AWS Lambda Function Creation
sic setup configuration. Specify the function name, runtime, and architecture. The
architecture selection depends on the development environment’ s hardware; for
instance, arm64 is chosen for systems using Apple Silicon, while x86_64 is appro-
priate for most other cases. After entering this information, click “Create function” .
The main page for the Lambda function will then be displayed. The code ed-
itor under thelambda_function tab is where the function’ s logic is implemented,
with various configurable options available in the menus. T o test the function,
click the “T est” button, name the test case, click “Save” , and then click “T est” again.
The execution results will appear, including the T est Event Name, Response, Func-
tion Logs, and Request ID. Particular attention should be paid to the Response and
Function Logs. In the Response, verify that the status code is 200 and that the body
matches the expected output. In the Function Logs, the Billed Duration and Mem-
ory Size are important metrics, as these factors determine the execution cost and
responsiveness of the function.
ScriptsB.1 andB.2 provides the complete program for this purpose. The script
contains methods such asput_ipc_to_s3, get_bot_data, and lambda_handler.
Theput_ipc_to_s3 method handles data storage in Amazon S3. It takes a Po-

--- PAGE 393 ---
372 Appendix B
lars DataFrame (to_write_object) as input, serializes it into the efficient Apache
Arrow IPC format, and uploads it to the designated S3 bucket (se233) with the
specified filename and.ipc extension.
Theaws_access_key_id andaws_secret_access_key can be configured and
retrieved from the A WS Identity and Access Management (IAM) service page. Un-
der “ Access management, ” navigate to “Users. ” From there, locate the “ Access keys”
menu under the “Security credentials” tab, as illustrated in FigureB.3, and create
an access key for programmatic access to A WS services. Once created, the key in-
formation should be set as environment variables in the Lambda function’ s “Con-
figuration” tab, as shown in FigureB.4. This allows the key to be accessed within
the Lambda code using theos.environ variable, as demonstrated in Line 8 of the
script.

--- PAGE 396 ---
Appendix B 375
Theget_bot_data method fetches currency exchange rate data for a specific
symbol (e.g., USD) from the BOT API. Because the API restricts data fetching to a
maximum of 31 days per request, this method dynamically calculates date bound-
aries and iterates through a list of periods. It makes aGET request to the provider’ s
API endpoint, passing theBOT_DATA_API_KEY securely via headers. It then parses
the JSON response into a Polars DataFrame, extracts themid_rate, and builds a
consolidated DataFrame.
Thelambda_handler method is the main entry point executed by A WS Lambda,
orchestrating the data retrieval and processing pipeline. It defines a list of cur-
rency symbols and fetches historical data for each symbol using theget_bot_data
method. It then combines the resulting DataFrames, cleans the data by filling any
missing dates (forward fill), and adds a baseline currency column (THB). Because
the original data represents currencies in relation to THB, the script recalibrates
the data using theUSD column, allowingUSD to act as the base currency for the
final cached file. It then casts numeric columns to a more memory-efficient data
type,Float32. Finally, it saves the processed data to S3 asshort_list.ipc using
theput_ipc_to_s3 method and returns a success status code.
The next configuration step involves managing dependencies.Numpy, Pandas,
andrequests are the primary dependencies for this application. While some tuto-
rials suggest using thepip command for installation, libraries likeNumpy are closely
tied to the system architecture. Therefore, it is recommended to build these depen-
dencies manually. This can be done by downloading the appropriate wheel files
directly from a repository likepypi.org (e.g., fromhttps://pypi.org/project/
numpy/#files forNumpy). Once all dependency files are collected, they should be
placed in a directory namedpython and compressed into a zip file. For conve-
nience, a pre-built layer file is available for download athttps://cmu.to/SE233\
lambdalayer.
After preparing the dependencies zip file, add it to the function as an A WS
Lambda Layer. Click the “ Add a layer” button at the bottom of the function’ s page,
choose “Create a new layer” , and configure it as shown in FigureB.5. Note that the
provided file is prepared for the arm64 architecture, so ensure the configuration
matches FigureB.5. Once the layer is created, confirm its addition to the function
by clicking “ Add a layer” again and configuring it as shown in FigureB.6.

--- PAGE 397 ---
376 Appendix B
The final configuration step involves setting up the S3 bucket. Navigate to the
Amazon S3 service page, click “Create bucket” , provide a unique name, uncheck
“Blockall public access” , and confirm by clicking “Create bucket” .
Figure B.5: Creating Layers for Lambda Dependencies
Additionally, by default, A WS Lambda functions have a timeout of 3 seconds.
Since this data-fetching task can be time-consuming, the timeout should be in-
creased. In the function’ s general configuration, set the timeout to 2 minutes and
the memory limit to 256 MB, as illustrated in FigureB.7. The application should
now be ready. Return to the Lambda function page and click “T est” . Upon suc-
cessful execution (indicated by a response code of 200), thecached_list.ipc file
should be present in the configured S3 bucket. The bucket name can be changed
(if notse233 as in the example) on Line 14 of ScriptB.1.

API Endpoint
The second part of the application is the API endpoint, which provides responses
to user requests. The architecture for this component is shown in FigureB.9. In
this application, users interact with the system via API requests that are managed
by the A WS API Gateway. This gateway is responsible for receiving input queries
from users and triggering a second A WS Lambda function to process them. This
Lambda function loads thecached_currency.ipc file from Amazon S3, selects the
relevant information based on the user’ s query, transforms the data back into JSON
format, and sends it through the API Gateway as the response.
Figure B.9: API Endpoint Architecture Diagram
ScriptsB.3 andB.4 provide the code for this second Lambda function. It con-
sists of three methods:get_ipc_from_s3, lambda_handler, andget_currency_-
history. Theget_ipc_from_s3 method facilitates data retrieval from an S3 bucket.
It constructs a temporary local file path, downloads the file (in Apache IPC format)
from the designated S3 bucket (se233), reads the file into a Polars DataFrame, and
returns this DataFrame for further processing.

+ 32 def get_currency_history(base, symbol, start_date_str, end_date_str):
+ 33 start_date = datetime.datetime.strptime(start_date_str, "%Y-%m-%d").date()
+ 34 end_date = datetime.datetime.strptime(end_date_str, "%Y-%m-%d").date()
+ 35 sys_start_date = datetime.datetime.strptime('2022-01-01', "%Y-%m-%d").date()
+ 36 today = datetime.datetime.now(pytz.timezone('Asia/Bangkok')).date()
+ 37
+ 38
+ 39 if (end_date - start_date).days > 30:
+ 40 return {'message': 'You have exceeded the maximum day limit (30 days) for
historical currency conversion. Please request for a shorter time period.'}
+ 41 elif end_date <= start_date:
+ 42 return {'message': 'Invalid date range: End date must be later than the start
date.'}
+ 43 elif start_date < sys_start_date:
+ 44 return {'message': 'Invalid start date: The minimum start date is 2022-01-01.'}
+ 45 elif start_date > today or end_date > today:
+ 46 return {'message': 'Invalid date range: Start and end dates cannot exceed today
.'}
+ 47 all_df = get_ipc_from_s3('cached_currency')
+ 48 if base not in all_df.columns[1:]:
+ 49 return {'message': base + ' is not a supported currency.'}
+ 50 if symbol not in all_df.columns[1:]:
+ 51 return {'message': symbol + ' is not a supported currency.'}
+ 52 all_df = all_df.with_columns(pl.col("date").str.strptime(pl.Date, "%Y-%m-%d"))
+ 53 end_date = min(end_date, today)
+ 54 all_df = (
+ 55 all_df.filter((pl.col("date") >= start_date) & ( pl.col("date") <= end_date))
+ 56 .with_columns(pl.col("date").dt.strftime("%Y-%m-%d"))
+ 57 .select(['date', base, symbol])
+ 58 .with_columns((pl.col(base) / pl.col(symbol)).alias("result"))
+ 59 .select('date', 'result')
+ 60 )
+ 61 return {
+ 62 'message': 'Success',
+ 63 'based': base,
+ 64 'symbol': symbol,
+ 65 'start_date': start_date_str,
+ 66 'end_date': end_date_str,
+ 67 'rates': dict(zip(all_df["date"].to_list(), all_df["result"].to_list()))
68 }

--- PAGE 403 ---
382 Appendix B
Thelambda_handler method serves as the main entry point for this A WS Lambda
function. It parses the input event, extracting the base and target currency sym-
bols along with the start and end dates. It then calls theget_currency_history
function to retrieve the relevant exchange rate data. Finally, it returns a Hypertext
Transfer Protocol (HTTP) 200 status code with a JSON body containing the result
fromget_currency_history. Note that the try and except block on Line 18 is de-
signed to handle input formats from both a Lambda test event and the A WS API
Gateway.
Theget_currency_history method first validates the input parameters (base
currency, target currency, and date range), ensuring they meet specific criteria such
as a valid date range and supported currency symbols. It then loads the cached
currency data from S3 using theget_ipc_from_s3 method, filters the data based
on the specified dates, and calculates the exchange rate between the base and target
currencies for each date. The method returns a dictionary containing a success
message, the base currency, the target currency, the date range, and a dictionary of
exchange rates for each date within the specified range.

386 Appendix B
This concludes the overview of the application developed for the Chapter2
case study. While the setup may seem complex, the effort and resources required
are significantly less than those for owning and configuring a physical server. Fur-
thermore, the operational cost is remarkably low; for instance, millions of requests
to a minimally configured Lambda function can be served for approximately one
US dollar.
Selecting appropriate storage for an A WS Lambda function involves a trade-off
between latency and cost. The primary options include:
• Amazon S3: Offers high availability with low latency (typically tens to hun-
dreds of milliseconds), making it suitable for general-purpose storage. Its
cost is moderate for storage, low for GET requests, and moderate for PUT
requests and data transfer.
• Amazon Glacier: Optimized for long-term, archival storage. It features low
storage costs, but retrieval times can range from minutes to hours. Retrieval
and data transfer costs are moderate and vary based on retrieval speed.
• Amazon DynamoDB: A NoSQL database designed for single-digit millisec-
ond latency, making it well-suited for applications requiring fast, consistent
responses. Its storage cost is moderate, with data transfer costs also being
moderate. Throughput pricing can be high for provisioned capacity but is
moderate for on-demand mode.
• Amazon ElastiCache: Utilizes in-memory caching (e.g., Redis) to provide
sub-millisecond latency, which is ideal for caching and real-time data pro-
cessing. Costs are generally high for instances but low for data transfer within
the same region.
Each storage option has distinct advantages: S3 for versatility, Glacier for cost-
effective archiving, ElastiCache for low-latency caching, and DynamoDB for rapid,
consistent access in high-transaction applications. TableB.1 summarizes these op-
tions. For this course, Amazon S3 was chosen to minimize the combined costs of
storage, requests, and data transfer. With code optimizations, this choice satisfies
the project’ s requirements with an acceptable latency (a few hundred milliseconds)
for its educational purpose.
